                                          vii
 Solubility Selective Membrane Materials for Carbon Dioxide Removal

                         from Mixtures with Light Gases



                              Publication No.



                                    Haiqing Lin, Ph.D.

                         The University of Texas at Austin, 2005



                              Supervisor: Benny D. Freeman



       Membrane technology has attracted interest for the selective removal of carbon

dioxide from mixtures with light gases such as H2, CH4 and N2. While conventional

structure-property correlations have focused mainly on improving the separation

performance by increasing polymer size sieving ability (i.e., diffusivity selectivity), this

project explores the possibility of harnessing favorable interactions between CO2 and

polymers containing polar groups to improve permeability/selectivity properties.

       Ether oxide groups are discovered to be among the best moieties known to

interact with CO2, leading to high CO2 solubility and CO2/light gas solubility selectivity,
while still providing polymer chain flexibility, leading to high CO2 diffusivity and

favorable CO2/H2 diffusivity selectivity.

       Poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO) has a high concentration of ether oxygen groups and

exhibits high CO2/light gas selectivities. However, gas permeability is low due to the

high crystallinity in PEO. Crosslinking and introduction of short chain branching are


                                            viii
efficient methods to inhibit crystallization. Three series of crosslinked poly(ethylene

oxide) rubbers have been prepared using prepolymer solutions containing: (1)

poly(ethylene glycol) diacrylate (PEGDA) and H2O, (2) PEGDA and poly(ethylene
glycol) methyl ether acrylate (PEGMEA), and (3) PEGDA and poly(ethylene glycol)

acrylate (PEGA). Independent of the prepolymer composition, all of these polymers have

similar ethylene oxide (EO) content (approximately 82 wt.%).

       Crosslink density decreases with decreasing PEGDA content in the prepolymer

solution, which is estimated from water swelling experiments and/or dynamic mechanical

testing and has essentially no effect on gas transport properties. Increasing PEGMEA

content increases the average size of free volume elements, resulting in a decreased glass

transition temperature, and increased CO2 permeability and CO2/H2 selectivity.         In

contrast, the presence of PEGA or water has a negligible impact on these properties.

       Due to the affinity between EO and CO2, decreasing temperature increases

CO2/light gas solubility selectivity. PEGDA/PEGMEA-30 (a copolymer containing 30

wt.% PEGDA and the balance PEGMEA) demonstrates very favorable mixed gas

CO2/H2 and CO2/CH4 separation performance. The separation properties improve with

decreasing temperature and have excellent resistance to impurities in the feed streams

such as high pressure CO2, higher hydrocarbons and water. Interestingly, the highly
sorbing impurities can even improve CO2/H2 separation. Pure and mixed gas diffusivity

and permeability in these crosslinked PEO rubbers were successfully modeled using a

new free volume model, which was based on the Cohen-Turnbull and Fujita free volume

models coupled with Chow’s model for estimating glass transition temperatures in

polymer/gas mixtures as a function of temperature and penetrant concentration.

                                               xxxi
CHAPTER 1




Introduction




     1
1.1    CO2 REMOVAL FROM LIGHT GAS

       Carbon dioxide is an impurity that must be removed from mixtures with light
gases such as H2, CH4, and N2, and the scale of these separations is enormous [1].
Hydrogen is a basic chemical in the fertilizer and refinery industries, and its annual
production is 8.1 × 10 9 kg in the U.S. alone [2]. More than 95% of H2 is produced via
steam reforming of hydrocarbons [2]:
                                                         m    
                        C n H m + nH 2 O + heat ←
                                                → nCO +  + n  H 2 ,
                                                         2    

followed by a shift reaction to convert CO to CO2 to obtain additional H2 [2]:

                        CO + H 2 O ←
                                   → CO2 + H 2 .

       As the result, hydrogen is contaminated with CO2 during production; and the CO2
must be removed to produce highly purified H2 [1]. Hydrogen production is expected to
increase as refinery demands for H2 increase and particularly since H2 has been proposed
as an energy carrier for future fuel cell technologies [3].
       Natural gas is a vital energy source expected to be the fastest growing primary
energy sources in the coming decades [4]. Currently, the annual U.S. production of
natural gas is 5.6×1011 m3(STP), and approximately 20% of this gas contains excess CO2
which must be removed to meet the U.S. pipeline specification, i.e., 2 vol.% or less [5].
CO2 recovery from flue gas (primarily in mixtures with N2) is becoming more important
since it is one of the main global warming agents, and there are initiatives under way that
might eventually require CO2 removal from flue gas [6].
       Due to the large volume in these applications, even small improvement in the
efficiency of CO2 removal from gas streams could lead to significant cost reduction.




                                              2
1.2    MEMBRANE TECHNOLOGY

       Currently, CO2/H2 separation is achieved mainly by pressure swing adsorption
(PSA) [1], which can yield H2 product at or near its feed partial pressure with a purity
higher than 99%. However, it is a complicated process; as shown in Figure 1.1, a typical
PSA train can contain six parallel adsorbent beds (labeled “A” in the figure) connected in
series with 3 more parallel adsorbers (labeled “B” in the figure), which undergo six- and
seven-step cycles, respectively [7]. More importantly, H2 recovery in PSA units is often
between 70% and 85%, due to the H2 loss in the purge gas, as shown in Figure 1.1 [8].
The purge gas contains only 25-60% H2, and the cost to recover using PSA is prohibitive
(PSA achieves only 40-50% recovery for such feeds [8]). Currently, the H2 in the purge
gas is burned to recover some energy [1], which is a low value use of the H2.




                                                                        Purge gas (H2: 25-60%)




Figure 1.1   Schematic flow diagram for the Gemini pressure swing adsorption process
             (patented by Air Products and Chemicals) to produce H2 and CO2
             simultaneously [7]. The process contains 6 parallel adsorbers (i.e., A beds)
             in series connection with three parallel B beds [7].




                                            3
       The purification of natural gas by removing CO2 is often achieved using
absorption technology (such as amine or hot potassium carbonate aqueous solutions),
which is advantageous on very large scales [1]. However, amine sorption units also have
drawbacks. The amine itself is corrosive and can be energy-intensive to regenerate [1].
       Economically, membranes may be advantageous in small and medium scale
separations and when product purity requirements are not extremely stringent [9].
Membrane technology enjoys inherent advantages, such as small footprint, mechanical
simplicity, and high energy efficiency, relative to PSA and absorption processes [10].
       The emergence of membrane technology for gas separation in the 1980’s resulted
from several breakthroughs. Although gas permeation in membranes has been studied
since the 1940s, membrane fabrication technology was not sufficiently developed to
provide high enough gas flux for industrial applications until Loeb and Sourirajan
introduced techniques to prepare high flux anisotropic membranes with selective layer
thicknesses of less than 0.5 µm and often less than 0.1 µm [9]. The second breakthrough
came with the development of high surface-to-volume membrane module designs, such
as spiral-wound and hollow-fiber modules [9].        These designs accommodate large
membrane area in small volumes, significantly reducing the footprint of membrane
systems. These two advances contributed to the successful development of reverse
osmosis membrane systems. However, Loeb-Sourirajan type anisotropic membranes
could not be directly used for gas separations, because pinholes or defects are always
introduced during the membrane preparation process, and they diminish selectivity
substantially. Henis and Tripodi resolved this limitation by applying a thin layer of
silicon rubber to the membrane (e.g., polysulfone) to eliminate nonselective convective
flow through pinholes [11].       The composite membrane exhibited separation and




                                            4
permeation properties similar to those of polysulfone since silicon rubber has much
higher gas permeability than polysulfone.      These achievements allowed membrane
technology to become a viable alterative to conventional gas separation technologies such
as absorption and adsorption.     Recent efforts have focused on membrane materials
optimization to achieve better separation performance and better stability in process
environments [12]. Optimal membrane materials should have high gas permeability to
reduce the required membrane area, and high separation factor to increase the product
purity and reduce the product loss [5].
       For membrane-based separations in the applications mentioned above, it is highly
desirable to selectively remove CO2 from mixtures with light gases such as H2, N2 and
CH4, thereby maintaining the light gas at or near feed pressure (in the case of H2 and
CH4) to avoid expensive recompression of the desired light gas product [5]. For CO2
removal from N2, selectively removing CO2 avoids permeation of the major component
(N2) across the membrane, which could vastly reduce membrane area requirements.
       Membrane units have been used to separate CO2/CH4, due to their inherent
advantages mentioned above; they are attractive for offshore and remote-location natural
gas processing, and they can be used for bulk separation, i.e., membrane units are
combined with absorption units for debottlenecking. Figure 1.2 illustrates one advantage
of membrane technology over amine absorption units. Initially, the amine sorption unit
in this photograph was built to remove CO2 from the natural gas, and the amine system
contains a large sorption tower, amine regeneration tower, and a complex heat exchanger
network [1]. However, after some time, the unit can not operate due to the amine
corrosion, and it was replaced with the membrane unit shown in the photograph, which
achieves the same separation with a much smaller footprint.




                                           5
                                                          Amine
                                                          Scrubber




                       Air Liquide       Membrane Unit


Figure 1.2   Comparison between an amine scrubbing unit and a membrane unit for
             natural gas purification. This photograph was obtained from Dr. Gregory
             Fleming at MEDAL, L.P.


       In general, most membrane materials have favorable CO2/CH4 selectivity; CO2
(which has a kinetic diameter of 3.3 Å) is small than CH4 (which has a kinetic diameter
of 3.8 Å) and, thus, CO2 has a higher diffusivity than CH4. Additionally, CO2 is more
condensable than CH4, represented by higher critical temperature of CO2, leading to
favorable solubility selectivity for CO2/CH4. Commercial membrane materials for this
separation, such as cellulose acetate and aromatic polyimides, are glassy polymers with
strong size-sieving ability (or high CO2/CH4 diffusivity selectivity). Most efforts to date
on materials design to improve CO2/CH4 separation performance aim to increase the size
sieving ability of the polymer [13,14], probably because the fact that penetrant diffusion
coefficients are often far more sensitive to polymer structure changes than solubility, so




                                            6
diffusion coefficients usually change in a much broader range than penetrant solubility.
However, raw natural gas feeds often contain high pressure CO2 and a high content of
higher hydrocarbons [15,16]. These highly condensable impurities are usually strongly
sorbed in polymers.     As such, they plasticize the polymer matrix and decrease the
size-sieving ability in the polymer, leading to the significant reduction in CO2/CH4
separation [16,17]. Figure 1.3 presents the effect of CO2 partial pressure on mixed gas
CO2/CH4 selectivity in a glassy polyimide (6FDA-mPD) at 35oC [17]. At low CO2
pressure, the polyimide exhibited high CO2/CH4 selectivity due to high diffusivity
selectivity. However, the selectivity decreased by more than an order of magnitude due
to the decrease in diffusivity selectivity at a CO2 partial pressure of 10 atm [17], which is
in the range of typical CO2 partial pressures in natural gas. Such disadvantage limits the
potential applications of membrane units for this separation. On the other hand, the
favorable solubility selectivity, which may be influenced to a smaller extent by such
impurities, has often been overlooked.
       Current membrane technology is not competitive with PSA for hydrogen
purification, mainly because the selectivity in most membrane materials are dominated by
diffusivity selectivity and, therefore, they are more permeable to small penetrants such as
H2 than to larger molecules such as CO2. As a result, the H2 product is produced on the
low pressure permeate side of the membrane, but it is often required at high pressure for
further use, and the recompression costs could be prohibitive [5]. On the other hand,
membrane materials could be more permeable to CO2 than to H2 if the selectivity in these
materials were dominated by solubility selectivity, because CO2 is usually more soluble
than H2. Membrane units with solubility selective membrane materials would produce
purified H2 at or near feed pressure and, for this reason, they might be more competitive




                                             7
                                             50



                                             40




                        CO /CH Selectivity
                                             30

                                       4

                                             20
                                       2




                                             10



                                             0
                                                  0   2       4      6       8    10
                                                      CO Partial Pressure [atm]
                                                          2




Figure 1.3   Effect of CO2 pressure (the degree of plasticization) on mixed gas CO2/CH4
             selectivity in a polyimide, 6FDA-mPD (4,4’-
             (hexafluoroisopropylidene)diphthalic anhydride-m-phenylene diamine) [17].
             The CO2/CH4 feed gas mixture was 50:50.

with PSA, or at least they might be used for debottlenecking and recovery of H2 from fuel
gas, as shown in Figure 1.1 [8,18].
       For CO2/N2 separation, the selectivity in conventional size-sieving membrane
materials is not high enough, since the kinetic diameters of CO2 (3.3 Å) and N2 (3.64 Å)
are rather close [5].              Therefore, the approach of pursuing solubility selectivity is a
reasonable choice.

1.3    GOALS AND ORGANIZATION OF THE DISSERTATION

       The successful competition of membrane technology for CO2/light gas separation
with conventional gas treatment technologies relies critically on the ability of membrane
materials to achieve high gas permeability and high separation factor in realistic gas feed




                                                              8
streams. While structure/property guidelines have been extensively explored in an effort
to improve the gas separation performance of polymer membranes by the increasing
polymer size sieving ability (i.e., diffusivity selectivity) in the past decades [13,14],
diffusivity selectivity alone may not provide separation properties good enough for the
removal of CO2 from mixtures with light gases. This project explores structure-transport
property guidelines for designing polymers with high CO2 permeability and CO2/light gas
selectivity; it focuses mainly on materials that achieve high selectivity as a result of high
solubility selectivity.   More specifically, the possibility of harnessing favorable
interactions between CO2 and polymers containing polar groups to improve
permeability/selectivity properties is explored.
       This dissertation is comprised of eleven chapters including this introduction
chapter. Chapter 2 presents background on gas transport in polymeric membranes and
the effect of various types and amount of polar groups in liquids and polymers on CO2
sorption and CO2/light gas solubility selectivity and permselectivity. Based on results of
this literature survey, ethylene oxide units are identified as promising polar groups to
achieve high CO2 permeability and high CO2/light gas solubility selectivity and CO2/H2
permselectivity. Chapter 3 describes materials and experimental techniques. In Chapter
4, gas transport properties in pure poly(ethylene oxide) are investigated and compared
with those in nonpolar polyethylene.         The effect of crystallinity on gas solubility,
diffusivity and permeability is discussed.
       The remaining of chapters focuses on new amorphous crosslinked poly(ethylene
oxide) rubbers. Chapter 5 discusses the effect of crosslinking on gas transport properties.
Chapter 6 reports the effect of temperature and pressure on gas solubility in crosslinked
PEO, which highlights that the affinity between CO2 and ether oxygen linkages becomes




                                               9
more favorable with decreasing temperature.        Chapter 7 discusses the effect of
temperature and pressure on gas permeation and diffusion. Chapter 8 describes the effect
of chain end groups (methoxy and hydroxyl groups) on polymer physical properties, and
methoxy groups are shown to improve CO2 pure gas permeability and CO2/H2 pure gas
selectivity while hydroxyl groups do not. Mixed gas CO2/H2 and CO2/CH4 separation
properties in these crosslinked PEO rubbers are presented in Chapter 9 and Chapter 10,
respectively. Finally, Chapter 11 presents the conclusions and recommendations for
future work.

2.1     THEORY

2.1.1   Permeability and Selectivity

        Gas transport through a dense or nonporous polymeric film is often described by
the solution-diffusion mechanism [19]. Feed gas at a high (i.e., upstream) pressure, p2,
dissolves into the feed side surface of the film, diffuses through the film due to a
concentration gradient, and finally desorbs from the permeate side surface at the
downstream (i.e., low pressure) face of the film. The one dimensional flux of gas A
through the film in the x direction (i.e., NA) can be described by Fick’s Law [20,21]:
                                   Dloc dC A
                        NA = −                                                           (2.1)
                                 1 − wA dx

where Dloc is the gas local diffusion coefficient in the film, CA is the local concentration
of dissolved gas, and wA is the weight fraction of gas A in the film.
        The steady state permeability of gas A, PA, through a film of thickness l is

defined as [9,21,22]:
                               NA ⋅l
                        PA ≡                                                             (2.2)
                               p 2 − p1

where p2 and p1 are upstream (i.e., high) and downstream (i.e., low) pressures,
respectively. Permeability coefficients are commonly expressed in Barrers, where
                      cm 3 ( STP) ⋅ cm
1 Barrer = 1 × 10 −10                  . Combining eqs 2.1 and 2.2 and integrating from x =
                      cm 2 ⋅ s ⋅ cmHg

0 (C = C2) to x = l (C = C1), one obtains:
                                   C2 − C1
                        PA = D A                                                         (2.3)
                                   p2 − p1




                                               12
where DA is the concentration averaged effective diffusion coefficient in the range from
C1 to C2:
                                             C2                        C2
                                  1               Dloc         1
                        DA =
                               C2 − C1   ∫   C1 1 − wA
                                                       dC =
                                                            C2 − C1   ∫ D dC
                                                                       C1
                                                                            eff        (2.4)


and Deff is the local effective diffusion coefficient.
        For simplicity, experiments are often designed so that p1 << p2 and, consequently,
C1 << C2. In this limit, eq 2.3 reduces to:

                        PA = D A × S A                                                 (2.5)

and

                        S A ≡ C 2 p2                                                   (2.6)

SA is the apparent sorption coefficient or the solubility of penetrant A in the polymer at
the upstream pressure, p2.
        The ideal selectivity of a membrane for gas A over gas B is the ratio of their pure
gas permeabilities [23]:
                                 PA  DA   S A 
                        αA B =     =        ×                                          (2.7)
                                 PB  DB   S B 

where DA/DB is the diffusivity selectivity, and SA/SB is the solubility selectivity.

2.1.2   Solubility

        Gas solubility in rubbery polymers depends on gas condensability (usually
represented by the critical temperature of the gas), interaction of the gas with the polymer
matrix, and also weakly on the free volume of the polymer [23]. In rubbery polymers,
sorption of gases at low pressure usually obeys Henry’s law [24]:

                        C = kD p                                                       (2.8)




                                                  13
where kD is the solubility coefficient, or Henry’s Law constant.
        For highly sorbing penetrants, such as organic vapors, penetrant concentration in
the polymer is often described using the Flory-Huggins model [24]:
                             p
                       ln         = ln φ 2 + (1 − φ 2 ) + χ (1 − φ 2 ) 2               (2.9)
                            p sat

where psat is the penetrant vapor pressure at the experiment temperature, χ is the Flory-
Huggins interaction parameter, and φ 2 is the volume fraction of penetrant dissolved in
the polymer matrix. The volume fraction, φ 2 , is given by [24]:

                       φ2 =
                                (C 22,414)V2                                          (2.10)
                              1 + (C 22,414 )V2

where V2 is the partial molar volume (cm3/mol) of the penetrant in the polymer, and C
has units of cm3(STP)/(cm3 polymer). For low concentration of sorbed gas, φ 2 << 1 , eqs

2.9 and 2.10 reduce to Henry’s law [25]. For high concentration of sorbed gas, this
model predicts an increasing value of solubility (i.e., C/p) as pressure increases.

2.1.3   Diffusivity

        The local effective diffusion coefficient, Deff, characterizing penetrant diffusivity
in the polymer at a penetrant concentration of C2, can be evaluated using an equation
obtained by taking the derivative of both sides of eq 2.4 with respect to C [26]:
                                            dP   dp 
                       Deff (C 2 ) =  PA + p A                                   (2.11)
                                            dp  p2  dC2  p
                                                                2




which requires the pressure dependence of permeability and solubility to calculate Deff.
        Diffusion of small molecules in a polymer is often qualitatively related to free
volume by [27,28]:




                                                    14
                                     B 
                       DA = AD exp −                                             (2.12)
                                   FFV 

where AD is a pre-exponential factor, B is a constant proportional to penetrant size, and
FFV is the fractional free volume in the polymer. Gas diffusivity is also determined by
polymer chain flexibility, often characterized by polymer glass transition temperature, Tg
[23]. By combining eqs 2.5 and 2.12, the following expression can be obtained:
                                     B 
                       PA = AP exp −                                             (2.13)
                                   FFV 

where AP is a pre-exponential factor, which is equal to SAAD. FFV is often estimated
using the following expression [29]:
                               V − Vo
                       FFV =                                                       (2.14)
                                 V

where V is the specific volume of the amorphous polymer at the temperature of interest,
and Vo is the specific occupied volume at 0 K, which is estimated as 1.3 times the van der
Waals volume [30]. The van der Waals volume can be calculated using the group
contribution method [30].

2.1.4   Temperature Effect

        The effect of temperature on gas permeability, solubility and diffusivity is often
described using the Van’t Hoff-Arrhenius approach [31]:
                                    − EP 
                       PA = PA0 exp                                              (2.15)
                                    RT 

                                      − ∆H S 
                       S A = S A0 exp                                            (2.16)
                                      RT 




                                              15
                                      − ED 
                       D A = D A0 exp                                                (2.17)
                                      RT 

where PA0, SA0 and DA0 are pre-exponential factors, R is the ideal gas constant, T is
absolute temperature, and Ep, ∆HS and ED are the activation energy of permeation,
enthalpy of sorption and activation energy of diffusion, respectively. From eqs 2.15-
2.17, the following expression can be obtained:

                       E P = E D + ∆H s                                                (2.18)

       Sorption is typically considered to be comprised of two hypothetical
thermodynamic steps, i.e., condensation of the pure penetrant and mixing of the
hypothetical pure condensed penetrant with polymer segments. Within the context of this
picture, the above equation can be rewritten as follows [31]:

                       E P = E d + ∆H cond + ∆H mix                                    (2.19)

where ∆Hcond and ∆Hmix are the enthalpy of condensation and mixing, respectively.

2.2    APPROACH

       Generally, CO2/H2 separation properties reflect a tradeoff between favorable
solubility selectivity (CO2 is more condensable and, therefore, more soluble than H2 in
polymers) and unfavorable diffusivity selectivity (CO2 is larger than H2, so DCO2/DH2<1).

In strongly size-sieving polymers such as glassy polysulfone (as shown in Table 2.1), the
diffusivity selectivity in favor of H2 permeation overwhelms the solubility selectivity in
favor of CO2 permeation, and the materials are H2 selective. In most of rubbery polymers
with weak size-sieving ability, the diffusivity selectivity in favor of H2 permeation is still
comparable to the solubility selectivity in favor of CO2 permeation, and the materials
such as poly(dimethyl siloxane) (as shown in Table 2.1) have low CO2/H2 selectivity.




                                             16
Table 2.1    Pure gas CO2 permeability and CO2/H2 selectivity at infinite dilution (i.e.,
             the upstream pressure was extrapolated to 0 atm [25]) and 35oC

       Polymer                                      PCO2 [Barrer]       PCO2 / PH 2

       Polysulfone [32]                                  5.6               0.40

       Cellulose acetate [33]                            5.5               0.40

       Poly(1-trimethylsilyl-1-propyne) [34]           27,000              1.8

       Poly(dimethyl siloxane) [34]                    3,800               4.3




       To achieve very high levels of reverse selectivity (i.e. to be much more permeable
to CO2 than to H2), a membrane must exhibit specific favorable interactions with CO2 to
enhance solubility selectivity, and it must exhibit very weak size-sieving ability to bring
DCO2 / D H 2 as close to 1 as possible. Using either of these effects alone does not work, as

illustrated in Table 2.1. Strong size-sieving materials such as glassy polysulfone and
cellulose acetate are more permeable to H2, though cellulose acetate enjoys specific
interactions with CO2; CO2/H2 selectivities of no more than 4 are achieved using the most
weakly size-sieving materials known, such as poly(1-trimethylsilyl-1-propyne) (PTMSP)
and poly(dimethyl siloxane) (PDMS) [34].
       One strategy to enhance CO2/H2 solubility selectivity is to introduce polar groups
into the polymer matrix, which could have favorable interactions with quadrupolar CO2
but not H2 [35,36].     However, a detailed analysis on the effect of the type and
concentration of polar groups on the CO2/polymers interactions has never been
quantitatively presented before, even though this knowledge is critical to designing
optimal polymer structures to achieve high CO2/H2 solubility selectivity. Furthermore,




                                            17
incorporating polar groups in polymers to increase S CO2 / S H 2 might be compromised by

increases in size sieving ability caused by decreasing free volume and increasing the
polymer chain stiffness, two common consequences of including highly polar moieties in
polymers [25].
        In general, a judicious balance may be required when adding, for example, polar
groups to a polymer matrix so that CO2 solubility and CO2/H2 solubility selectivity can be
increased with little or no loss in CO2 diffusivity and CO2/H2 diffusivity selectivity. The
following section describes structure-transport property guidelines for designing
polymers with high CO2 permeability and CO2/light gas selectivity; it focuses mainly on
materials that achieve high selectivity as a result of high solubility selectivity. More
specifically, the effect of various polar groups on CO2 solubility, diffusivity and
permeability and CO2/light gas selectivity is discussed.                     The present study focuses
primarily on rubbery polymers, because, as will be demonstrated, such polymers display
the requisite weak size-sieving behavior needed to achieve high CO2/H2 reverse
selectivity.

2.2.1   Structure-Gas Solubility Correlation

        When the ratio of polymer to penetrant partial molar volumes (m = V1 V2 ) is

finite, the Flory-Huggins model is written as [24]
                             p
                       ln         = ln φ 2 + (1 − 1 / m )(1 − φ 2 ) + χ (1 − φ 2 ) 2            (2.20)
                            p sat

which is reduced to eq 2.9 when m is infinite.                   Combining eqs 2.10 and 2.20, an
expression for the gas infinite dilution solubility, S A∞ , (cm3(STP)/(cm3 atm)) can be

obtained [35]




                                                    18
                                  22414 − (1+ χ −1 / m )
                         S A∞ =          e                                           (2.21)
                                  psatV2

Therefore, penetrant solubility depends not only on gas physical properties such as
saturation vapor pressure and partial molar volume, but also on the gas’s interactions with
the polymer matrix. As interactions become more favorable (i.e., as χ decreases),

penetrant solubility increases exponentially, according to this simple model.
        The Flory-Huggins interaction parameter, χ , is typically related to the penetrant

solubility parameter as follows [37]:
                               V (δ − δ )
                                                    2

                       χ = β0 + 2 1 2                                                (2.22)
                                   RT

where β 0 is a constant which, in the original Flory-Huggins theory, is set to zero [37],

and δ 1 and δ 2 are the solubility parameters of the polymer and penetrant, respectively.

Based on this model, maximum values of CO2 solubility would be observed when the
polymer has a solubility parameter equal to that of CO2.
        Generally, the effect of liquid solvent chemical structure on gas sorption has been
used as a guideline for designing highly solubility selective polymers, because the
solubility selectivity of polymers is presumed to be similar to that of liquid solvents with
similar structure [35]. One rationale for this approach is that, in the limit of infinite
dilution, the Flory-Huggins model, together with eq 2.22, is consistent with solubility
predictions from regular solution theory, which is widely used for correlating gas
solubility in liquids [38]. For liquids, regular solution theory provides the following
expression for the mole fraction of gas dissolved in a liquid solvent at 1 atm, x2, as a
function of the solubility parameter difference between the liquid solvent and the gas
[38]:

and coworkers for H2S [41].
b
  Tc is critical temperature [39], and psat is the penetrant saturation vapor pressure at 25oC
[42].
c
  solubility parameters, which are estimated for the hypothetical liquids at 25oC and 1
atm. The values are taken from [43], except that of H2S [42].


                                  1        −V2 (δ1 − δ 2 )2 
                           x2 =       exp                                             (2.23)
                                  f2L            RT


where f2L (atm) is the fugacity of the pure liquid penetrant (i.e., hypothetical condensed
gas) at atmospheric pressure. As δ 1 , the liquid solubility parameter, approaches δ 2 , x2
reaches a value of 1 / f 2L , or 1/psat for ideal gases, which is Raoult’s Law. The values of
V2 and psat used in this calculation are recorded in Table 2.2.

           If the liquid solvent and penetrant have the same partial molar volume (i.e.,
V1 = V2 or, equivalently, m=1), then x2 = S A∞V2 / 22414 according to eq 2.21. Under

these circumstances, eq 2.23 can be derived from eqs 2.21 and 2.22, so the expression for
S A∞ is:




                                                      20
                                  22414  V2 (δ1 − δ 2 )2
                       ln S = ln
                           ∞
                           A
                                          −                                        (2.24)
                                   p  V
                                  sat 2       RT


This equation predicts that gas solubility reaches a maximum value when δ1 equals to δ2.
This concept has been successfully applied to obtain, for example, xenon’s solubility
parameter. Steinbery and Manowitz graphed xenon solubility as a function of solvent
solubility parameter and found a sharp maximum at a solvent solubility parameter of 16
MPa0.5 [44,45]. This value is in good agreement with the solubility parameter of xenon
estimated from an empirical correlation between δ2 and Tc proposed by Hildebrand et al.
[38] While eq 2.24 is a satisfactory model for describing gas solubility in solvents with
solubility parameters near that of the gas considered, Steinbery and Manowitz’s results
also show that eq 2.24 enormously overestimates the decrease of gas solubility with
increasing δ1 when δ1 is far from δ2 [44]. When δ1 and δ2 differ substantially, the
following empirical equation is often used to correlate the solubility coefficients of a gas
in a series of liquids of varying solubility parameter [38]:

                       ln S A∞ = a'+b'δ12                                            (2.25)

where a′ and b′ are treated as empirical adjustable constants.

chemical structure and solubility data is recorded in Table 2.3. In this example, N2 is
used as a marker for nonpolar gases (e.g., CH4 and H2) because N2 solubility data are
widely available, and liquid solvents are used because there are many more data for gas

            solubility selectivity at 25oC; (b) N2 solubility at 25oC as a function of the
            square of liquid solubility parameter; and (c) Effect of solvent solubility

                                                                                                                                        23
solubility in liquids than in polymers. The solubility parameter values for the liquids are
calculated from the definition [38]

                       δ ≡ (∆H vap − RT ) V                                         (2.26)

where ∆H vap is the enthalpy of vaporization, and V is the liquid molar volume at 25°C

[38]. Historically, these parameters are evaluated at 25°C, but they can be evaluated at
any convenient temperature as long as it is well below Tc and as long as all parameters
are evaluated at the same temperature [38]. These δ1 values, recorded in Table 2.3, are
very close to the estimated Hansen solubility parameters, which consider the effect of
polar groups and even hydrogen bonding on solubility parameter values [45]. As the
solvent solubility parameter increases, CO2 solubility increases to a maximum before
decreasing. This behavior can be approximately modeled using eq 2.24 by setting the
CO2 solubility parameter, δ2, to 21.8±0.2 MPa0.5. The uncertainty in δ2 is estimated using
the propagation of errors technique [59] and assuming that the CO2 solubility data in
Table 2.3 have an uncertainty of ±10%.
       The model fit, with the CO2 solubility parameter as the only adjustable parameter,
is the solid curve in Figure 2.1a.     This result is unexpected because the solubility
parameter of hypothetical liquid CO2 at 25oC is reported to be much lower than 21.8
MPa0.5. Prausnitz and Shair [43] report a value of 12.2 MPa0.5, and Lawson [60] reports
6.8 MPa0.5. These values were obtained by treating δ2 as an adjustable parameter and
then fitting CO2 solubility data to equations similar to eq 2.24, which is a typical method
for estimating gas solubility parameter values since the CO2 critical temperature (i.e.,
31oC) is very close to 25oC, so δ2 cannot be calculated from eq 2.26 [38,61].
       To understand this enormous discrepancy between the reported CO2 solubility
parameter values and the apparent value that we obtained, a closer examination of the




                                            24
calculation of CO2 solubility parameter is useful. The value of 12.2 MPa0.5 was obtained
from CO2 solubility in toluene at 25oC, where the CO2 mole fraction was 0.0105 at a CO2
partial pressure of 1 atm [46]. In fact, fitting this mole fraction to eq 2.23 yields two δ2
values since eq 2.23 is second order in δ2; these values are 12.2 and 24 MPa0.5. The
higher value is rather similar to what we have obtained, but it was not mentioned in the
earlier reference [43]. The value of 6.8 MPa0.5 was calculated using CO2 solubility in
various polar and nonpolar liquids with solubility parameters ranging between 12 and
20.4 MPa0.5 [60].    The reference for this value does not provide the experimental
solubility values used and does not provide any comparison between experimental and
calculated values. However, the fitting results could not be good, since it would predict
that CO2 solubility would decrease monotonically as liquid solubility parameter
increases, which is not the case, as shown in Figure 2.1a. For example, at 25oC, CO2
solubility in n-hexane is only 2.1 cm3 (STP)/(cm3 atm) [62], but it is 6.6 cm3 (STP)/(cm3
atm) in acetone [50], which has a solubility parameter of 19.8 MPa0.5. Lawson [60] also
used a value of 105 cm3/mole for the partial molar volume of liquid CO2, which is
approximately three times higher than typical reported values (i.e., about 45 cm3/mole
[40,42,63]). Additionally, the value of 6.8 MPa0.5 is rather low compared with values of
10.4 MPa0.5 for the solubility parameter of H2 and 10.6 MPa0.5 for the solubility
parameter of N2 reported in the same paper and obtained using the same model fitting
method [60]. More importantly, the values of 12.2 or 6.8 MPa0.5 cannot predict a
maximum in CO2 solubility where it is observed experimentally, and this ability to
predict a maximum in solubility when the solubility parameters of the solvent and solute
are similar is a key concept of regular solution theory [45]. On the other hand, Figure




                                            25
2.1a suggests that a solubility parameter value of 21.8 MPa0.5 for CO2 does capture its
tendency to dissolve in the liquids considered [38].
       As illustrated in Figure 2.1a, CO2/N2 solubility selectivity reaches a maximum at
a solubility parameter value quite near that associated with the maximum in CO2
solubility. Since light gases such as N2 have much lower solubility parameters than any
of the liquid solvents shown (cf. Tables 2.2 and 2.3) and no specific interactions with
these liquids, increasing the solvent solubility parameter should monotonically decrease
their solubilities, which is often described using the empirical model given by eq 2.25.
As illustrated in Figure 2.1b, the model fit describes quite well most of the gas solubility
data except that of methanol.       Consequently, CO2/N2 solubility selectivity can be
modeled using eqs 2.24 and 2.25, and the result is shown as the dashed line in Figure
2.1a. The change in CO2 solubility from one liquid to another dominates the solubility
selectivity and, therefore, CO2/N2 solubility selectivity exhibits a peak at a similar δ2
value as that of CO2 solubility.
       Figure 2.1a contains many common polar groups and demonstrates that ether
oxygens (cf. tetrahydrofuran), nitriles (cf. acetonitrile), carbonyls (cf. acetone), acetates
(cf. methyl acetate) and amides (cf. N,N-dimethylformamide) are more useful for
improving CO2 solubility and CO2/N2 selectivity than polar groups such as halogens (cf.
trichloromethane or chloroform), carbonates (cf. propylene carbonate), sulfoxides (cf.
dimethyl sulfoxide) and hydroxyls (cf. methanol). It is worth mentioning that propylene
carbonate and methanol are used as physical absorbents to remove acid gases such as
CO2 and H2S from light gases [1].         Table 2.3 also presents CO2/H2 and CO2/CH4
solubility selectivity values, and they exhibit trends similar to those of CO2/N2 as the
matrix solubility parameter changes. Gas solubility values in water are also listed in




                                             26
Table 2.3; water has the highest solubility parameter and the lowest CO2 solubility among
all of the liquids considered.
       Figure 2.1c presents H2S solubility and H2S/N2 solubility selectivity in liquids
containing various polar groups. H2S is also an acid gas and has a reported solubility
parameter of 18 MPa0.5, however, the method to obtain this value was not discussed in
detail [64]. As illustrated in Figure 2.1c, H2S solubility also reaches a maximum at a
liquid solvent solubility parameter of approximately 22.3±0.2 MPa0.5, based on fitting eq
2.24 to the data and treating the H2S solubility parameter as an adjustable constant. The
uncertainty is estimated using the propagation of errors technique [59] and assuming that
the H2S solubility data have an uncertainty of ±10%. The dashed line in Figure 2.1c was
calculated using the calculated H2S solubility values from using eq 2.24 and N2 solubility
values from eq 2.25.
       Similar to CO2, THF, which contains polar ether oxygens, also exhibits high H2S
solubility and high H2S/N2 solubility selectivity. Figures 2.1a and 2.1c clearly indicate
the discrepancy between the reported solubility parameter of CO2 and H2S and the
practical effective solubility parameter value, which is taken to be the value of the solvent
with the highest CO2 and H2S solubility, respectively. Unfortunately, there are few data
for solvents with solubility parameters in the range from 20 to 25 MPa0.5, which is a very
interesting range since, presumably, a maximum in solubility and solubility selectivity
would be observed there. Consequently, the apparent effective solubility parameter
values for CO2 and H2S are somewhat approximate.
       Figure 2.2 presents CO2 solubility and CO2/N2 solubility selectivity at 35oC in
various rubbery polymers. The polymer solubility parameters were estimated by Fedor’s
group contribution method [30]. The polymers selected for this comparison are basically




                                             27
polyethylene (PE) containing various types and amounts of polar groups. Four polar
groups are present in these polymers: ether oxygens [36,65], acetates [33], nitriles [66]
and carbonates [67]. For example, poly(ethylene oxide) is polyethylene with an ether
oxygen separating each pair of carbon atoms; polyacrylonitrile may be viewed as PE
containing a nitrile substituent on every other carbon, etc.        Although the data are
somewhat more scattered than those for the liquids, both CO2 solubility and CO2/N2
solubility selectivity appear to peak at a polymer solubility parameter value of about 22
MPa 0.5, similar to that of the liquids (cf. Figure 1a). Polymers with the highest CO2
solubility contain polar groups such as ether oxygens, nitriles and acetates.
Unfortunately, even though carbonyl groups can improve CO2 sorption and CO2/N2
solubility selectivity, there has been no systematic study of gas sorption in rubbery
polymers containing high concentrations of such groups to the best of our knowledge.
Instead, these groups, along with carbonates and amides, are typically used to improve
barrier properties by increasing chain rigidity and, in turn, lowering diffusion coefficients
[29]. Polymers containing these groups are usually glassy at 35oC, which removes them
from the scope of this study [29,33]. The survey of both liquid solvents and polymers
suggests that incorporation of ether oxygens or nitrile groups may be useful for
improving CO2 sorption and CO2/light gas solubility selectivity. Additionally, increasing
the concentration of these polar groups in the polymers might help. For example, as ether
oxygen concentration increases from polybutadiene to poly(tetramethylene oxide) to
poly(ethylene oxide), CO2 solubility increases monotonically from 0.89 to 1.4 cm3
(STP)/(cm3 atm) [25,36,66]. In copolymers of butadiene and acrylonitrile [66], CO2
solubility and CO2/N2 selectivity increase systematically with increasing acrylonitrile
content [66].

Figure 2.2: The effect of polymer solubility parameter on CO2 solubility (i.e., unfilled

            PVAc: poly(vinyl acetate) [33].

       Qualitative correlations between the concentration of polar moieties in the
polymer matrix and CO2/light gas solubility selectivity have been mentioned previously
[35,36,68]. Koros suggested that CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity increases as the mass
density of carbonyl or sulfonyl groups in polymers increases [35]. Bondar et al. proposed
that CO2/light gas solubility selectivity increases as the concentration of ether linkages or
carbonyl groups increases [36].                                             However, such simple correlations fail in some
circumstances. For example, Morisato et al. studied CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity in a
series of polyamides containing sulfonyl groups; they found that the CO2/CH4 selectivity




                                                                                      29
was much lower than predicted based on the total concentration of carbonyl and sulfonyl
groups. They explained this finding by arguing that the carbonyl groups preferred to
interact with amide linkages on adjacent chains to form hydrogen bonds rather than
interact with CO2 [69]. The current work might explain this phenomenon in a more
systematic way. Strong hydrogen bonding in polymers increases the solubility parameter
to values beyond 22 MPa1/2, which would increase the Flory-Huggins interaction
parameter, χ, thereby decreasing CO2 solubility and CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity.
       While eq 2.21 suggests a correlation of gas solubility in low molar mass solvents
with that in analogous polymers, this result cannot fully account for the differences
between gas solubility in these two matrices. Figures 2.3a and 2.3b replot the CO2 and
N2 solubility data in liquids from Figure 2.1a and polymers from Figure 2.2. Polymers
exhibit much lower gas solubility than liquids, even if the polymer has the same
solubility parameter as a liquid. Koros found that eq 2.21 gives 1.36 as the ratio for CO2
solubility in n-heptane to that in high molecular weight polyethylene using 1/m values of
1/3 and 0 for heptane and polyethylene, respectively. However, CO2 solubility in heptane
is 260% higher than that in polyethylene [35]. This trend is also observed for CO2
sorption   in   liquid   tetrahydrofuran   (THF)   and   in   the   analogous    polymer,
poly(tetramethylene oxide) (PTMO). CO2 solubility in THF [47] and in PTMO [36] is
5.12 and 1.12 cm3 (STP)/(cm3 atm) at 35oC, respectively. However, eq 2.21 predicts a
factor of only 1.6 for this solubility ratio using 1/m values of 1/2 and 0 for THF and
PTMO, respectively. Koros argued that other factors might contribute to the difference
between the model prediction and the experimental data. For example, the inadequacies
of lattice representations of Flory-Huggins theory, and the differences between VCO2 and

χ for liquid solvents and solid polymers were thought to play a role in this discrepancy




                                            30
[35]. In our opinion, an important reason for the observed differences could be fractional
free volume (FFV) differences between liquids and polymers. The Flory-Huggins and
regular solution theories do not allow empty lattice sites (i.e., free volume), and this
shortcoming has been cited as a major drawback of these models [70]. Generally, liquids
have higher free volume than polymers, and higher free volume provides more space to
accommodate penetrant molecules, so solubility might be expected to be correlated with
the amount of free volume in the matrix that is absorbing the gas molecules.
                                         FFV has been defined using eq 2.14, which assumes that the occupied volume
does not change with temperature, which might not be true in reality [37]. Nevertheless,
this definition of fractional free volume is widely used to correlate gas diffusion and
permeability values and often yields satisfactory results [29]. In Figures 2.3a and 2.3b,
all of the polymers have FFV values lower than 0.21 while most liquids have FFV much
larger than 0.21, except N,N-dimethylforamide, which has a FFV value of 0.21 and

                                                                                          31
dimethyl sulfoxide, whose FFV is not known since the van der Waals volume of
sulfoxide group is not available (cf. Table 2.3). Clearly, liquids exhibit much larger gas
solubility than polymers, due, presumably, to the much greater free volume in liquids. To
provide some indication of the magnitudes of differences in free volumes for liquids and
polymers, THF would have an estimated FFV of 0.292, while PTMO has a value of only
0.188 if the densities of THF and PTMO are taken as 0.88 and 1.01 g/cm3 [36,42],
respectively. As a result, THF exhibits much higher CO2 sorption than PTMO, although
PTMO has an estimated solubility parameter (i.e., 18.5 MPa0.5) very close to that of THF
(i.e., 19.1 MPa0.5).

2.2.2   Structure-Gas Diffusivity and Permeability Correlations

        Based on the free volume model, eq 2.12, higher free volume would generally
increase gas diffusion coefficients and diminish the effect of penetrant size on diffusion
coefficients. Therefore, one might expect that as free volume decreases, CO2 diffusivity
would decrease while CO2/CH4 diffusivity selectivity would increase, and CO2/H2
diffusivity selectivity would decrease.
        In general, addition of polar groups to polymers increases the solubility
parameter, which decreases free volume and increases polymer chain rigidity [37].
Therefore, while polar groups might increase CO2 sorption, they could decrease CO2
diffusion coefficients. Figures 2.4a and 2.4b present CO2 diffusion coefficients and
CO2/N2 diffusivity selectivity values in various polymers as a function of the polymer
solubility parameter.    Roughly speaking, gas diffusion coefficients decrease with
increasing polymer solubility parameter. In addition to the effect of polar groups on
polymer free volume and chain rigidity, the affinity of polar groups for CO2 might further
retard CO2 diffusion. For example, as acrylonitrile content increases from 0 to 39 wt.%

Figure 2.4: The effect of polymer solubility parameter on (a) CO2 diffusion coefficient
            and (b) CO2/N2 diffusivity selectivity at 35oC in polymers containing
            various polar groups. The definition of the symbols is given in the caption
            of Figure 2.2. PTMC1093: crosslinked poly(tetramethylene carbonate) diol
            [67].

in copolymers with butadiene, CO2/N2 diffusivity selectivity decreases from 0.93 to 0.62
(i.e., by 30%), presumably due a combination of these factors [66].
                                          Figure 2.4b suggests that CO2/N2 diffusivity selectivity may have a minimal value
at a polymer solubility parameter of about 22 MPa0.5, where the affinity between polymer
segments and CO2 reaches a maximum. However, the data are somewhat scattered, so
this judgment needs to be tested by more experimental results.
                                          The result in Figure 2.4b is interesting since CO2 is similar in size to N2 (based on
critical volume values) or slightly smaller than N2 (based on the kinetic diameter of CO2
(3.3 Å) relative to that of N2, 3.64Å) [71]. So, materials with solubility parameters either
much greater or lower than 22 MPa0.5 exhibit CO2/N2 diffusivity selectivity values that
are near to or greater than one. Materials with solubility parameters near 22 MPa0.5 can




                                                                                              33
exhibit diffusivity selectivity values substantially lower than one, suggesting significant
restriction of CO2 mobility beyond that experienced by N2. There is no diffusion theory
currently available to explain such mobility reduction of penetrants due to specific
interactions with polymers [72], even though this phenomenon has been observed for
other systems [72,73].    For example, Davies and Griffiths measured penetrant (i.e.,
toluene, aniline and phenol) diffusion in solutions of poly(vinyl acetate) dissolved in
d-methanol using nuclear magnetic resonance; they reported a decrease in all penetrant
diffusion coefficients as polymer content increased [72]. For toluene, which does not
interact specifically with poly(vinyl acetate), the reduction in its diffusion coefficient
could be explained, using free volume theory, by the decrease of fraction free volume of
the polymer solution as polymer content increased. However, for aniline or phenol,
where hydrogen bonds are formed between the polymer and the penetrant, their diffusion
coefficient reductions were much more significant than that of toluene and cannot be
fully ascribed to the decrease of fraction free volume [72]. In this latter case, interaction
of the aniline or phenol molecules with the polymer presumably slowed their diffusion
rates, qualitatively similar to the reduction in CO2 mobility in solvents with similar
solubility parameters to that of CO2.
       Figures 2.5a and 2.5b present CO2 permeability and CO2/N2 selectivity as a
function of polymer solubility parameter, respectively.            Roughly speaking, the
permeability decreases as the polymer solubility parameter increases, and this decrease is
similar to the trend of diffusion coefficients with solubility parameter (cf. Figure 2.4a).
This result suggests an important role of solubility parameter on free volume, diffusion
coefficients, and, in turn, permeability coefficients. The effect of polymer solubility
parameter on CO2/N2 selectivity represents a tradeoff between solubility selectivity and




                                             34
diffusivity selectivity. Solubility selectivity has a maximum value at approximately 22
MPa1/2, and the lowest values of diffusivity selectivity are observed at approximately the
same polymer solubility parameter value. Except for ether oxygens, all polar groups tend
to decrease CO2 permeability. For example, as acrylonitrile content increases from zero
to 39 wt.% in copolymers with butadiene, CO2 permeability decreases from 180 to 13
Barrers [66].                                      On the other hand, the addition of ether oxygens improves CO2
permeability. For example, amorphous poly(tetramethylene oxide) has an estimated CO2
permeability of 300 Barrers [65] compared with 180 Barrers in polybutadiene [66].
Interestingly, the addition of ether oxygens does not significantly reduce polymer chain
flexibility as characterized by glass transition temperature (Tg). In fact, the hypothetical
Tg of wholly amorphous poly(ethylene oxide) is -85oC, which is slightly lower than the

hypothetical Tg of wholly amorphous polyethylene (i.e., -80oC) [74]. In contrast, as
acrylonitrile content in copolymers with butadiene increases from zero to 39 wt.%, Tg
increases significantly from -80oC to -26oC [66].
        The effect of polar groups on CO2/CH4 separation is more subtle. Polar groups
increase solubility selectivity and should increase diffusivity selectivity if polar groups do
not retard CO2 transport too strongly. Ghosal et al. found that aryl substituent of basic
-CH2-NH2 groups in polysulfone increased CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity, restricted sub-
Tg torsional motion, and decreased fractional free volume, which should contribute to
improvements in size sieving ability and, therefore, CO2/CH4 diffusivity selectivity [73].
However, CO2/CH4 diffusivity selectivity actually decreased due to the affinity between
the amine moieties and CO2, and this combination of somewhat higher solubility
selectivity coupled with much lower diffusivity selectivity resulted in a decrease in
CO2/CH4 permselectivity [73].
        On the other hand, CO2/H2 separation always faces unfavorable diffusivity
selectivity, and this would become even more detrimental if the size sieving ability of the
polymer is increased by, for example, introducing polar groups into the polymer which
brings about an overall reduction in polymer free volume. Furthermore, if the polar
groups enjoy specific and sufficiently strong interactions with CO2, CO2/H2 diffusivity
selectivity will be further reduced. As illustrated in Figures 2.6a and 2.6b, the increase of
polar acrylonitrile content in copolymers with butadiene results in systematically higher
CO2 solubility and CO2/H2 solubility selectivity. However, the decrease in CO2/H2
diffusivity selectivity is larger than the increase in solubility selectivity, leading to a sharp
decrease in permselectivity as polar acrylonitrile content increases. So far, polar ether
oxygens appear to be the only well-known groups which improve CO2/H2

permselectivity. For example, polymers of polybutadiene, PTMO and PEO, in order of
increasing ether oxygen content, exhibit CO2/H2 permselectivity values of about 3.0 (at
35oC and 1 atm [66]), 3.7 (at 35oC and 10 atm [65]) and 6.7 (at 35oC and infinite dilution
[25]), respectively.

2.2.3                                   Structural Design of Poly(ethylene oxide) Containing Polymers

                                        The unique property of polar ether oxygens for CO2 separation has attracted much
interest. There have been numerous efforts to design polymers containing poly(ethylene
oxide) (PEO) for CO2/N2 and CO2/H2 separations [6,36,65,75-86], in part because
ethylene oxide units have a high concentration of ether oxygens and are relatively easy to
prepare. In comparison, poly(methylene oxide) would have an even higher ether oxygen
content. However, gas transport properties in poly(methylene oxide) have not been




                                                                                          37
reported to the best of our knowledge, presumably because it has very high crystallinity
[87] and is difficult to fabricated as a defect-free film for permeation test. PEO is also
subject to a similar disadvantage, i.e., a strong tendency to crystallize, which is
deleterious for gas permeability [88].      The following sections review three main
techniques in the literature to reduce crystallinity in PEO: (1) using low molecular weight
liquid PEO or poly(ethylene glycol) (PEG) as an additive to other polymers; (2)
designing phase separated block copolymers with runs of ethylene oxide (EO) segments
that are too short to crystallize effectively at room temperature; and (3) designing highly
branched, crosslinked networks with high concentrations of PEO.

2.2.3.1 Liquid Poly(ethylene glycol)

       A natural approach to obtaining the beneficial separation performance of PEO
without crystallinity is to use low molecular weight PEO or poly(ethylene glycol) (PEG),
which is a liquid at temperatures such as 35oC. For example, PEG with a molecular
weight of 600 g/mole has a melting temperature of 17-22oC, according to the Aldrich
catalogue. PEG has been fabricated into membrane systems in two ways: as liquid
membranes and as blends with solid polymers [75-77,79,89,90].
       Kawakami et al. impregnated PEG (MW=300 g/mole) into the microporous
regions of a cellulose membrane filter with an average pore size of 0.2 µm [75,76]. This
membrane exhibited a CO2 permeability of 49 Barrers and CO2/N2 selectivity of 13 at
25oC and 0.2 atm. The addition of inorganic salts to PEG can improve CO2 separation
efficiency. For example, liquid membranes of PEG containing KF exhibited a CO2
permeability of 100 Barrers and CO2/N2 selectivity of 45 at 25oC and 0.2 atm. However,
liquid membranes are subject to two inherent disadvantages: potential instability resulting




                                            38
from liquid lost due to the pressure difference across the film and lower gas permeability
due to the micropores tortuosity in the solid substrate.
        Blends of liquid PEG with a compatible solid polymer can provide the necessary
mechanical integrity to sustain significant pressure differences across the membrane.
Heterogeneous PEG blends such as those with poly(dimethyl siloxane) (PDMS) [89] or
poly(1-trimethylsilyl-1-propyne) (PTMSP) [90], and homogeneous PEG blends such as
those with cellulose nitrate [79] or cellulose acetate have been reported [77]. Generally,
the non-PEG component (i.e., the second component) needs to be carefully chosen
because blends generally exhibit transport properties intermediate between those of the
two constituent components. In heterogeneous blends, the second component is usually
the continuous phase to maintain good mechanical properties. Therefore, this component
should have lower gas permeability so that the blends will exhibit gas selectivity similar
to that of PEG. On the other hand, the barrier property of the second component will
reduce CO2 permeability in the blend. The balance needs to be critically evaluated. The
underlying rationale will be further discussed in the next section. Currently PDMS and
PTMSP have been used as the continuous phase in such studies, even though they are
among the most permeable polymers known. For example, CO2 permeability is 3,200
and 28,000 Barrers in PDMS [26] and PTMSP [90], respectively, but only 140 Barrers in
estimated amorphous PEO [25]. The addition of 30 wt.% PEG (MW=300 g/mole) to
PTMSP increases CO2/N2 selectivity from 5.6 to 25, and the CO2 permeability of the
blend is 146 Barrers at 30oC. Entrapping liquid PEG (MW=600 g/mole) within solid
PDMS yields a CO2/H2 selectivity of about 7.5 at 25oC, compared to 4 in PDMS alone
[89].




                                             39
       In a homogeneous blend, gas transport properties, X, such as permeability or
selectivity, are usually expressed empirically as follows [91]:

                       ln X = Φ1 ln X 1 + Φ 2 ln X 2                              (2.27)

where Φ i is the volume fraction of component i, and the subscripts 1 and 2 represent

components 1 and 2, respectively. Eq 2.27 predicts that CO2 separation performance in a
blend would approach that of PEG as the PEG content increases; however, mechanical
properties would also approach those of PEG. For example, as PEG (MW=300 g/mole)
content increases from zero to 50 wt.% in blends with cellulose nitrate, CO2 permeability
increases from about 20 Barrers to about 120 Barrers, and CO2/N2 selectivity increases
from about 20 to about 45 at 25oC. On the other hand, the films become tacky, fragile,
and finally too weak to test for gas permeation properties when the PEG content reaches
60 wt.% [79]. In general, by carefully choosing the proper components and compositions
in blends containing PEG, one might achieve CO2 separation performance close to that of
PEG membranes with sufficient mechanical integrity to withstand high pressure.
However, this has yet to be demonstrated.

2.2.3.2 Block Copolymers

       Block copolymers containing PEO segments have been extensively investigated
for CO2 removal from light gases.           These copolymers typically have microphase
separated structures containing soft PEO segments and hard segments such as polyamides
[36,65,81,84] and polyimides [78]. The hard segments provide mechanical stability and
inhibit crystallization of PEO.     For example, in a commercial PEBAX® copolymer
containing 57 wt.% PEO and 43 wt.% Nylon 6, the melting temperature of the PEO phase
is 13oC, which is much lower than that of high molecular weight PEO (65oC) [36].




                                              40
Although block copolymers are more difficult to prepare than polymer blends, they
present a more efficient opportunity to incorporate a high content of PEO while still
maintaining good mechanical properties, since PEO is directly connected to relatively
immobile and tough hard segments by chemical bonds. For example, a polymer blend of
cellulose nitrate and PEG becomes too weak for gas permeation test when the PEG
content reaches 60 wt.%[79]. In contrast, PEBAX® containing 57 wt.% PEO can form a
robust film capable of sustaining gas pressures well in excess of 15 atm [65].
       Petropoulos reviewed approaches for evaluating gas permeability in binary
composite polymeric materials and observed that the Maxwell model could be used over
the whole composition range for a dispersion of isometric particles of such shape and
mode of packing that the interparticle gaps are uniformly maximized [92]:
                                P + 2Pc − 2 Φ d (Pc − Pd ) 
                       Pb = Pc  d                                                 (2.28)
                                Pd + 2Pc + Φ d (Pc − Pd ) 

where Φ d is the volume fraction of the discontinuous phase, and Pb, Pc and Pd are gas

permeability in the composite, continuous phase and discontinuous phase, respectively.
The composite could be heterogeneous polymer blends or phase separated block
copolymers. Eq 2.28 predicts that gas permeation properties of the composite would be
dominated by the continuous phase, suggesting that optimal phase separated materials
should have amorphous PEO as the continuous phase. Table 2.4 presents gas transport
properties in various phase separated block copolymers containing PEO. The detailed
chemical structures are recorded in Table 2.5. The values of CO2/N2 selectivity are quite
similar to that of PEO in all of these materials, suggesting that the PEO phase in these
block copolymers is the continuous path for gas diffusion [65,80]. On the other hand, the
widely varying gas permeability values demonstrate that gas permeability depends

strongly on the detailed morphology, such as the domain shape and spatial arrangement,
which could be influenced by the hard segment composition and the lengths of the PEO
and hard segment blocks.
       In most copolymers reported to date, the rigid phase has a much lower
permeability than the PEO phase [65,78]. For example, at 35oC, Nylon 6 exhibits a CO2



                                                         46
permeability of 0.21 Barrers [33], compared with 66 Barrers at 10 atm for PEBAX®
containing 57 wt.% PEO and 43 wt.% Nylon 6 [65]. On this basis, eq 2.28 can be
simplified by letting Pd/Pc=0:
                                1 − Φd 
                       Pb = Pc                                                     (2.29)
                                1 + Φ d 2 

       This model has been applied to the reported block copolymer data to provide a
crude estimate of effective gas permeability in the PEO phase, and these values are
summarized in Table 2.4. Some copolymers, such as B1, exhibit incomplete phase
separation, i.e., the PEO phase is partially miscible with the hard phase [80]. The
estimated CO2 permeability in the PEO phase of B1 is only 7 Barrers (cf. Table 2.4),
which suggests the existence of an intermixed phase that has properties between those of
pure, amorphous PEO and the hard segment component (cf. Tables 2.4 and 2.5). Table
2.4 illustrates that the hard phase affects physical properties of the soft phase; for
example, the measured Tg in the PEO phase varies by 30oC depending on the hard phase
composition and amount. The deviation of Tg in a microphase of a block copolymer from
its bulk value has been well documented and has been ascribed to the existence of a zone
of material, near the interface between the PEO phase and the hard segment phase, where
both components coexist and intermix [93]. Another view proposed by Bares is that the
interface of the PEO phase and the hard phase imposes constraints on the mobility of
PEO chains and thus influences Tg [93]. He was able to quantitatively relate the Tg
change in one phase to the surface area per unit volume of the phase undergoing the
change in Tg [93].    In both of these scenarios, the Tg of a microphase in a block
copolymer depends on the block molecular weight and microphase morphology. Table
2.4 also suggests a general trend, i.e., gas permeability in the PEO phase increases as the
Tg of PEO phase decreases.


                                                47
2.2.3.3 Crosslinked Poly(ethylene oxide)

       Graham proposed empirically that significant crystallinity in crosslinked PEO is
not evident when the molecular weight between crosslinks is lower than 1500 [94].
Various techniques are available to crosslink PEO [94]. For instance, radiation or radical
crosslinking of high molecular weight PEO has been reported, and crosslinking by
reactions of end groups such as hydroxyl or vinyl groups has been studied. Hirayama et
al. prepared crosslinked PEO from mixtures of poly(ethylene glycol) methacrylate (a
monomer containing 9 EO units) and poly(ethylene glycol) dimethacrylate (a crosslinker
containing 14 EO units) by plasma irradiation [6]. They reported that CO2 permeability
increases as monomer content increases while CO2/N2 selectivity remains almost
unchanged. They prepared a polymer containing 70 wt.% monomer; it exhibited a CO2
permeability of 260 Barrers and a CO2/N2 selectivity of about 48 at 35oC and 1 atm.
Such separation properties represent a significant improvement over those exhibited by
block copolymers.

2.2.3.4 Discussion

       Table 2.4 summarizes CO2 permeability and CO2/N2 selectivity in various PEO
containing materials at 35oC along with the glass transition temperature, estimated CO2
permeability and fractional free volume of the PEO phase. For consistency, all of the
permeability data are reported at 35oC, based on either direct measurement or
interpolation using data from the corresponding references. While CO2/N2 selectivity
remains relatively constant, indicating that PEO is continuous and provides the dominant
contribution to the permeation properties, CO2 permeability ranges from 7 to 345 Barrers.
Clearly, understanding the underlying mechanism leading to such a wide range of gas

Figure 2.7: CO2 permeability at 35oC measured in crosslinked PEO prepared by

permeability might lead to materials design rules to further improve acid gas separation
performance.
       Interestingly, Tg seems to be correlated with CO2 permeability, as illustrated in
Table 2.4. Figure 2.7 demonstrates the trend that PEO phases with lower Tg values
exhibit higher CO2 permeability. The CO2 permeability coefficients in the PEO phase
were estimated using eq 2.19 for the block copolymers.

2.3    SUMMARY

       Structure/property guidelines have been extensively explored in an effort to
improve the separation performance of polymer membranes for gas separation by
increasing polymer size sieving ability (i.e., diffusivity selectivity) [13,14]. However,
favorable solubility selectivity has not been fully pursued as a route to enhance gas


                                                                      49
separation properties, probably due to the fact that penetrant diffusion coefficients are
often more sensitive than solubility to polymer structure, and diffusion coefficients
usually change in a much broader range than penetrant solubility [14]. In this chapter, we
explore the possibility of harnessing interactions between CO2 and polymers containing
various polar groups to improve permeability/selectivity properties. By surveying CO2
and N2 sorption and/or transport in liquids and polymers containing different types and
amount of polar groups, liquids and polymers with a solubility parameter of about 21.8
MPa0.5 achieve the highest CO2 solubility and CO2/N2 solubility selectivity. So far, ether
oxygen3s in ethylene oxide (EO) units appear to be the most useful groups for achieving
high CO2 permeability and high CO2/light gas selectivity (e.g., CO2/H2).
       Crosslinking insures good chemical resistance of poly(ethylene oxide) containing
materials, simply because crosslinked networks are not soluble. It is straightforward to
incorporate more than 80 wt.% PEO in crosslinked polymers, which is higher than the
maximum value (about 60%) reported for block copolymers and blends [78,80].
Crosslinking could completely suppress crystallization at all temperatures of practical
interest [95], while block copolymers or blends would still exhibit a melting temperature
at temperatures near ambient [36]. Consequently, with the crosslinked PEO materials,
temperature can be lowered to optimize CO2 separation performance because lower
temperatures favor CO2/light gas solubility selectivity [95].
       This fundamental study further explores the favorable interactions between CO2
and ethylene oxide units, and their dependence on temperature and pressure.          New
crosslinked PEO materials were prepared, and the effect of crosslink density, comonomer
composition and chain end groups on gas transport was examined carefully to achieve
high CO2/H2 separation performance.

3.1    MATERIALS AND NOMENCLATURE

       Pure gases were used as received.        Helium, hydrogen, nitrogen, oxygen and
carbon dioxide had a purity of 99.9%. Methane, ethane, ethylene, propane and propylene
were of chemical purity (i.e., 99%). Mixed gas cylinders (such as CO2/H2 and CO2/CH4)
with certified composition were purchased from Air Liquide America Corporation
(Houston, TX). Sometimes, gas mixtures were made in the laboratory by adding two
gases into an initially evacuated cylinder. The cylinder was then mechanically rotated to
increase gas mixing efficiency. The cylinder was measured every 30 minutes using a gas
chromatograph (which had been calibrated using certified gas mixtures). The cylinder
was considered to be well-mixed and homogeneous when the composition did not change
with time.
       Poly(ethylene glycol) diacrylate (PEGDA: MW=743 g/mole), poly(ethylene
glycol) methyl ether acrylate (PEGMEA: MW=460 g/mole), poly(ethylene glycol)
acrylate (PEGA: MW=380 g/mole), deuterated chloroform (CDCl3), hexane, dodecane,
and 1-hydroxylcyclohexyl phenyl ketone (HCPK) were purchased from Aldrich
Chemical Company (Milwaukee, WI). Poly(ethylene glycol) dimethacrylate (PEGDMA:
MW=754 g/mole) was purchased from Monomer-Polymer & Dajac Labs (Feasterville,
PA). HCPK is also available under the Irgacure 184 trade name from Ciba Specialty
Chemicals (Tarrytown, NY). Irgacure 2959, 2-hydroxy-1-[4-2-(hydroxyethoxy)phenyl-
2-methyl-1-propanone], was purchased from Ciba. All solvents had a purity of at least
99%, except for the CDCl3 used for nuclear magnetic resonance studies, which had a
purity of 99.99%. All chemicals were used as received unless otherwise indicated.
Ultrapure water produced by a Milli-Q water purification system (Millipore Corporation,
Bedford, MA) was used throughout this study. The chemical structures of PEGDA,



                                           52
PEGMEA and PEGA are presented in Table 3.1.                   PEGDA and PEGDMA are
crosslinkers since they have two acrylate groups, while PEGMEA and PEGA are
monomers since they have only one polymerizable acrylate group.                     A scheme of
crosslinked PEO rubbers is presented in Figure 3.1.
       Three series of polymers, PEGDA/PEGMEA-XX, PEGDA/PEGA-XX and
XLPEGDA-XX, have been prepared using prepolymer solutions which contain
PEGDA/PEGMEA, PEGDA/PEGA and PEGDA/H2O, respectively. “XX” represents the
weight percent of PEGDA in the prepolymer solution.
