3.2    MONOMER AND CROSSLINKER CHARACTERIZATION

       Proton nuclear magnetic resonance (1H NMR) [96,97] and fast atom
bombardment mass spectrometry (FAB-MS) [96,98] were used to confirm the molecular
weight of the monomers and crosslinker. FAB-MS also provides information regarding
polydispersity.
       Samples for 1H NMR were prepared by dissolving dry monomer or crosslinker in
CDCl3 at concentrations between 5 and 10 wt.%. 1H NMR spectra were recorded on a
VARIAN INOVA-500 spectrometer operating at 500 MHz. 1H NMR data are reported in
ppm relative to tetramethylsilane (TMS). The spectrometer was routinely adjusted as
follows: spin rate, 20 rpm; pulse width, 2.0 µs; sweep width, 9256 Hz; line broadening
(for exponential multiplication), 0.1 Hz with 128K data points. The relaxation delay was
24 seconds (i.e., 5T1). PEGDA spectrum is illustrated in detail as an example here. 1H
NMR (CDCl3): δ 6.43 (2 H, cis CH2=CH), 5.82 (2H, trans CH2=CH), 6.15 (2H,


                                          54
ROOCCH=CH2), 4.32 (4H, RCOOCH2-CH2O), 3.73 (4H, RCOOCH2CH2O), 3.65 (4(n-
2) H, OCH2CH2OCH2CH2OCH2CH2O). The integrals for δ 6.43, δ 5.82 and δ 6.15
should be the same and equal to half of the integral of δ 4.32 or δ 3.73. The number of
repeat units of ethylene oxide in PEGDA, n, can be expressed based on the integral ratio
of δ 3.65 to that of any of the other peaks. Therefore, the molecular weight of PEGDA
can be calculated as follows:
                               1 Integral δ 3.65      
                       M n =                    + 2  × 44 + 126                   (3.1)
                               2 Integral δ 6.43      

The spectra for monomers are similar to that of PEGDA and not discussed for brevity.
       The FAB-MS instrument was a Finnigan MAT TSQ 700 mass spectrometer/data
system. The matrix used was m-nitrobenzylic alcohol (MNBA). The fast atom gun (Ion
Tech Model FAB-11-GG) provided xenon atoms at 7.2 keV. The instrumental resolution
(M/∆M) was 1000, and the ion source temperature was 70oC. Typical FAB-MS results
are presented as a graph of relative intensity versus mass/charge (m/z). Number (Mn) and
weight average (Mw) molecular weights are calculated as follows [99]:

                       M =
                           ∑N M , M = ∑N M
                                   i       i         i    i
                                                           2

                                                                                      (3.2)
                            ∑N        ∑N M
                         n                     w
                                       i              i   i



where Ni is proportional to the relative intensity, and Mi corresponds to m since z (charge)
usually equals 1. The polydispersity index is the ratio of Mw to Mn.

3.3    POLYMER PREPARATION

       The amount of initiator, either HCPK or Irgacure 2959, was 0.1 wt.% relative to
the total amount of crosslinker and monomer. After stirring, the solution was mixed with
a known amount of ultrapure water to form the target composition before being sonicated
for about 10 minutes to eliminate bubbles (Ultrasonic cleaner, Model FS60, Fisher



                                               55
Scientific, Pittsburg, PA). In some experiments, N2 was bubbled through the solution for
30 minutes prior to polymerization to remove dissolved oxygen. The solution was
sandwiched between two quartz plates, which were separated by spacers to control film
thickness. The solution was polymerized by exposure to 312 nm UV light in a UV
Crosslinker (Model FB-UVXL-1000, Fisher Scientific) for 90 seconds at 3 mW/cm2.
The solid films obtained by this process were three dimensional networks (i.e., gels) and
contained a negligible amount of low molecular weight polymer (i.e., sol) that was not
bound to the network. The as-synthesized film was immersed in a large amount of
ultrapure water for at least 5 days to allow any sol to diffuse out of the gels. The water
was changed daily.

3.4    DENSITY AND FILM THICKNESS

       The density of dry polymer samples was determined by hydrostatic weighing
using a Mettler Toledo balance (Model AG204, Switzerland) and a density determination
kit [25]. The film density ( ρ p ) is calculated as follows:

                                 MA
                        ρp =          ⋅ ρo                                            (3.3)
                               MA −ML

where MA is the film weight in air, ML is the film weight in the auxiliary liquid, and ρo is
the density of the auxiliary liquid. Hexane or dodecane was used as the auxiliary liquid
since poly(ethylene oxide) does not have an affinity for these alkanes [100,101].
       Film thickness was measured using a digital micrometer (Mitutoyo Corporation,
Japan), which can measure thickness to the nearest micrometer. To measure thickness of
rubbery polymers, the films were covered with a uniform thin plate (quartz, glass or some
other flat, rigid material) to prevent direct contact of the measuring tip of the digital
micrometer with the soft film. The measurement tip can press into flexible films or even




                                              56
penetrate them; in either case, the thickness value provided by the micrometer is an
underestimate of the true film thickness.

3.5    FOURIER TRANSFORM INFRARED SPECTROSCOPY

       Attenuated total reflection Fourier transform infrared spectroscopy (FTIR-ATR)
was used to determine the conversion of acrylate groups.          The spectrometer was a
Thermo Nicolet Nexus 470 (Madison, WI). For each measurement, 128 spectra were
accumulated at a resolution of 4 cm-1. The disappearance of acrylate double bonds due to
polymerization leads to the decrease of sharp peaks at 810 cm-1 (the twisting vibration of
the acrylic CH2=CH bond) [102], at 1410 cm-1 (deformation of the CH2=CH bond)
[103,104], and at 1190 cm-1 (acrylic C=O bond) [103].

3.6    WIDE ANGLE X-RAY DIFFRACTION

       Wide angle X-ray diffraction (WAXD) patterns were obtained using a Bruker-
Nonius D8 Advance Theta-2Theta Powder Diffractometer or a Scintag theta-theta
diffractometer with Cu Kα radiation (1.54 Å). The generator was operated at 40 kV and
30 mA for Bruker-Nonius and 45 kV and 40 mA for Scintag. Polymer samples were
supported on a silicon wafer, which does not produce any peak in the diffraction range of
interest, i.e. from 5o to 40o (2θ). Low temperatures (i.e., -20oC) were achieved using an
enclosed, evacuated sample cell that was cooled with liquid N2.
       Because of short range order, amorphous polymers exhibit a broad halo. The
d-spacing, which corresponds to the position of the diffraction maximum, can be
calculated using Bragg’s Law [105]:
                                 λo
                       ds =                                                         (3.4)
                              2 sin θ s




                                            57
where λo is the wavelength of Cu Kα radiation (1.54 Å), and θs is the angle of the
reflection peak.

3.7    THERMAL CHARACTERIZATION

       Thermal transitions were determined using a TA Instruments (New Castle, DE)
Q1000 differential scanning calorimeter (DSC). Samples were scanned at a heating rate
of 20oC/min from -90oC to 0oC or above under a dry N2 purge at a flow rate of 50
ml/min. The glass transition temperature was taken as the midpoint of the heat capacity
step change.
       Dynamic mechanical thermal analysis was performed in the vicinity of the glass
transition using a Polymer Laboratories DMTA operating in single cantilever bending
geometry. The polymer films had a thickness of 1mm and were dried under vacuum at
room temperature prior to measurement. Storage modulus (E′) and loss tangent (tanδ)
were recorded at a heating rate of 1°C/min with test frequencies in the range of 0.1 to 10
Hz.    All measurements were carried out under inert (N2) atmosphere.               These
measurements were performed in the laboratory of Professor Douglass S. Kalika at the
University of Kentucky.

3.8    POSITRON ANNIHILATION LIFETIME SPECTROSCOPY (PALS)

       PALS measurements were performed using an automated EG&G Ortec fast-fast
coincidence system with a resolution of 240 ps. Polymer films were stacked ( ≈ 1 mm
thick) on either side of a 22NaCl source, and the assembly was sealed in aluminum foil
(which has no influence on the o-Ps lifetimes measured). At least 5 spectra of 30,000
peak counts were collected for each sample in a dry N2 atmosphere, and each spectrum
took approximately 1.5 hours to collect. The data were fit with three lifetimes using the
PFPOSFIT program; the shortest lifetime was fixed to 125 ps, which is characteristic of



                                           58
p-Ps self-annihilation. The intermediate lifetimes are related to the free and trapped
positrons, which are typically between 350 and 450 ps for polymers [10,106]. The
longest measured lifetimes (τ3) and their intensities (I3) correspond to the annihilation of

ortho-positronium (o-Ps) components in the free volume cavities of the polymer matrix,
and these parameters are used to estimate free volume [10,106]. The PALS experiments
were conducted in the laboratory of Dr. Anita Hill at the CSIRO Manufacturing Science
and Technology, Clayton, Victoria, Australia.

3.9    PURE AND MIXED GAS PERMEATION MEASUREMENTS

       Pure gas permeability in polymers was determined using a constant
volume/variable pressure apparatus [65]. The permeation cell is a stainless steel filter
holder from Millipore Corporation (47 mm disc filter, Bedford, MA) with an area of 13.8
cm2. Polymer samples were partially masked using impermeable aluminum tape on the
upstream face, or on the upstream and downstream faces as described by Mogri and Paul
[107]. Both masking methods gave very similar permeability values, consistent with the
observations of Mogri and Paul [107]. The o-ring in the permeation cell was in direct
contact with the aluminum tape so that the soft rubbery films would not be damaged by
the o-ring. After aluminum tape masking, the surface area of the sample available for gas
transport was 5.1 or 1.98 cm2.
       After a film was mounted in the system, both upstream and downstream volumes
were exposed to vacuum overnight to degas the film. The leak rate in the system was
always measured before starting the permeation experiments, and then the pressure
increase in the downstream volume was recorded. Gas permeability (cm3(STP) cm/(cm2
s cmHg)) was calculated from the steady state rate of pressure increase in a fixed
downstream volume:




                                            59
                                Vd l   dp         dp
                      PA =           [( 1 ) ss − ( 1 )leak ]                        (3.5)
                             p2 Am RT dt          dt

where Vd is the downstream volume (cm3), l is the film thickness (cm), p2 is the

upstream absolute pressure (cmHg), Am is the film area available for gas transport (cm2),
the gas constant, R is 0.278 cmHg cm3/(cm3(STP) K), T is absolute temperature (K) and
  dp           dp
( 1 ) ss and ( 1 ) leak are the steady state rates of pressure rise (cmHg/s) in the
   dt           dt

downstream volume at a fixed upstream pressure and under vacuum, respectively.
 dp                                      dp
( 1 ) leak was usually less than 10% of ( 1 ) ss . The downstream pressure was always
  dt                                      dt

less than 2 cmHg, which was very low compared with lowest upstream pressure
considered (4 atm).
        Mixed gas permeability coefficients were measured using a constant
pressure/variable volume system connected to a gas chromatograph equipped with a
thermal conductivity detector (TCD) and a Flame Ionization Detector (FID) [34]. For
CO2/CH4 studies, the TCD was used to detect CO2, and the FID was used to detect CH4
while He was used as the carrier gas. For CO2/H2 studies, the TCD was used, and the
carrier gas was argon. By measuring the permeate gas concentration in the sweep gas
and the sweep gas flow rate, S, the permeability of gas A can be calculated as follows
[34]:
                                   x1 A Sl
                      PA =                                                          (3.6)
                                   (
                             x Am p2 x2 A − p1 x1 A
                              P
                              c                       )
where x1 A and xcP are the mole fractions of component A and the sweep gas in the

permeate stream, respectively, and x2 A is the mole fraction of component A in the feed

gas.




                                               60
3.10   SORPTION MEASUREMENTS

       Gas solubility was determined using a dual-volume, dual-transducer apparatus
based on the barometric, pressure-decay method [36,108]. The system consists of a
sample cell containing a polymer sample and a charge cell connected to a gas cylinder.
In the first step, the sample cell is evacuated and then isolated from the charge cell, which
is subsequently charged with a target gas at a known pressure. The valve between the
charge and sample cells is opened briefly to introduce gas to the sample cell. The valve
between the sample and charge cells is closed, and the pressure in the sample cell is
monitored as a function of time. The difference between the initial and final pressure in
the charge cell can be used to calculate the number of moles of gas admitted into the
sample cell. As the polymer sample sorbs gas, the pressure in the gas phase of the
sample cell decreases. When the sample cell pressure reaches a stable value, the polymer
has sorbed all of the gas that it can at the final pressure in the sample cell (i.e.,
equilibrium is achieved). From the final pressure in the sample cell, the number of moles
of gases in the gas phase of the sample cell can be calculated. The difference between the
moles of gas admitted to the sample cell from the charge cell and the final number of
moles of gas in the gas phase of the sample cell is the number of moles of gas sorbed by
the sample at the final pressure of the sample cell. The second step is to add more gas to
the charge cell and repeat the procedure outlined above to obtain a data point at a higher
pressure.
       Special care was taken to make the sorption measurements as accurate as possible
because, in these rather polar PEO-containing materials, gas solubility is low. Dry
polymer samples for sorption studies were selected so that they always had similar
volumes, and the same routine of pressure increase was followed for each penetrant
studied. Before beginning the experiments, a blank experiment was run using the gas at


                                             61
the temperature of interest. That is, steel spheres with almost the same volume as that of
the polymer sample were put in the sample cell, and the same pressure increment history
was followed for the blank experiments (i.e., those with steel balls in the sample cell) and
the actual experiments (i.e., those with polymer samples in the sample cell). Ideally, the
spheres adsorb immeasurable amounts of gas, and the hypothetical sorption of gases on
the spheres is zero. However, the uncertainty in the pressure and volumes used in the
experiments always contributes to an uncertainty in the solubility. For example, the
apparent solubility values from the blank experiments were -0.015 cm3 (STP)/(cm3 atm)
for CH4 and -0.01 cm3 (STP)/(cm3 atm) for CO2. These values represent only about 10%
of the CH4 solubility in the polymers, and about 0.6% of the CO2 solubility in the
polymers. The reported solubility coefficients were the measured gas solubility values
after subtraction of the apparent solubility values from the blank experiments.

3.11   UNCERTAINTY ANALYSIS

       Uncertainty in a parameter was estimated using a standard propagation of errors
analysis, where uncertainties of all relevant measured parameters propagate and
contribute to the uncertainty of the parameter of interest [59].




                                             62
                                    CHAPTER 4




    Gas Solubility, Diffusivity and Permeability in Poly(ethylene oxide)1




1
  This chapter has been adapted with permission from an article published under the same
title in the Journal of Membrane Science, 239, 105-17, 2004.

                                           63
4.1    SUMMARY

       The effect of pressure on the solubility, diffusivity, and permeability of He, H2,
O2, N2, CO2, CH4, C2H4, C2H6, C3H6 and C3H8 in poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO) is reported
at 35°C. Additionally, the temperature dependence of permeability is reported. The
effect of polar ether linkages in PEO on gas transport is illustrated by comparing
transport properties in PEO with those in polyethylene (PE). For example, at 35°C and
infinite dilution, semi-crystalline PEO exhibits CO2 permeability coefficient of 12
Barrers, and CO2/H2 and CO2/N2 pure gas selectivities of 6.7 and 48, respectively. In
contrast, at similar conditions, the permeability of PE to CO2 is 13 Barrers, but the
CO2/N2 selectivity is only 13.        In addition to good separation properties for
quadrupolar/nonpolar gas pairs, PEO also shows interestingly high selectivity for olefins
over paraffins, which is ascribed to favorable interaction between the polar ether groups
in PEO and olefins. For example, the infinite dilution permeability of PEO to propylene
is 3.8 Barrers and pure gas propylene/propane selectivity is 2.7 at 35oC. At similar
conditions, PE exhibits propylene/propane selectivity of only 1.4.




                                            64
4.2     INTRODUCTION

        Polymers containing polar moieties, such as ether groups, have an affinity for CO2
due to dipole-quadrupole interactions [109]. This affinity may be harnessed to prepare
materials that are more permeable to CO2 than to H2.               Because of the interest in
improving CO2 separation performance, there have been many recent studies of polymer
membranes containing poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO), poly(ethylene glycol) (PEG) or other
groups bearing polar ether segments that can interact favorably with acid gases such as
CO2 [6,36,65,76-86,110-112]. Despite this interest in PEO-based materials for acid gas
selective separations, a systematic study of the solubility, diffusivity, and permeability
properties of pure poly(ethylene oxide) has not been reported, due in large measure to its
high crystallinity and, consequently, low transport property values. In this chapter, we
present physical characterization and gas transport properties of solution cast PEO films.
The effect of pressure on gas permeability, diffusivity and solubility is reported for a
variety of non-polar gases (He, H2, O2, N2), a series of olefinic and aliphatic
hydrocarbons (CH4, C2H6, C2H4, C3H8, C3H6), and CO2. The effect of temperature on
permeability is also reported. Relevant physical properties of these penetrants, which
will be used later in the discussion, are recorded in Table 4.1.

4.3     EXPERIMENTAL

4.3.1   Materials and Sample Preparation

        Poly(ethylene oxide) with a weight-average molecular weight of approximately
1,000,000 was purchased from Aldrich Chemical Company, Milwaukee, WI and used as-
received.




                                             65
Table 4.1   Penetrant parameters characterizing size (critical volume, Vc, and kinetic
            diameter, d) and condensability (critical temperature, Tc)

                                   Size                              Condensability

              Vc [39] [cm3/mole]             d [71] [Å]                Tc [39] [K]

He                   57.4                       2.6                       5.19

H2                   65.1                       2.89                      33.24

O2                   73.4                       3.46                      154.6

N2                   89.8                       3.64                      126.2

CO2                  93.9                       3.3                      304.21

CH4                  99.2                       3.8                      191.05

C2H4                 130.4                                               282.40

C2H6                 148.3                                               305.35

C3H6                 181.0                                                364.9

C3H8                 203.0                                                369.8



       Gas permeation and sorption experiments were conducted using dense PEO films
of uniform thickness, prepared by dissolving solid PEO in ultrapure water, which is
produced by a Milli-Q water purification system (Millipore Corporation, Bedford, MA).
Films were cast from 3 wt% water solutions into plastic or Teflon flat-bottomed Petri
dishes. After air-drying at ambient conditions for 3 to 7 days, films were removed from
the dish and placed on a Teflon plate. PEO films were stored under vacuum in an oven at
room temperature for 24 hours to assist in removal of residual water and then annealed at
70-80oC for two hours. After annealing, samples were removed from the oven and




                                           66
cooled at ambient conditions before being placed in a desiccator, where they were stored
until use. Film thicknesses were in the range of 100 to 300 µm.
        Initially, it was difficult to prepare defect free PEO films. We arrived at the film
preparation protocol described in the previous paragraph after trying several solvents
(acetonitrile, chloroform, and water), concentrations and evaporation rates. However,
these variables did not have as much impact on the ability to prepare defect free samples
as annealing. Generally, samples prepared without annealing were defective (i.e., they
showed similar permeabilities to all gases). In contrast, annealing solvent cast films
above the polymer melting temperature resulted in defect free films. Because PEO is
susceptible to oxidative degradation, due to the presence of weak C-O bonds in the
backbone [113], the annealing time needed to be as short as possible and the annealing
temperature needed to be as low as possible. Therefore, to minimize the degradation and
prepare a nonporous film, we decided to anneal the sample under vacuum just above its
melting temperature for 2 hours.

4.3.2   Thermal Characterization

        Thermal transitions were determined using a Perkin Elmer (Shelton, CT) DSC 7
differential scanning calorimeter (DSC). Experiments were performed at a heating rate
of 20oC/min over temperatures ranging from 0 to 120oC. The degree of crystallinity was
evaluated from the area under the melting endotherm in the first scan [105].

4.4     RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

4.4.1   Thermal and Physical Characterization

        Figure 4.1 presents a first scan DSC thermogram of PEO. The peak of the
melting endotherm is 68oC, which is near the value of 66oC reported for PEO with a




                                            67
molecular weight of 1,000,000 [114]. The weight fraction crystallinity in the film, χc,
was estimated as follows [105]:
                                           ∆H
                      χc =                                                           (4.1)
                                           ∆H c

where ∆H is the melting enthalpy, obtained from the area under the melting endotherm,
and ∆Hc is the melting enthalpy of 100% crystalline PEO. From the DSC experiment,
∆H was 121 J/g, which is close to the literature value of 125 J/g for PEO with a molecular
weight of 4,000,000 [105]. A variety of values of ∆Hc exist in the literature ranging from
166.4 to 265 J/g [115]. In this study, we used the value of 166.4 J/g suggested by Simon
and Rutherford [114]. Using this value in eq 4.1 yields a crystallinity value of 72.7
weight percent.
                         )

                                                            68
       Volume fraction crystallinity, φ c , was calculated as follows:

                      φc = (ρ P / ρc ) × χ c                                        (4.2)

where ρP is the measured PEO density, and ρc is the crystal density calculated from the
unit cell dimensions of a PEO crystallite, which is 1.234 g/cm3 at room temperature
[114]. PEO film density, ρP, was 1.209 g/cm3, which is consistent with values (1.20-1.22
g/cm3) reported in the literature for PEO [116]. Using eq 4.2, the crystallinity was 71
volume percent.
       The volume percent crystallinity can also be estimated from the density using the
following relation:
                             ρP − ρa
                      φc =                                                          (4.3)
                             ρc − ρ a

where ρa is the density of amorphous PEO (1.124 g/cm3) [114]. The φ c value calculated

from eq 4.3 was 0.77, which is consistent with that obtained from eq 4.2. In the
remainder of this report, the crystallinity calculated from the DSC result will be used to
estimate amorphous phase permeability and solubility values.
       Transport properties in polymers are sensitive to the amount of free volume in the
polymer matrix [23]. In semi-crystalline polymers such as PE and PEO, transport is
presumed to occur only in the amorphous regions of the polymers, so it is of interest to
compute the fractional free volume in the amorphous regions of such polymers. The
densities of amorphous PEO and PE were taken to be 1.124 g/cm3 at 20oC [114] and
0.854 g/cm3 at 25oC [117], respectively. Using these values and eq 2.14, the FFV of the
amorphous phase of PEO is 0.139. This value is significantly lower than that of PE,
which has an amorphous phase FFV of 0.188. These values are recorded in Table 4.2.

4.4.2   Permeability

        Figures 4.2a and 4.2b present permeability coefficients for gases at 35oC as a
function of upstream pressure.       For the permanent gases (He, H2, O2, and N2),
permeability coefficients are essentially independent of pressure, while the permeability
coefficients of CO2 and hydrocarbons such as C3H8 increase with increasing pressure.
This behavior is consistent with gas permeation properties in rubbery polymers [26]. For
strongly sorbing penetrants, such as CO2 and C3H8, high penetrant concentrations can
plasticize the polymer matrix by increasing polymer local segmental motion, thus
enhancing penetrant diffusion coefficients and, in turn, permeability [119]. Additionally,
the solubility of condensable components typically increases with increasing pressure,
and this factor also acts to increase permeability coefficients [120].

Figure 4.2                                     Permeability coefficients of (a) light gases and (b) hydrogen and several
                                               hydrocarbons in semi-crystalline PEO at 35oC as a function of upstream
                                               pressure. The scale for gas permeability values is shown on the left y-axis
                                               for all gases except CO2, whose scale is on the right y-axis.

                                  For the data in Figures 4.2a and 4.2b, the pressure dependence of the permeability
coefficients may be described by the following simple empirical model [26]:

                                                            PA = PA, o (1 + mP , L ∆p ) = PA, o (1 + mP , L p2 )                                                                                  (4.4)

where PA,o is the permeability coefficient at an upstream pressure, p2, of 0 (i.e., infinite
dilution permeability), mP,L is an adjustable constant, and ∆p is the difference between the
upstream and downstream pressure, ∆p=p2-p1. Since downstream pressure, p1 is much
less than upstream pressure p2, ∆p can be replaced by p2. Infinite dilution permeability
coefficients for different penetrants were calculated using eq 4.4.
                                  Gas transport properties in a semi-crystalline polymer such as PEO are usually
modeled by assuming that the crystals act as an impermeable dispersed phase imbedded
in an amorphous phase and by then developing models for the influence of crystallinity



                                                                                                                      71
on solubility and diffusivity. In a rubbery polymer, the effect of crystallinity on penetrant
sorption is typically represented as follows [117]:

                        S A = S A, a φ a                                               (4.5)

where SA is the observed solubility coefficient, SA,a is the solubility coefficient in the
amorphous polymer, and φ a is the amorphous phase volume fraction.

        The influence of crystallinity on diffusivity is traditionally described as follows
[88]:
                                D A, a
                        DA =                                                           (4.6)
                               τ c βc

where DA,a is the diffusion coefficient in the amorphous polymer, τ c is a tortuosity factor,
and β c is a chain immobilization factor. τ c characterizes the tortuosity of the amorphous

phase caused by the presence of impermeable crystallites dispersed in it. Simple models
from composites theory, such as the one given below, are often used to describe the
influence of crystallinity on tortuosity [88]:
                               1
                       τc =                                                            (4.7)
                              φa

        The chain immobilization factor, β c , accounts for the restricted segmental
mobility in the amorphous phase by crystallites. In the simplest case, when β c = 1 (i.e.,

no chain immobilization), gas permeability is given by [88]:

                        PA = S A × D A = PA,a × φ a2                                   (4.8)

where PA,a is the estimated permeability of penetrant A in amorphous phase polymer.
        The amorphous phase permeability coefficients were estimated using eq 4.8. This
calculation presumes that tortuosity obeys eq 4.7 and that the chain immobilization
factor, β , is unity. Because we did not have PEO samples with varying levels of



                                                 72
crystallinity, neither of these assumptions can be tested. As a result, the estimated
amorphous phase permeability coefficients should be regarded as rather crude estimates
of the actual permeation properties of the amorphous material. These data are reported in
Table 4.3. The permeability coefficients decrease in the following order:
                CO2 > C3H6 > C2H4 > H2 ≈ He > C3H8 ≈ C2H6 > CH4 ≈ O2 > N2
       Except for CO2 and the olefins, this order of permeability coefficients is typical
for rubbery polymers [26]. Generally, penetrants with higher critical temperature are
more condensable and, therefore, more soluble; however, more condensable penetrants
often have larger critical volume values (cf. Table 4.1), which reduces diffusion
coefficients.   Permeability reflects the tradeoff between these often conflicting
contributions from solubility and diffusivity.
       The gas transport properties of polyethylene (PE) have been well studied
[88,117].   Polyethylene provides an interesting material for comparison with PEO
because PE is essentially PEO without the polar ether linkages.         Several physical
properties of these two polymers are compared in Table 4.2. The amorphous phase FFV
of PEO is significantly lower than that of PE, and the solubility parameter of PEO is
higher than that of PE. These trends are qualitatively consistent with the much more
polar nature of PEO.
       Table 4.3 presents a comparison of infinite dilution permeability coefficients in
amorphous PEO and PE.          Unlike PEO, literature values for tortuosity and chain
immobilization factors are available for PE [88], and these data were used to estimate
amorphous phase permeability coefficients in PE.       With the exception of CO2, PE
exhibits higher permeability values than PEO, even though the PE data were determined
at lower temperature (25oC, rather than 35oC for PEO). CO2 is the only gas exhibiting

higher permeability in PEO than in PE. Interestingly, CO2 is much more permeable than

selectivity in PEO is 6.7 at infinite dilution and increases to 9.9 at 14.7 atm, which is

can also consider the CO2/N2 selectivity. In PE, the pure gas CO2/N2 selectivity at
infinite dilution is approximately 12. However, in polar PEO, the CO2/N2 pure gas
selectivity at infinite dilution is 48, which is essentially equivalent to the selectivity in
block copolymers containing PEO, as shown in Table 2.4. These results are consistent
with enhanced solubility of quadrupolar CO2 in PEO.

analogues. The pure gas C3H6/C3H8 selectivity at infinite dilution and 35°C is 2.7, which

interactions between the polar ether linkages and olefins. Sorption data will be presented
later to support this hypothesis.
       The effect of temperature on permeation properties was determined. Permeability
coefficients were measured at 25oC, 35oC and 45oC. Table 4.5 presents representative
permeability coefficients at different temperatures and pressures. All penetrants exhibit
higher permeability at higher temperature. After experiments at 45oC, which was the
highest temperature explored and was, therefore, closest to the melting point of PEO, H2
permeability at 35oC was re-measured to confirm that PEO transport properties had not
changed as a result of performing experiments at 45°C. No significant difference in H2
permeability coefficients was observed.
       At each temperature, with the exception of N2 at 25oC, the permeability
coefficients were extrapolated to infinite dilution by fitting the data to eq 4.4. Since there
is only one data point for N2 at 25oC, this value was used as the effective infinite dilution
permeability coefficient for this case. This should be a reasonable approximation since
N2 permeability is essentially insensitive to pressure.       Using these infinite dilution
permeability coefficients, the activation energy of permeation was estimated using eq
2.15. EP values in PEO were positive, indicating that permeability coefficients increased
with increasing temperature. Generally, penetrant activation energy of diffusion (ED) is
positive and increases with increasing penetrant size [23]. Condensation is exothermic
(i.e., ∆Hcond <0), and the magnitude of the enthalpy change on condensation increases
with increasing penetrant condensability, as characterized by properties such as critical
temperature. The enthalpy of mixing (∆Hm) can be negative or positive, depending on
the energetic interactions between the condensed penetrant and the polymer. If penetrant


                                             78
molecules have favorable interactions with polymer segments, such as dipole-quadrupole
interaction, the mixing enthalpy can be negative. As shown in Table 4.3, there is no
monotonic trend of Ep values with penetrant size, which suggests that each term in eq
2.19 may have an important influence on Ep.
        Based on the data presented in Table 4.5, pure gas selectivity values were
calculated as a function of temperature and pressure, and these data are recorded in Table
4.6. In general, selectivity increases as temperature decreases. For example, the pure gas
selectivity of CO2/H2 at 7.8 atm is 6.9 at 45oC and increases to 9.5 at 25oC. These results
suggest that operation of a PEO membrane at lower temperature should result in better
CO2/H2 separation performance.

4.4.3   Solubility

        Figures 4.3a and 4.3b present sorption isotherms of various penetrants at 35oC in
semi-crystalline PEO. For less soluble penetrants, such as O2, N2, CH4, C2H6 and C2H4,
the isotherms are linear and solubility, SA, is constant.        We represent the average
solubility for these gases as S A∞ . The more soluble penetrants, such as C3H6, C3H8 and

CO2, exhibit slightly convex isotherms which may be described by the Flory-Huggins
theory, as illustrated in eq 2.9. Since penetrants are not soluble in the crystalline regions,
volume fraction of penetrants is given by:

                       φ2 =

                              φa + (C / 22,414 )V2

where φa is amorphous phase volume fraction in the polymer. χ values were obtained

by fitting experimental measurements of C as a function of p to eqs 2.9 and 4.9. At
infinite dilution, eqs 2.9 and 4.9 can be simplified to:

data are compared with solubility values in amorphous PE at 25oC [117]. For permanent

Figure 4.4   Estimated amorphous, infinite dilution solubility coefficients in
             poly(ethylene oxide) (35oC) (•) and poly(ethylene) (25oC) (ο) [117]. The

CO2 solubility in PEG was 1.3 cm3(STP)/(cm3 atm), which is also consistent with our
estimate of CO2 solubility in amorphous PEO.
       Figure 4.4 further demonstrates the extraordinarily high level of CO2 sorption in
PEO relative to solubilities of penetrants, such as C2H6, that have similar critical
temperatures but cannot participate in specific interactions with the polymer matrix. In
PE, for example, the CO2/C2H6 solubility selectivity is 0.35. However, in more polar
PEO, the CO2/C2H6 solubility selectivity is 3.1, which is nearly an order of magnitude
higher than that observed in PE. Apparently, CO2 experiences favorable interactions with
the ether groups in PEO. The interaction between quadrupolar CO2 and polar groups in
polymers is well known [124]. Van Amerongen studied the effect of polar acrylonitrile
content in butadiene/acrylonitrile copolymers on CO2 solubility and found that polymers


                                                                                           82
with higher concentrations of polar acrylonitrile had higher CO2 sorption [66]. Koros
suggested that the solubility selectivity for the CO2/CH4 system increases as the mass
density of polar carbonyl or sulfone groups in the polymer system increases [35]. Bondar
et al. studied gas sorption in a series of copolymer (Pebax) containing polyether
segments; they found that solubility selectivity of carbon dioxide over nonpolar gases
increases as ether linkage concentration in the polymer increases [36].
       Interestingly, olefins exhibit higher solubility than their paraffin analogs in PEO.
Often, an olefin (e.g., propylene) exhibits lower solubility than its paraffin analog (e.g.,
propane) in polymers (e.g., PE) due to the slightly lower critical temperature of the olefin.
However, PEO exhibits the opposite behavior.              As shown in Table 4.4, the
propylene/propane solubility selectivity at infinite dilution is 1.7, which, together with a
propylene/propane diffusivity selectivity of 1.6, gives an overall selectivity of 2.7. On
the other hand, in amorphous PE, the propylene/propane solubility selectivity is
approximately a factor of two lower than that in PEO, which diminishes the
propylene/propane permeability selectivity to 1.4. Both polymers have similar diffusivity
selectivity values (1.5 in PE and 1.6 in PEO). Additionally, the C2H4/C2H6 solubility
selectivity in PEO is 1.4, even though C2H4 has a lower Tc value and, on this basis, would
be expected to be less soluble than C2H6. Together, these results indicate that olefins
undergo favorable interaction with the polar ether oxygens in PEO.
       Olefin solubility is also enhanced in liquids such as tetrahydrofuran, a cyclic
ether. Polar organic solvents, such as tetrahydrofuran (THF), are Lewis acids and, as
such, they can interact with Lewis bases, such as the π electrons in aromatic
hydrocarbons or olefins [125,126].       Therefore, such polar solvents form solubility-
enhancing complexes with olefins but not with paraffins [125,126].

Figure 4.5                                 Effect of temperature on C2H4 and C2H6 solubility in (a) liquid
                                           tetrahydrofuran (THF) [48] and (b) liquid n-hexane [127,128].

                                    To better illustrate the interesting behavior of olefin sorption in polar and non-
polar polymers, olefin and paraffin solubilities in a polar solvent were compared with
those in a non-polar solvent. THF was selected as the polar solvent since it has an ether
oxygen, and its interactions with aromatic hydrocarbons have been documented [125].
Hexane, a well-studied paraffin, was chosen as a typical non-polar hydrocarbon solvent.
Figure 4.5a presents the solubilities of C2H4 and C2H6 in THF as a function of
temperature. The solubilities of C2H4 and C2H6 in n-hexane as a function of temperature
are illustrated in Figure 4.5b. In THF, C2H6 has somewhat higher solubility at higher
temperature. However, C2H4 solubility increases more rapidly than that of C2H6 as
temperature decreases. As a result, at 0oC, C2H4 is significantly more soluble than C2H6
in this polar, ether-containing solvent. In contrast, over the entire range of temperatures
investigated, C2H6 is more soluble than C2H4 in n-hexane, consistent with the higher
critical temperature of C2H6. These results suggest that olefins are more soluble than


                                                                                            84
paraffins in polar liquids such as THF as long as the temperature is low enough to allow
specific interactions to play a dominant role in the thermodynamic interactions between
the olefin and the liquid.

4.4.4   Diffusivity

        Based on eqs 2.5, 4.6 and 4.7, amorphous phase diffusion coefficients for various
penetrants at infinite dilution in PEO at 35oC were estimated and are recorded in Table
4.8, where the gases are arranged in order of increasing critical volume. The diffusivity
in PEO was also compared with that in amorphous phase PE at 25oC [88]. Generally,
diffusion coefficients decrease with increasing critical volume except for CO2 which, due
to its strongly non-spherical shape, presents a significantly smaller effective cross-section
for diffusion than indicated by its critical volume. Ethylene also shows similar behavior;
its diffusivity is higher than that of methane, despite ethylene’s larger critical volume.
Generally speaking, diffusion coefficients are higher in PE than in PEO. However, there
are several complicating factors that make a direct comparison difficult. First, the data
for PE and PEO are at different temperatures and different crystallinities. Additionally,
diffusivity is very sensitive to the distribution of inter-crystalline spacings in semi-
crystalline polymers [88], which is dependent on the solid film preparation process. The
crystallite structure in the PE sample was not discussed in the paper in which the
transport data were reported [88].    Nevertheless, it is unlikely that the PEO studied in
this paper would necessarily have similar macro-crystalline structure. For example,
polyethylene crystallites have dimensions in the 4-30 nm range [129], while PEO may
contain macrospherulites with diameters up to 2 cm [113]. Therefore, tortuosity and
chain immobilization factors characterizing the effect of crystallites on penetrant
diffusion could well be different in PE and PEO. Since, in this study, we do not have

       Figure 4.6 presents calculated effective diffusion coefficients in semi-crystalline
PEO as a function of local concentration for several hydrocarbons and CO2. For all of
the penetrants shown, Deff increases with increasing local concentration, suggesting that
these penetrants plasticize the polymer matrix. This behavior is consistent with our
previous argument that higher penetrant pressure can increase permeability by enhancing
penetrant average diffusivity through the film.                              Interestingly, poly(dimethylsiloxane)
(PDMS), which does not exhibit a particular affinity for CO2, exhibits plasticization to


                                                                87
C2H6 and C3H8 but not to CO2, even at CO2 concentrations as high as 80 cm3(STP)/cm3
[26,119];      on the other hand, PEO was significantly plasticized by CO2 even at
concentrations of the order of 10 cm3(STP)/(cm3 amorphous polymer).

4.5    CONCLUSION

       Permeation and sorption properties of a variety of gases were determined in
poly(ethylene oxide), a polar rubbery polymer.         Relative to its non-polar analog,
polyethylene, PEO exhibits much higher CO2/H2 pure gas selectivity due to high CO2/H2
solubility selectivity, which is qualitatively consistent with the notion that polar, basic
ether linkages interact favorably with acidic penetrants such as CO2, thus increasing CO2
sorption. Interestingly, PEO is more permeable to olefins than to paraffins due, in part, to
enhanced olefin/paraffin solubility selectivity. Presumably, the double bonds in the
olefins interact favorably with the polar ether linkages, and paraffins cannot access this
interaction.




                                            88
                                           CHAPTER 5




      The Effect of Crosslinking on Gas Permeability in Crosslinked
                     Poly(ethylene glycol diacrylate)1




1 Reduced in part with permission from Macromolecules, submitted for publication. Unpublished work
copyright 2005 American Chemical Society



                                                 89
5.1    SUMMARY

       Crosslinking is introduced to disrupt the crystallization of ethylene oxide units. A
series of crosslinked polymers, XLPEGDA, were prepared by photopolymerizing
poly(ethylene glycol) diacrylate (PEGDA) in the presence of varying amounts of water or
mono-functional poly(ethylene glycol) methyl ether acrylate (PEGMEA) to vary
crosslink density. All of the polymers had essentially the same chemical composition but
displayed a systematic variation in crosslink density as estimated from water swelling
experiments, models such as the Flory-Rehner and modified Flory-Rehner theories, and
dynamic mechanical testing.      Crosslink density decreased with increasing water or
PEGMEA content in the prepolymer solutions. Interestingly, gas solubility, diffusivity
and permeability were essentially independent of crosslink density for the series of
materials prepared from PEGDA and water.           The polymer density, fractional free
volume, glass transition temperature and polymer d-spacing were also constant when
water was used to vary crosslink density. Based on this result, it appears that crosslink
density does not necessarily affect gas diffusion and permeation, polymer glass transition
temperature or fractional free volume in network polymers, and ascribing changes in
these properties to changes in crosslink density alone, as is commonly seen in the
literature, should be done with great care.




                                              90
5.2    INTRODUCTION

       Polymer modification by crosslinking has recently attracted interest in membrane-
based separations of gases or vapors.       Crosslinked poly(dimethylsiloxane) is used
commercially as a vapor separation membrane material [5]. Crosslinking may enhance
thermal and chemical resistance; this strategy has been explored to prepare pervaporation
membranes that operate at elevated temperatures and high solvent activity [130].
Crosslinking can improve CO2/CH4 separation stability in glassy polymers, such as
polyimides, by suppressing the plasticization of the polymer matrix by CO2 or
hydrocarbons [131]. Poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO) is an interesting membrane platform for
the removal of CO2 from mixtures with light gases, such as H2, N2 and CH4 [25,132].
Because CO2 has an affinity for the polar ether linkages in PEO, this material exhibits
high solubility and permeability selectivity for gas pairs involving CO2 or related acid or
polar gases (e.g., H2S) [132]. However, the high crystallinity of PEO (>70 volume
percent) severely reduces gas permeability [25]. For example, semi-crystalline PEO
exhibits a CO2 permeability of only 12 Barrers at 35oC and infinite dilution, whereas the
estimated CO2 permeability of wholly amorphous PEO would be 140 Barrers [25].
Therefore, if one could prepare membranes from amorphous PEO, CO2 flux could be
improved by at least one order of magnitude while maintaining permeability selectivity.
According to Graham, crosslinked PEO is amorphous when the molecular weight of
poly(ethylene oxide) segments between two crosslinking points is 1500 g/mole or less,
while linear PEO with a molecular weight of 600 g/mole or above is highly crystalline at
ambient conditions [94].     We have prepared amorphous crosslinked PEO samples
exhibiting CO2 permeability coefficients of 570 Barrers at 35oC and infinite dilution [95].
Thus, one fundamental issue is to understand the effect of crosslinking on gas transport



                                            91
properties. This chapter describes an investigation of the effect of crosslinking on gas
permeation and related physical properties in polymers comprised primarily of PEO
(about 82 wt.%).
       To study the effect of crosslinking on polymer properties, the first challenge is to
prepare a series of polymers with systematic variation in crosslink density without
changing other properties, such as chemical composition [133]. Some early studies in
this area used natural rubber, and crosslink density was varied by changing sulfur
[134,135] or peroxide [135] content used to vulcanize the polymer. For example, Barrer
and Skirrow studied gas permeation and diffusion in natural rubber of various crosslink
densities by systematically varying the sulfur content used for vulcanization [134,136].
Gas solubility was relatively independent of crosslink density [134]; however, gas
permeability decreased as crosslink density increased, and the decrease was more
pronounced for larger penetrants [136]. For example, when the sulfur content in natural
rubber increased from 1.7 to 15.15 wt.%, CH4 permeability at 40oC decreased by one
order of magnitude, from 17.3 to 1.13 Barrers. In contrast, CH4 solubility at 60oC
changed only slightly, from 0.238 to 0.204 cm3(STP)/(cm3 atm), over the same range of
sulfur content [136]. However, introducing polar sulfur groups during the crosslinking
inevitably changes the chemical composition of the natural rubber; this factor alone could
increase glass transition temperature (Tg) and decrease gas permeability [66].         For
example, as sulfur content increased from zero to 16 wt%, Tg increased from about -80oC
to about +15oC [66]. Therefore, it is difficult, if not impossible, to decouple these two
factors to clearly understand the effect of crosslinking in such systems [66,137]. A
similar system is poly(propylene glycol) crosslinked at the terminal hydroxyl groups with
triisocyanate [138]. Decreases in hydrogen and carbon monoxide permeability were




                                           92
observed with increasing crosslink density achieved by increasing the content of rigid
urethane-type units in these polymers [138].
       In other studies, high energy radiation has been used to crosslink semi-crystalline
polyethylene. Gas diffusion coefficients decreased as radiation dose increased above a
certain threshold [139]. However, radiation not only crosslinks the polymer, but it also
affects the polymer in other ways; it can oxidize the polymer if it is radiated in the
presence of O2, crystallinity can decrease, and unsaturation and chain scission may
increase [139-141]. All of these factors, along with crosslinking, contribute to changes in
the gas transport properties. For example, oxidation would introduce polar groups into
the polymer, which could have a significant effect on gas diffusivity.

Table 5.1    Structures of relevant monomer and crosslinker

Chemical                         Structure

                                             O                      O
tetra(ethylene glycol)            H2C   C    C O   CH2    CH2   O C      C   CH2
dimethacrylate (TEGDM)
                                        CH3                     4
                                                                         CH3

                                               O
ethylacrylate (EA)
                                 H2C    CH C O CH2 CH3



       In addition to the studies mentioned above, Barrer and coworkers prepared a
series of copolymers from mixtures of tetra(ethylene glycol) dimethacrylate (TEGDM)
and ethylacrylate (EA) and studied their permeability [142]. The chemical structures of
TEGDM and EA are presented in Table 5.1.            Crosslink density was controlled by
changing TEGDM content, i.e., crosslinker concentration [142]. However, changing the
TEGDM content also changes the polymer chemical composition. More importantly,



                                             93
increasing EA content increases the concentration of chain ends (-OC2H5), which would
typically increase the polymer fractional free volume and, in turn, gas permeability
[143,144].
       Some researchers have even observed increases in gas permeability with
increasing crosslink density [131,145-147]. For example, Siegel and Coughlin reported
that gas permeability in semi-crystalline polyethylene increases as crosslink density
increases, probably due to the decrease in crystallinity that accompanies crosslinking
[145]. Wind and coworkers employed ethylene glycol to crosslink polyimides containing
carboxylic acid groups and reported an increase in CO2 permeability from 30 to 40
Barrers at 35oC and an upstream pressure of 4.4 atm [131].
       To summarize, the effect of crosslinking has not been clearly determined,
although a majority of the literature reports suggest that crosslinking reduces gas
permeability.   However, from a simple point of view, if gas diffusion requires the
collective movements of only a few repeat units in a polymer chain, why would one
crosslink junction per 20 (or more) repeat units significantly affect gas permeation? One
crosslink junction per 20 repeat units is mentioned because it is within the range of values
in the TEGDA/EA copolymers prepared by Barrer and coworkers [142] and the polymers
considered in the current study.
       In this work, a series of crosslinked poly(ethylene oxide) samples have been
prepared by ultraviolet (UV) photopolymerization of poly(ethylene glycol) diacrylate
(PEGDA, c.f. Table 3.1) in aqueous solution.         Figure 5.1 presents a schematic of
representative network structures in crosslinked PEGDA. By adjusting the water content
in the prepolymer solutions, crosslinked poly(ethylene oxide) (XLPEGDA-XX) samples,
where “XX” represents the weight percent of PEGDA in the prepolymer solution, can be
prepared with different crosslink densities. In general, increasing crosslink density as

                     R           R          R

Figure 5.1   Schematic of representative network elements in XLPEGDA. PEGDA:
             CH2=CH—R—CH=CH2. (a) represents the smallest network possible in
             these polymers; (b) represents a wasted crosslink or loop; (c) represents an
             entanglement, which is permanent due to the crosslinks.


crosslinker concentration in prepolymer solutions increases has been very well
documented for polymer systems both theoretically [148-150] and experimentally
[151,152]. In XLPEGDA, only a small amount of initiator (i.e., 0.001 g initiator per g
PEGDA) was used to minimize the influence of initiator fragments on the chemistry of
the polymers. With complete reaction of the vinyl groups in PEGDA, these amorphous
polymers have the same chemical composition, independent of the crosslink density.
Therefore, they are ideal models to isolate the effect of crosslinking on gas transport
properties. In addition, a separate series of copolymers (PEGDA/PEGMEA-XX) were
prepared using PEGDA and poly(ethylene glycol) methyl ether acrylate (PEGMEA).
Both PEGDA and PEGMEA contain about 82 wt.% PEO; therefore, there is essentially
no variation in chemical composition across this series of copolymers. By systematically
varying PEGMEA content, one can control the crosslink density and the concentration of



                                           95
chain end groups in the copolymers. The effect of these changes on gas permeation
properties is investigated.

5.3     DETERMINATION OF CROSSLINK DENSITY

        Equilibrium water swelling experiments at room temperature were used to
estimate crosslink density [100,101,153]. After being immersed in ultrapure water for
more than 5 days, the sample was rapidly wiped with a paper towel to remove surface
water, and the sample weight, wT,1, was measured. The film was then dried in a fume
hood at ambient conditions for 2 days, and then in a vacuum oven at room temperature
for another 2 days. The weight of dry polymer film, wP,1, was measured. The dry film
was immersed in ultrapure water again, and the procedure described above was repeated
to obtain wT,2 and wP,2, which are the weights of the swollen film and dry film,
respectively, in the second swelling experiment.
        Based on these measurements and a suitable model, the following parameters
could be calculated: polymer volume fraction after polymerization and before swelling
(the so-called relaxed state polymer volume fraction), v1,r; polymer volume fraction at
equilibrium swelling, v1,s; number average molecular weight between crosslinks, Mc; and
crosslink density, ve.
        Generally, a solvent sorbed in a polymer network exhibits its pure liquid density
[154]. If volume additivity is assumed, v1,r and v1,s can be calculated as follows:
                                              wp ρ p
                         v1,r =                                                       (5.1)
                                   (w
                                    T ,r   − wp ) ρ2 + wp ρ p

                                              wp ρ p
                         v1, s =                                                      (5.2)
                                   (w
                                    T ,s   − wp ) ρ2 + wp ρ p




                                                    96
where ρ2 is the dry polymer density, ρ2 is the density of liquid water at room temperature,
wp is the weight of dry polymer, and wT,r and wT,s represent the total weight of the
polymer and solvent in the relaxed and equilibrium swollen states, respectively.
       An affine deformation theory was used to estimate number average molecular
weight between crosslink junctions, Mc [100,153,155]:
                                        1
                                      ρ pV2
                                             [
                                            ln (1 − v1, s ) + v1, s + χv12, s   ]
                       1    2
                          =       −                                                          (5.3)
                       M c M n, p               v 1 3 1  v 
                                         v1, r  1, s  −  1, s 
                                                v1, r    2  v1, r 
                                                                      

where Mn,p is the number average molecular weight of the primary polymer chains before
crosslinking, V2 is the molar volume of water (18 cm3/mole) and χ is the Flory-Huggins

interaction parameter between the polymer and water. This equation is a modification of
the Flory-Rehner equation that considers the polymer system just after polymerization to
be in the relaxed state, where no elastic force is present [156]. By setting v1,r to 1, the
Flory-Rehner equation is recovered. To estimate Mn,p values in the polymers under
investigation, polymerization is assumed to follow two hypothetical steps: the first step is
to form linear polymer chains with an average molecular weight of Mn,p and the second
step is to form crosslinks among these chains [101,157]. In general, acrylate groups have
high conversion and the hypothetical Mn,p values would be large enough so that 2/Mn,p
can be neglected in eq 5.3 [101,157]. This assumption is used here. Eq 5.3 also assumes
a Gaussian chain distribution, i.e., the average number of repeat units between crosslinks
is presumed to be large enough that crosslinking junctions do not perturb the chain
configuration [158]. For highly crosslinked polymers such as those described here, the
number of repeat units between crosslinks could be less than 100.                   Therefore, the
assumption of a Gaussian chain distribution might not be valid [153]. Peppas and



                                                 97
coworkers developed the following equation to account for deviations from a Gaussian
distribution [153]:
                                                                                                         3

                                              1, s

where N is the number of links per chain (between two effective crosslink junctions) and
is defined as [153]:
                              λM c

where Mn is the molecular weight of the initial monomer or crosslinker (which is PEGDA
in this study) and λ is the number of links per PEGDA after polymerization, which is 4
since every vinyl group has 2 links after polymerization [153]. By combining eqs 5.4 and
5.5, Mc can be calculated if the Flory-Huggins interaction parameter between the polymer
and water is known.
       The effective number of chains in the networks per unit volume, or the effective
number of network junctions per unit volume, ve (moles/cm3), is given by [158]:
                              ρp

                              Mc

       It would be interesting to compare the value of ve to the number of theoretical
network junctions, which is defined as the number of chains when PEGDA completely
reacts and forms a perfect network without dangling ends [158,159]. The theoretical
number of network junctions, vt (moles/cm3), is given by [101,158-160]:

                       vt = 2c                                                                               (5.7)



                                                    98
where c (moles/cm3) is the concentration of crosslinking agent (i.e., PEGDA) in the dry
polymer, and the constant, 2, indicates that every PEGDA forms two junctions. Based
upon eqs 5.6 and 5.7, the theoretical molecular weight between crosslinks, M c ,ideal , is

[101,158]:
                                      Mn
                       M c ,ideal =                                                  (5.8)
                                      2

        In addition to solvent swelling experiments, crosslink density can also be
estimated from mechanical properties, such as elastic modulus measured using dynamic
mechanical testing. The following equation is often used [161]:
                            E'
                      ve =                                                           (5.9)
                           3RT

where E' is the elastic modulus in the rubbery plateau, R is the gas constant, and T is the
temperature at which E' is evaluated (room temperature in our case). The value of ve
from this model typically overestimates the effective crosslink density since this model
accounts for the effect of chain entanglements, in addition to crosslinking [162].

5.4     RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

5.4.1   PEGDA Molecular Weight Characterization

        Based on information from the supplier, the PEGDA sample used in these studies
has an average molecular weight between 699 and 743 g/mole. Since the molecular
weight and its distribution will inevitably affect the polymer network structure, it was
important to confirm the molecular weight and obtain the polydispersity index. Figure
5.2 presents the 1H NMR spectrum of PEGDA. The average number of ethylene oxide
repeating units is 14, and the number average molecular weight is 743 g/mole, which is
consistent with the specification provided by the supplier. Figure 5.3 presents a FAB-MS
spectrum, where signals corresponding to noise have been neglected. Using eq 3.2, the

                                                                                     n=14
                             Relative Abundance




                                                  567      611       655       699   743       787       831     875   919   963   1007

Figure 5.3           Fast atom bombardment mass spectrum in the range m/z 500-1100 for

                                                                                             100
number average molecular weight is 730 g/mole, and the polydispersity index is 1.08.
The Mn values estimated by NMR and FAB-MS are similar, and the polydispersity value
suggests a narrow distribution of molecular weight. This result is consistent with the
typical process for making poly(ethylene oxide)-based materials by ring opening
polymerization of ethylene oxide;      usually performed under living polymerization
conditions, the method yields materials with low values of polydispersity [113].

5.4.2   Polymerization Conditions

        Polymerization of acrylate monomers under UV light, i.e., UV radiation curing, is
a well-established technology in industry [163]. Acrylate double bonds often exhibit
very high reactivity, mainly because of large propagation rate constants and relatively
low termination rate constants [163]. Both acrylate groups of PEGDA are expected to
have similar reactivity [158], with the reaction of more than 90% of the acrylate groups
occurring within a few seconds under typical polymerization conditions [163]. In this
study, prepolymer solutions were exposed to UV for 90 seconds to ensure full reaction of
the acrylate groups. The effect of time on polymerization of these materials is reported
separately [164].
        Typical initiators for acrylate monomers are aromatic ketones [163]. Since water
was used as the solvent, two initiators were used: water insoluble HCPK and water
soluble Irgacure 2959. Both initiators undergo an α-cleavage process upon exposure to
UV radiation, forming a benzoyl (for HCPK) or substituted benzoyl (for Irgacure 2959)
radical and a counter radical [165]. Both radicals formed in each case are very active for
initiating polymerization [165]. Consequently, both initiators lead to high reaction rates
and a high degree of conversion during polymerization [165]. The amount of initiator
used is critical. Introducing a high concentration of initiator in the curing solution
produces a polymer with a non-uniform structure across its depth because of too much


                                           101
UV absorption on the top surface layer, which is closer to the UV source [163]. In this
study, the initiator amount was 0.1% relative to the weight of PEGDA. Mercury lamps,
with an average wavelength of 312 nm, were used because the initiators have high
absorption in this range [166].
       In general, crosslinked polymers contain an infinite network (gel) and a sol
fraction which is not attached to the network. The gel fraction typically approaches 1 as
the extent of reaction of the monomer reaches 100% [158]. In our studies, the weight of
PEGDA added for polymerization was always very close to the weight of dried polymer
after washing, suggesting that very little sol was formed. For example, for a polymer
prepared from 35 wt.% PEGDA and 65 wt.% H2O, the weight of the dry polymer sample,
after repeated extraction with water to remove the sol as described earlier, was 98% of
the weight of the PEGDA added. FTIR-ATR was used to probe the amount of un-reacted
acrylate groups in the dry polymer samples. Figure 5.4 compares a typical spectrum of a
polymer prepared from 20% PEGDA and 80% H2O (XLPEGDA-20) with that of liquid
PEGDA. The spectra for other polymers are very similar to that of XLPEGDA-20 and
are not shown for brevity. All of the characteristic peaks for the acrylate groups (i.e.,
those at 812, 1190 and 1410 cm-1) [102-104] practically disappear in the IR spectrum of
the polymer, which indicates that the reaction conversion of PEGDA is close to 100%.
Additionally, the spectra taken from the two sides of the polymer sample are shown to be
almost identical, so there is no measurable effect of film depth on polymerization for the
range of thicknesses considered in this study. As a result, film thickness should not play
an important role in other physical properties, such as water uptake and gas permeation.
Additionally, this result confirms that sufficient initiator was used to perform the
polymerization.




                                           102
                                            C=C-C                          C=C




                                                 o=
                                PEGDA



                Absorbance

                                XLPEGDA

Figure 5.4   FTIR spectra of liquid PEGDA and each side of a solid XLPEGDA-20 film.
             For the solid polymer film, one side was directly exposed to UV light and
             the other side was positioned opposite to the UV source. This film has a
             thickness of about 500 µm. These spectra have been displaced vertically for
             clarity.

        The polymers prepared should have similar theoretical crosslink density and
chemical composition. However, higher contents of diluent during polymerization would
increase intramolecular crosslink-forming loops, which do not contribute to the elastic
force of the network; these structures are called wasted crosslinks, as illustrated in Figure
5.1 [158]. In this way, the effective crosslink density in this series of polymers can be
manipulated by varying the amount of water content in the prepolymer solution.

5.4.3   DSC and WAXD Results

        Figures 5.5a and 5.5b present wide angle X-ray diffraction patterns for semi-
crystalline poly(ethylene oxide) and a representative XLPEGDA sample, XLPEGDA-50.
The diffraction patterns of other XLPEGDA samples are very similar to that of
XLPEGDA-50, except for a slight shift in the position of the amorphous halo. The PEO
WAXD pattern exhibits sharp characteristic peaks for PEO crystals at 2θ values around


                                                             103
17-19o and 22-24o, which is consistent with previous reports [105]. However, those
peaks are absent in the XLPEGDA pattern, which clearly indicates the amorphous nature
of XLPEGDA samples at room temperature.                                                                    Because of short range order, these
crosslinked polymers exhibit a broad halo. The d-spacing, which corresponds to the
position of the diffraction maximum, can be calculated based on eq 3.4. The results are
summarized in Table 5.2, and there is no significant effect of prepolymer composition
(i.e., effective crosslink density) on d-spacing.                                                           d-spacing has been widely used to
characterize the spacing between polymer chains, and it has been related to gas
permeability [167].

these rubbery polymers. The calorimetric Tg values for all of the polymers fall within a
very narrow range, which suggests that Tg is not dependent on the effective crosslink
density; a similar result is obtained for the glass transition temperatures determined via
dynamic mechanical testing (Tα, see Table 5.2). The Tg values will be discussed further
after calculations of effective crosslink density are presented.

Figure 5.6   First scan differential scanning calorimetry thermograms of XLPEGDAs.
             All polymers were prepared using HCPK as the initiator, except
             XLPEGDA-80*, which was prepared using Irgacure 2959 as the initiator.
             Additionally, the inhibitor was removed and N2 was bubbled through the
             prepolymer solution of XLPEGDA-80* to determine the influence of these
