                                                                    106
        Generally, high molecular weight PEO is highly crystalline [113]. However, the
crosslinked polymers do not crystallize in the temperature range of the DSC experiments,
which was between -90 and 30oC. Since crystallization does not occur at temperatures
below Tg, it can be concluded that PEO crystals are not present in the XLPEGDAs. A
unit cell of a PEO crystal contains four PEO chains with a 7/2 helix structure, i.e., each
chain in the unit cell has 7 repeating units that undergo 2 turns in the unit cell [115]. In
XLPEGDA there is, on average, only 7 ethylene oxide units per acrylate group, so the
acrylate linkages appear to behave as defects regularly introduced into the PEO backbone
of XLPEGDA. These defects apparently disrupt the efficient packing of 7 consecutive
ethylene oxide units as needed to form a crystal unit cell. Additionally, crosslinking may
reduce the possibility that 4 PEO chains could be co-located with sufficient
conformational freedom to arrange perfectly into a unit cell.          Thus, PEO crystal
formation is suppressed.     For comparison, the unreacted crosslinker, PEGDA, does
crystallize; it has a melting temperature between 12 and 17oC, according to Aldrich.

5.4.4   Crosslink Density

        Water swelling results are presented in Table 5.2. The dry sample weight after
the second cycle of soaking in water was essentially the same as the dry weight after the
first equilibration with water, suggesting no significant sol fraction removal during these
steps (if, in fact, there was any sol present in the materials following polymerization and
extraction). Except for XLPEGDA-20, the water uptake was the same in both swelling
experiments. For XLPEGDA-20, the film swollen just after the polymerization appears
opaque, and contains about 80 wt.% (or 82 vol.%) water. However, in the second
swelling experiment, the film becomes transparent, and the water content of the film is
only 71 vol.% [164]. This phenomenon has been observed in other systems, and it is
ascribed to phase separation during crosslinking because of excess diluent and/or


                                            107
excessive crosslinking [168,169]. In such samples, a macroporous structure is formed
during polymerization, which leads to the opaque character and high water sorption in
initial water uptake measurements [164,169].      However, this structure in a rubbery
polymer collapses during the first drying, and the material becomes a nonporous film that
sorbs less water upon subsequent soaking [169]. In all cases, the water uptake after the
second hydration step was used to calculate crosslink density. In some glassy polymers,
the formation of such a macroporous structure can be sustained during drying [169].
However, this effect has not been reported in rubbery polymers.
       The calculation of crosslink density is very sensitive to the value of the Flory-
Huggins interaction parameter (i.e., χ), which varies with temperature and polymer
concentration [170]. In previous studies of poly(ethylene oxide) crosslinked either by
acrylate groups [100,171], peroxide [172], γ radiation [173-175] or other methods [176],
the χ value was taken to be the same as that of linear poly(ethylene oxide). Two χ values
are used in the literature. The first value, 0.426, was determined by osmotic pressure
measurements and found to be nearly constant for polymer volume fractions from 0.04 to
0.20 [175]. The second χ value, 0.45, was measured by light scattering for a dilute
solution of PEO in water [177]. However, these values might not be appropriate for the
polymers prepared in this study since they have polymer volume fractions of 0.30-0.60 at
equilibrium with water. The corresponding χ value would need to be obtained from
linear PEO/water solutions at similar volume fractions.        Furthermore, XLPEGDA
contains about 18 wt% ethyl-ester linkages which might affect χ values. Another method
for χ determination is based on the use of the stress-strain relationship to estimate
crosslink density, and then combining this result with water swelling studies to determine
χ [104,178]. The χ values obtained in this manner for the PEO/water system are about
0.50 at 25oC for polymer volume fractions between 0 and 0.2 [104,178]. The underlying


                                           108
assumption in this approach is that crosslink density calculated from mechanical
properties and solvent swelling should be consistent.        However, the inconsistency
between these two approaches has been well documented, possibly because polymer
chain disentanglement occurs during mechanical testing, but not in solvent swelling
studies, according to some researchers [151,153].
       In a companion paper [164], χ values in XLPEGDA have been carefully
determined as a function of water content using water vapor sorption experiments. Here,
we propose a simple method to estimate the χ values between XLPEGDAs and water.
Flory noted that the relaxed state for a polymer network should be that just after
polymerization from the prepolymer solution [156]. Therefore, XLPEGDA-20 would be
in the relaxed state at a water content of 80 wt.% (or 82 vol.%) if there were no phase
separation. Since the maximum water uptake of nonporous XLPEGDA-20 is only 71
vol.% in the second swelling experiment (i.e., at thermodynamic equilibrium), it should
be reasonable to assume that there is no elastic force present in this film when it is in
equilibrium with water (i.e., after being dried and re-swollen in water). Thus, eqs 5.3 and
5.4 reduce to the Flory-Huggins model for liquid water swelling (i.e., a = 1) in this case,
as shown in eq 2.9. The χ value obtained by this analysis is 0.626±0.004, which is
somewhat higher than that discussed above for linear PEO. One possible reason for the
difference could be the higher polymer content in XLPEGDA-20 (0.29 volume fraction),
compared with polymer volume fraction of 0.20 or less when χ values of 0.426 or 0.45
are reported. Also, XLPEGDA contains hydrophobic ethyl ester linkages which could
reasonably act to increase the χ value for water in these samples relative to that in linear
PEO.
       In a study of water vapor sorption in XLPEGDA-20, at a water vapor activity
(i.e., a) of 0.94, the polymer volume fraction is 0.57 [164]. Due to the lack of elastic


                                            109
force in this polymer at the swollen state, the Flory-Huggins model, eq 2.9, can be used to
estimate the χ value of the polymer and H2O at v1,s of 0.57. The χ value obtained is
0.652±0.004. An empirical equation could be used to correlate χ with polymer volume
fraction [101,157,170]:
                       χ = χ 0 + χ 1v1, s                                           (5.10)

where χ 0 and χ1 are adjustable constants. Since we have two χ values of XLPEGDA-20
and H2O at two v1,s (i.e., 0.29 and 0.57), the values of χ 0 and χ1 can be calculated and

are 0.599±0.009 and 0.0926±0.020, respectively. Since all of the polymers have very
similar chemical composition, the χ values of the polymers and H2O could be reasonably
regarded as very similar. Therefore, χ values for the other polymers can be calculated
using eq 5.10; they are reported in Table 5.2, which also presents the effective crosslink
density calculated from eqs 5.3 and 5.4. Additionally, crosslink density is estimated from
dynamic mechanical testing based on eq 5.9. All of these models suggest that decreasing
crosslinker concentration in the prepolymer solutions decreases effective crosslink
density or increases the molecular weight between crosslinks (Mc). For example, as
water content in the prepolymer solution increases from 0 to 50 wt.%, the effective
crosslink density decreases by about 50%. The Mc value of XLPEGDA-20 could not be
calculated using eq 5.3 or 5.4, as discussed above. However, dynamic mechanical testing
shows that XLPEGDA-20 has the highest Mc value among the samples considered. It
seems that eq 5.4 might be more suitable for this system. The ideal Mc is 370 g/mole
according to eq 5.8. However, Mc values from the modified Flory-Rehner theory (eq 5.3)
are only 125 g/mole, which corresponds to less than 3 ethylene oxide units. Instead, the
Mc values (e.g., 502 g/mole) given by eq 5.4 appear to be more realistic. On the other
hand, it should be noted that the formation of permanent entanglement in the polymers
could reduce the Mc values.


                                            110
        The trend of decreasing ve with decreasing crosslinker content in the prepolymer
solution has been well documented in other crosslinked polymer systems, such as
2-hydroxyethyl methacrylate polymerized with ethylene glycol dimethacrylate [101],
ethylene glycol monomethacrylate polymerized with ethylene glycol dimethacrylate
[159], acrylamide polymerized with N,N’-methylenebis(acrylamide) [151,152], and
poly(acrylic acid) with poly(ethylene glycol) diacrylate [149].          For example, in
copolymers of ethylene glycol monomethacrylate (EGMM) with ethylene glycol
dimethacrylate (EGDM) at an EGDM concentration of 0.11×10-4 mole/(cm3 polymer), as
water content in the prepolymer solution increases from 20 vol.% to 65 vol.%, the water
uptake in the copolymer increases from 46 vol.% to 61 vol.%, and the effective crosslink
density measured from mechanical properties decreases from 0.91×10-3 mole/cm3 to
0.24×10-3   mole/cm3 [159].     Such behavior is usually explained by the increase in
cyclization (i.e., wasted crosslinks) during gel formation at low crosslinker concentration
in the prepolymer solution [152]. Therefore, even though the diluent content does not
affect the crosslinker reaction conversion, the increase in ineffective crosslinks at higher
diluent content is significant enough to change effective crosslink density.

5.4.5   Density and Glass Transition Temperature

        Density can be used to estimate fractional free volume (cf., eq 2.14). The FFV
values in Table 5.2 are very similar for all polymers due to the similar density and
chemical composition. So, varying crosslink density does not change fractional free
volume in these materials.
        Interestingly, all of the XLPEGDAs exhibit similar glass transition temperatures
from both DSC and dynamic mechanical measurements indicating that Tg is independent
of the effective crosslink density.     It is customary to relate Tg to free volume at
temperatures above Tg by the following equation [132]:


                                            111
                       FFV = FFV (Tg ) + α r (T − Tg )                               (5.11)

where FFV and FFV(Tg) are the fractional free volumes of the polymer at temperatures T
and Tg, respectively, and αr is thermal expansion coefficient for the polymer in the
rubbery state.   This approach has been employed to explain the change of Tg in
crosslinked natural rubbers, for example [179]. By assuming that FFV(Tg) is independent
of the crosslink density and αr is constant, the experimental values of free volume
measured for these polymers can be related to the observed (invariant) values of Tg.
Thus, from the standpoint of free volume, Tg in these polymers would not be expected to
change, since the measured fractional free volume remains essentially the same.

5.4.6   Gas Transport Properties

        Figure 5.7 presents permeability coefficients of four gases (i.e., N2, CH4, H2 and
CO2) in XLPEGDA-50 at 35oC as a function of upstream pressure. The dependence of
permeability on pressure follows typical behaviors of rubbery polymers [26]. For light
gases such as N2, H2 and CH4, permeability remains almost the same as pressure varies;
for more condensable penetrants such as CO2, permeability increases as pressure
increases. Other XLPEGDAs exhibited similar behavior; however, they are not shown
for brevity.
        To compare the inherent permeability of polymers to various gases, infinite
dilution permeability can be estimated using eq 4.4 and the results are recorded in Table
5.3. There is no significant trend in gas permeability with effective crosslink density of
the polymer networks. N2 permeability remains almost the same and the permeability of
the other penetrants increase slightly with decreasing crosslink density. The selectivity of
other gases relative to N2 is calculated and recorded in Table 5.3.         It appears that
selectivity increases slightly with increasing crosslink density.

       Gas permeability of these amorphous polymers are compared with those of semi-
crystalline poly(ethylene oxide) (S-C PEO) in Table 5.3. In general, S-C PEO exhibits
permeability coefficients that are about one order of magnitude lower than that of the
XLPEGDAs because of the highly crystalline nature of S-C PEO [25]. If one applies
standard models to describe the effect of crystallinity on gas permeability, then the data
for S-C PEO can be used to estimate the gas permeability in the amorphous regions of the
semi-crystalline polymer. These permeability values are reported as those corresponding
to Am PEO in Table 5.3. Interestingly, the permeability of Am PEO is very close to that
of the XLPEGDA samples. It is also noteworthy that permeability selectivity of other
gases over N2 is quite similar in the XLPEGDAs and semi-crystalline PEO. CO2/N2
selectivity has been extensively characterized in various PEO-containing materials, such
as block copolymers of polyamide-b-PEO (PEBAX®) [65] and crosslinked polymers

                                                               114
prepared from poly(ethylene glycol) methacrylates [6]. These PEO-containing materials
exhibit CO2/N2 selectivity values in the range of 50-70 at 35oC [132], which are
consistent with the CO2/N2 selectivity values for the XLPEGDAs, as illustrated in Table
5.3.   Gas permeability in XLPEGDAs is also compared with that in a crosslinked
polymer    (XLPEGDMA)         prepared    using   poly(ethylene    glycol)   dimethacrylate
(PEGDMA, n=14).         This polymer was prepared in a procedure similar to the
XLPEGDAs, and no solvent was used. The PEGDMA molecular weight was about 750
g/mole according to the manufacturer and confirmed using NMR and FAB-MS. In
general, XLPEGDMA exhibits slightly lower gas permeability than the XLPEGDAs.
       Considering the remarkable difference in water uptake among these polymers, it
is interesting to observe so little change in gas permeability. To further understand the
gas transport properties, CH4 and CO2 sorption experiments were performed. Figure 5.8
presents the sorption isotherms of CO2 and CH4 in XLPEGDA-50 at 35oC. For typical
rubbery polymers, these isotherms would be linear for low sorbing gases such as CH4,
and exhibit mild positive curvature for highly sorbing gases such as CO2. However, both
isotherms are well described by straight lines (i.e., Henry’s law) over the pressure range
considered in this study, such that the gas solubility is given by the slope of the line that
best describes the data and passes through the origin. The same behavior is observed for
the other polymers in this series, and the results are presented in Table 5.4. All of the
polymers exhibit similar solubility values for CO2 and CH4, in contrast to the significant
variation in water solubility in these polymers (cf. Table 5.2). Therefore, crosslink
density or elastic force does not play a role when the penetrant is sparingly soluble in the
polymer. In this regard, crosslinking should not be important even for gas sorption at
high pressure since the volume fraction of sorbed penetrant in these polymers is always

to be independent of crosslink density [6,142,180].
       Diffusion coefficients of CO2 and CH4 at infinite dilution were calculated based
on eq 2.5, and they are recorded in Table 5.4. It appears that the difference in crosslink
density among these polymers does not have a significant impact on gas diffusion
coefficients. This conclusion is quite different from previous studies [6,142], though the
results are not necessarily in conflict. For example, one study regarding the effect of
crosslink density on gas diffusion is based on copolymers prepared from tetra(ethylene
glycol) dimethacrylate (TEGDM) and ethylacrylate (EA) [142]. Table 5.5 presents some

                                                             118
copolymers. As EA content increased from 40% to 80%, CO2 solubility remained almost
unchanged but CO2 diffusivity increased by about 300% while              crosslink density
decreased by about 60%. Such changes in effective crosslink density are very similar to
the changes in the XLPEGDA polymers considered in this study. It would be tempting to
conclude that the increased CO2 diffusivity in copolymers of TEGDM and EA (TEGDM-
co-EA) is solely due to the decrease in crosslink density, which was stated in the
reference [142]. However, this may not be the case. The decrease in crosslink density in
TEGDM-co-EA is accompanied by a significant decrease in glass transition temperature
and density, which partly results from the copolymer effect, i.e., the addition of EA
increases the concentration of chain ends, which is well known to decrease Tg and
increase fractional free volume, regardless of whether or not the system is crosslinked
[95]. As such, the role of crosslink density on gas diffusion in TEGDM-co-EA is not
unambiguous and needs to be examined carefully.
       To assess the effect of chain end groups, a series of copolymers was prepared
from PEGDA and PEGMEA (i.e., PEGDA/PEGMEA) and compared with the
XLPEGDAs. These two series of polymers have very similar chemical composition,
with approximately 82 wt.% overall ethylene oxide content. Therefore, any difference in
the physical properties of these two series of polymers should not be due to differences in
chemical composition. As PEGMEA content in PEGDA/PEGMEA increases, crosslink
density decreases and methoxy chain end group concentration increases.           Dynamic
mechanical data for these copolymers (i.e., E' values at 25oC) were used to estimate
crosslink density based on eq 5.9 and the results are compared with those of the
XLPEGDAs in Figure 5.9a.         Interestingly, the dependence of crosslink density on
PEGDA content in the prepolymer solutions in both series of polymers seems to follow

the same trendline. In contrast, Figure 5.9b shows that CO2 permeability at 35oC and
infinite dilution in these two series of polymers exhibits completely different trends. In
PEGDA/PEGMEA, as PEGDA content decreases from 100% to 30 wt.%, CO2
permeability increases by more than 200%, from 110 to 320 Barrers. On the other hand,
in the XLPEGDAs, as PEGDA content in the prepolymer solution decreases from 100%
to 20%, CO2 permeability changes only from 110 to 140 Barrers. This distinct difference
clearly suggests that crosslink density does not play an important role in the gas
permeability of these materials. Instead, the concentration of methoxy chain end groups
is the determining factor influencing gas permeability. The presence of methoxy chain
end groups can increase polymer fractional free volume, decrease polymer glass
transition temperature and increase polymer permeability to gases; the detailed analysis
was presented elsewhere [95]. In general, the comparison of PEGDA/PEGMEA and the




                                                                        120
XLPEGDAs suggests that the effect of crosslink density per se on permeation properties
has perhaps been overestimated.       The widely referenced work on gas transport in
TEGDM-co-EA is an example, as discussed above [142].
         The apparent overestimation of the effect of crosslink density also exists in the
literature when evaluating changes in Tg [162,181]. For example, an empirical equation
has been generalized for copolymers prepared from divinyl benzene (mole fraction = n)
and styrene. The change in Tg with varying divinyl benzene content, ∆Tg, is described
assuming that all of the vinyl groups in divinyl benzene participate in the crosslinking
[181]:
                        ∆Tg = Tg (n) − Tg (n = 0) = 586n                               (5.12)

This equation successfully modeled the Tg change among the copolymers examined, and
the authors concluded that the change was solely due to variations in the crosslink density
[181]. However, an increase in divinyl benzene content would inevitably decrease the
concentration of chain end (i.e., methyl) groups from the vinyl monomers and would
result in an increase in Tg based upon free volume theory, as illustrated above. Eq 5.12
fails to discern the effect of crosslink density from other influential factors, such as chain
end group concentration, and the assumption that changes in Tg are due solely to
differences in crosslink density needs to be re-evaluated.

5.4.7    Effect of Initiator and O2 on Polymerization

         The effect of initiator and the presence of O2 during polymerization on the
physical properties of XLPEGDA polymers was investigated.              Two initiators were
compared, hydrophobic HCPK and hydrophilic Irgacure 2959. O2 is a well known
inhibitor for free radical polymerization [163].           In our preparation protocol, the
prepolymer liquid is sandwiched between two glass plates, which can prevent the ingress




                                             121
of O2 into the system during polymerization. However, there is always some dissolved
O2 in the prepolymer solutions since they are prepared at ambient conditions. One way to
remove O2 is to bubble N2 through the prepolymer solution [182]. Commercial PEGDA
also contains about 100 ppm monomethyl ether hydroquinone (MEHQ) [183], which is
an inhibitor to prevent PEGDA from polymerizing.           MEHQ can be removed using
inhibitor removers purchased from Aldrich. For this study, four films of XLPEGDA-80
were prepared: (1) 0.1 wt.% HCPK; (2) 0.1 wt.% HCPK, O2 and MEHQ removed; (3) 0.1
wt.% Irgacure 2959; (4) 0.1 wt.% Irgacure 2959, O2 and MEHQ removed. FTIR spectra
of all of these films are very similar, and suggest that the conversion of acrylate groups is
essentially complete in all cases. All four films exhibit nearly the same density and water
swelling ratio, resulting in similar estimated fractional free volume and effective
crosslink density values.    Additionally, gas transport properties and Tg values were
measured for Film 4 and compared with those for Film 1, as illustrated in Table 5.6. In
general, there is no significant difference in any measured property due to the difference
in initiators nor the presence of free radical inhibitors such as O2 and MEHQ.

5.5    CONCLUSIONS

       A series of crosslinked polymers was prepared using prepolymer solutions
containing poly(ethylene glycol) diacrylate (PEGDA) and H2O. By adjusting the water
content of the prepolymer solutions, we could systematically vary crosslink density in the
resulting polymers while maintaining the same chemical composition; crosslink density
decreased as water content in the prepolymer solutions increased.            FTIR showed
essentially total disappearance of vinyl groups during polymerization, indicating
complete reaction of the PEGDA. For the prepolymer solution containing 80 wt.% H2O,
phase separation was observed during polymerization.

    Gas permeability was measured at 35oC




                                                             123
       Gas solubility and permeability in these polymer networks were measured, and
diffusivity was calculated. Interestingly, these properties were essentially independent of
crosslink density, which is in contrast to the general notion that higher crosslink density
leads to lower gas diffusivity and permeability. However, such behavior is consistent
with the fact that many of the physical properties in these polymers, such as density,
fractional free volume, glass transition temperature and d-spacing, were independent of
crosslink density.   Based on this result, it appears that crosslink density does not
necessarily affect gas diffusion and permeation, polymer glass transition temperature or
fractional free volume in network polymers, and ascribing changes in these properties to
changes in crosslink density alone, as is commonly seen in the literature, should be done
with great care. That is, the changes in gas diffusion and permeability often observed
with crosslinked systems are more the result of Tg changes than crosslink density
changes.

       Gas and Vapor Solubility in Crosslinked Poly(ethylene glycol

                                                125
6.1    SUMMARY

       The solubilities of CO2 and several hydrocarbons (CH4, C2H4, C2H6, C3H6 and
C3H8) were determined as a function of gas fugacity (i.e., pressure) at temperatures
ranging from -20oC to 35oC in XLPEGDA-80. Sorption isotherms were described using
the Flory-Huggins model. Unlike sorption in nonpolar rubbery polymers, solubility of
gases such as CO2 and olefins in polar XLPEGDA-80 do not vary systematically based
on parameters conventionally used to characterize gas condensability such as critical
temperature (Tc) or (Tc/T)2, so a new model is proposed to correlate the data. CO2
exhibits the lowest Flory-Huggins interaction parameter (i.e., χ) values among all
penetrants considered, indicating favorable interactions with XLPEGDA-80. Although
gas solubility at a given pressure generally increases as temperature decreases, χ values
increase with decreasing temperature for all penetrants except CO2. These results reflect
the interplay between increases in penetrant condensability as temperature decreases,
which tend to increase solubility, and increases in the unfavorable interactions between
the gas and polymer segments (reflected in the increased χ values) as temperature
decreases, which tend to reduce solubility. χ values for CO2 in XLPEGDA-80 decrease
with decreasing temperature, which is another indication of the affinity between CO2 and
XLPEGDA-80. To illustrate the effect of temperature on intermolecular interactions,
CO2/C2H6 solubility selectivity at infinite dilution increases from 2.7 to 6.6 as
temperature decreases from 35oC to -20oC, even though these two penetrants have almost
the same condensability as characterized by Tc. Furthermore, gas solubility values in
XLPEGDA-80 are very similar to those in amorphous PEG at infinite dilution and 35oC,
suggesting that the PEG linkages in XLPEGDA-80 dominate its sorption properties.



                                          126
6.2    INTRODUCTION

       Poly(ethylene oxide) containing materials exhibit relatively high CO2/light gas
permeability selectivity due to solubility selectivity favoring CO2 in these gas pairs. The
favorable interactions between CO2 and EO units often become stronger as temperature
decreases. In Chapter 5, we have reported calorimetry data for XLPEGDA-80, which
was prepared from prepolymer solution containing 80 wt.% PEGDA in water; this
material contains about 82 wt.% EO and does not crystallize at temperatures as low as
-20oC [184].   This chapter discusses the effect of temperature and pressure on gas
solubilities of CO2 and several hydrocarbons (CH4, C2H6, C2H4, C3H8 and C3H6) in
XLPEGDA-80, and it compares solubility behavior in amorphous XLPEGDA-80 with
that in semi-crystalline PEO at 35oC.
       Gas solubility, SA, is given by [23,185,186]:
                              C
                       SA =                                                          (6.1)
                              f

where C is the concentration of gas dissolved in the polymer when the fugacity of the gas
phase in contact with the polymer is f. Instead of pressure, fugacity is used in this study
to appropriately account for gas phase nonidealities [186], which can become significant
at low temperature (e.g., -20oC) for compressible penetrants such as CO2. For example,
Figure 6.1 presents the ratio of pure gas fugacity to pressure for CH4, CO2 and C3H8 as a
function of pressure at 35oC and -20oC. The gas is ideal if f/p=1 and becomes more non-
ideal as f/p deviates further from unity. The equation used to calculate fugacity is
presented in the Appendix. As temperature decreases, the gas phase becomes more non-
ideal. Non-ideal behavior is more significant for more compressible gas such as CO2

6.3     RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

6.3.1   Characterization of Polymer Samples

        Table 6.2 provides physical properties of the XLPEGDA-80 films used in this
study. These results have been reported elsewhere, along with the measurement or
calculation method [184]. The polymer is amorphous at room temperature, and it does
not show any crystallization or melting peaks in the temperature range from -90 to 0oC as
characterized by differential scanning calorimetry (DSC) [184]. Therefore, over the

6.3.2    Gas Sorption, Solubility and Interaction Parameter

         Sorption isotherms for CH4, C2H4, C2H6, CO2, C3H6 and C3H8 in XLPEO are
presented as a function of temperature in Figures 6.2a-f.            The detailed values are
summarized in Table 6.3. For low sorbing penetrants, such as CH4 at all temperatures
studied and C2H6 at 35oC, the isotherms are linear. For highly sorbing penetrants, such as
CO2, C2H4, C3H6 and C3H8 at any temperature studied and C2H6 at temperatures lower
than 35oC, the isotherms are slightly convex to the fugacity axis.
         Figures 6.3a and 6.3b present CO2 and C2H4 solubility as a function of
temperature and fugacity, respectively. At higher temperatures, such as 298 K and 308
K, CO2 and C2H4 solubility coefficients are essentially independent of fugacity.
However, at lower temperatures, such as 253 K, their solubility increases with increasing
fugacity. For example, at 253 K, the CO2 solubility coefficient increases by 30%, from
5.6 to 7.3 cm3(STP)/(cm3 atm), as CO2 fugacity increases from 0.8 to 9.9 atm.

Figure 6.3                           Effect of penetrant fugacity (f) and temperature on gas solubility in
                                     XLPEGDA-80. (a) CO2; (b) C2H4


                          All of these isotherms can be described by the Flory-Huggins model (i.e., eq 6.2)
with appropriate values of the Flory-Huggins interaction parameter, χ. In general, χ
depends on penetrant volume fraction and temperature, as described by the following
empirical model [170,187]:

                                               χ = χ 0 + χ1 / T + χ 2 (1 − φ2 )                                                                            (6.3)




                                                                             134
where χ 0 , χ1 and χ 2 are adjustable constants. By adjusting these constants, the sorption

data in Table 6.3 can be described using eqs 6.2, 6.3 and 2.10, as illustrated by the solid
lines and curves in Figures 6.2a-f. To fit the data to this model, saturation pressures for
the penetrants, used to calculate fsat, are obtained from the literature [42], and penetrant
partial molar volumes in the polymer are the average values obtained in various
polymer/gas systems by Kamiya and coworkers [40]; these values are recorded in Table
6.1. V2 values are taken to be independent of the temperature. Additionally, the effect of

temperature and penetrant fugacity on the polymer sample volume was neglected, and
this assumption is evaluated as follows. Typically, the sorbed penetrant swells the
polymer sample, and, thus, increases the sample volume and decreases the volume of the
gas phase in the sorption experiments. The maximum penetrant volume fraction (i.e.,
φ2, max , which is obtained at the lowest temperature and highest fugacity) is less than

0.010, except for C2H4 at -20oC (which is 0.03) and CO2 at -20oC (which is 0.12). If such
an effect is included in analyzing the sorption data, the calculated sorption values are
very close to the values reported in Table 6.3, and the difference is typically less than
10% (which is a typical uncertainty value for gas solubility, c.f. Table 6.8), even for CO2
at φ2, max . Furthermore, this swelling effect could be partially offset by the decrease in the

polymer sample volume as temperature decreases. For example, the thermal expansion
coefficient of XLPEGDA-80 was estimated to be 8.4×10-4 K-1 [132] and, thus, as
temperature decreases from 25oC to -20oC, sample volume decreases by about 4%.
Therefore, to simplify the analysis, these effects are neglected, i.e., polymer sample
density is assumed to be constant in the temperature range studied, and the swelling
effect on sorption results is neglected. The detailed values of χ 0 , χ1 and χ 2 are

recorded in Table 6.4. The uncertainty in these parameters was estimated by a standard


                                             135
propagation of error analysis [59]. Since the CH4 isotherms are essentially linear, the
solubility and Flory-Huggins χ parameter of CH4 do not depend on penetrant volume
fraction. Figure 6.4 presents the effect of temperature and penetrant volume fraction on
χ in XLPEGDA-80. The points are experimental data calculated using eqs 6.2, 6.3 and
2.10, and the lines are based on eq 6.3 using the adjustable parameters, χ 0 , χ1 and χ 2 .

In general, the empirical equation, eq 6.3, satisfactorily describes the data, and χ is a
weak function of penetrant volume fraction in the polymer, φ2 .

Figure 6.4       Effect of temperature and penetrant volume fraction, φ2 , on
                 polymer/penetrant interaction parameters, χ , in XLPEGDA-80. T = 308 K
                 (▲), 298 K ( ∇ ), 273 K (●), 263 K (□) and 253 K (♦). The points are

            (χ 0 + χ 2 ) has been added to the original Flory-Huggins model in an empirical
fashion to account for the concentration dependence of χ and to account for non-
combinatorial contributions to the entropy of mixing; the value of (χ 0 + χ 2 ) in the
original theory is 0.[162] For C2H4, C2H6 and C3H6 in XLPEGDA-80, (χ 0 + χ 2 ) values

are near zero (c.f., Table 6.4). However, this is not true for CH4, CO2 and C3H8 in
XLPEGDA-80. For example, CH4 has a value of -3.8 for (χ 0 + χ 2 ) . For all penetrants

except CO2, χ1 values are positive, so increasing temperature decreases χ . This trend

has been observed in many systems, such as CO2 sorption in 1,2-polybutadiene,
poly(ethylene-co-vinyl acetate) and PDMS [40], and methoxyflurane sorption in PDMS
[188]. However, for CO2 in XLPEGDA-80, χ1 is negative and, therefore, the χ value



                                                          138
decreases as temperature decreases, which suggests that the interaction between CO2 and
XLPEGDA-80 becomes stronger and more favorable at lower temperatures.
        Historically, χ1 has been related to solubility parameters as follows [189]:
                               V2
                        χ1 =      (δ1 − δ 2 ) 2                                                 (6.4)
                               R

where δ1 and δ2 are the solubility parameters of XLPEGDA-80 and the penetrant of
interest, respectively. Since XLPEGDA-80 contains 82% PEO, δ1 is presumed to be
similar to that of pure PEO, i.e., 19.8 MPa0.5 [25]. Since the value of (χ 0 + χ 2 ) in the

original theory is 0, χ1 in eq 6.4 is often replaced by χT in the literature [38,125].

Using this approach and 19.8 MPa0.5 for the solubility parameter of XLPEGDA-80, the
solubility parameters of the penetrants were estimated at 25oC. The resulting solubility
parameters (i.e., δ2exp) are listed in Table 6.4 [42].                 Literature values of penetrant
solubility parameters, δ2lit, are also included in the table for comparison; these values are
calculated from gas solubility in liquids, where χ1 is assumed to be equal to χT [38].

Except for CO2, δ2exp values are near but slightly lower than δ2lit. This agreement is
considered satisfactory given the assumptions involved in making this comparison.

6.3.3   Solubility Correlations

        Infinite dilution solubility coefficients, S A∞ , were estimated using eqs 6.1-6.3 and

2.10 in the limit as gas fugacity approaches zero:

                        S A∞ = lim
                                f →0
                                       C 22414
                                       f
                                         =
                                           f satV2
                                                        (
                                                   exp − 1 − χ ∞   )                            (6.5)


where χ ∞ is the Flory-Huggins interaction parameter, χ , at infinite dilution and at the

temperature of interest.       Figure 6.5 presents penetrant infinite dilution solubility at




                                                  139
various temperatures.      S A∞ increases in the following order at most of temperatures

considered:
                         CH4 < C2H6 < C2H4 < C3H8 < CO2 < C3H6
Olefins (e.g., C2H4 and C3H6) are more soluble than their paraffin analogs, even though
olefins have lower critical temperatures (as shown in Table 6.1) and are, therefore, less
condensable (and, in turn, often less soluble in polymers) than the corresponding
paraffins [117].   The higher sorption of olefins probably derives from favorable
interactions between the double bonds in the olefins and the polar ether oxygen in the
polymer; a similar phenomenon is observed in poly(ethylene oxide) [25]. Based upon
their similar critical temperatures, CO2 and C2H6 have essentially the same
condensability. In nonpolar rubbery polymers such as polyethylene (PE) [117] and
poly(dimethylsiloxane) (PDMS) [26], C2H6 has higher solubility than CO2. However,
polar XLPEGDA-80 exhibits much higher CO2 solubility than C2H6 solubility, which is
ascribed to specific interactions between quadrupolar CO2 and polar ether oxygens in
XLPEGDA-80. In general, the increasing order of penetrant solubility in XLPEGDA-80
is quite different from that observed in non-polar polymers, such as PE [117] and PDMS
[26], where gas solubility often increases systematically with increasing condensability
(as characterized by Tc). For example, gas solubility in low density PE increases in the
following order [190]:
                         CH4 < CO2 < C2H4 < C2H6 < C3H6 < C3H8
Due to the lack of specific interactions between PE and these penetrants, solubility
selectivity is mainly dominated by relative penetrant condensability.

Figure 6.5   Infinite dilution solubility of various penetrants as a function of temperature
             in XLPEGDA-80.

       It is customary to relate S A∞ to penetrant condensability [30], which could be

characterized by critical temperature, Tc (K). The following equation has been widely
used [30,191,192]:

                       ln S A∞ = a s + bs Tc                                                              (6.6)

where as and bs are constants depending on the polymer and the temperature. Figure 6.6
presents a fit of eq 6.6 to gas solubility data at 298 K and 263 K. Clearly, the model does
not describe the experimental data well, especially for CO2 and C3H6. Eq 6.6 assumes
that gas solubility is determined only by its condensability (as characterized by Tc), so the
effect of interactions between the polymer and a specific gas on solubility is negligible.
However, this is not the case in polar XLPEGDA-80. Additionally, eq 6.6 does not allow
gas solubility to vary with temperature, and this feature is clearly not consistent with the



                                                                        141
experimental data. For this reason, a different set of as and bs values are required for each
temperature considered. Gee developed an expression relating ln S A∞ to penetrant normal

boiling point (i.e., Tb) using rather straightforward thermodynamic arguments [193].
Empirically, Tb can be related to Tc by the Guldberg-Guye rule [134]: Tb = 0.6Tc . Using

this approach, Barrer and Skirrow obtained [134]:
                       ln S A∞ = −(a s + χ ∞ ) + bs Tc                                 (6.7)

where as = 4.5 and bs = 6/T, in the original theory when solubility has units of
                                                      (   )
cm3(STP)/(cm3 atm). Often in the literature, a + χ ∞ and bs are treated as adjustable

parameters to fit eq 6.7 to experimental data. The value of bs varies from 0.016 to 0.019
K-1 at 25oC or 35oC for polymer/penetrants systems without specific interactions [170].
In our case, to account for the differences in χ ∞ parameters among different penetrants,
                                    (             )
Figure 6.7 presents the values of ln S A∞ + χ ∞ as a function of Tc at various temperatures.

The lines in the figure are drawn based on eq 6.7. The values of as and bs are recorded in
Table 6.5, and they are roughly similar to the theoretical values.            However, the
temperature dependence of as and bs are not captured by this simple model (i.e., eq 6.7).
Qualitatively, the trend in infinite dilution solubility is well described by eq 6.7 at each
temperature. The success in describing the data using eq 6.7, compared with eq 6.6, is
due to inclusion of the Flory-Huggins interaction parameter in eq 6.7, which accounts for
the effect of polymer/gas interactions on solubility. In contrast, eq 6.6 assumes either a
negligible effect of interactions on gas solubility or equivalent interactions between the
polymer and various penetrants. This assumption may be valid in some cases [26].
However, for polar polymers like XLPEGDA-80, these interactions contribute
significantly to the sorption and, therefore, can not be ignored.

       It is of interest to examine other correlations between solubility and penetrant
condensability. Stern reported the following empirical equation [186]:
                                                       2
                                         T 
                      ln S ∞A = M '+ N '  c                                                             (6.8)
                                         T 

where M′ and N′ are adjustable constants. Bondar et al. derived an expression similar to
eq 6.8 [194]. Unlike eq 6.6 or 6.7, this relation accounts explicitly for the effect of
temperature on solubility and, thus, it offers the possibility of describing all of the
XLPEGDA-80 sorption data using only one set of adjustable constants (e.g., M′ and N′),
instead of 5 different sets of parameter values.                               This empirical equation has been
successfully used to describe the solubility of a wide range of non-interacting penetrants
in rubbery polymers such as polyethylene [186], polyisobutylene [195], PDMS [26,188],



                                                       144
polystyrene     [196,197]      and      glassy     polymers       such      as     poly(5,6-
bis(trimethylsily)norbornene) [198] and poly(2,2-bis(trifluoromethyl)-4,5-difluoro-1,3-
dioxole-co-tetrafluoroethylene) (AF2400 [199] and AF1600 [200]). Figure 6.8 presents
S A∞ as a function of (Tc/T)2. Three trendlines are observed: one for normal paraffins (e.g.,

CH4, C2H6 and C3H8) which do not interact specifically with the polymer; one for olefins
(e.g., C2H4 and C3H6) which interact somewhat favorably with the polymer; and the last
one for CO2 which interacts strongly and favorably with XLPEGDA-80. The fitting
parameters (i.e., M′ and N′) are presented in Table 6.6 for these three different lines, and
they are compared with those in other polymers. The satisfactory modeling of paraffins
is consistent with results obtained using this relation to describe sorption in nonpolar
polymers such as PE and PDMS [188]. For paraffins, differences in critical temperature
among penetrants provide the dominant contribution to their solubility in XLPEGDA-80.
The positive deviation of olefins and CO2 solubility from the paraffin line could be
ascribed to favorable interactions between the polymer and these gases.           The more
positive deviation of CO2 solubility is consistent with more favorable interactions
between CO2 and the polymer, which is also manifested in lower χ values. These
phenomena have also been observed for other polar polymers [201,202]. Tseng et al.
reviewed gas solubility in polar poly(vinyl acetate) and reported different fitting
parameters for different categories of penetrants, such as polar and non-polar penetrants,
as shown in Table 6.6 [201]. Interestingly, the N′ value for CO2 in XLPEGDA-80 is
quite close to the value for the sorption of polar penetrants in poly(vinyl acetate).
Sanchez and Rogers estimated gas solubility coefficients in three nonpolar or weakly
polar polymers (i.e., polystyrene, poly(1-butene) and atactic-polypropylene) and two
polar polymers (i.e., poly(vinyl acetate) and poly(methyl acrylate)) using a lattice fluid



                                            145
model and the geometric mean approximation for interactions between the polymer
segments and gases [202]. In general, excellent agreement between the estimated and
experimental solubility was observed for gas sorption in the 3 nonpolar polymers,
although the solubility of oxygen containing penetrants (ethers, esters, and ketones) were
overestimated.   For the 2 polar polymers, solubility coefficients of non-aromatic
hydrocarbons were systematically overestimated, while solubility coefficients of polar
and aromatic penetrants were well correlated [202]. They claimed that the deviations
were caused by interactions between polymers and penetrants that were not accounted for
by the geometric mean approximation [202].

6.3.4    Enthalpy of Sorption

         In general, S A∞ increases with decreasing temperature, as illustrated in Figure 6.5,

and the temperature dependence is usually described using eq 2.16. The lines in Figure
6.5 are best fits of the data to eq 2.16. The values of SA0 and ∆HS are recorded in Table
6.7. Based on eq 2.19, ∆HS can be further decoupled into ∆Hcond and ∆Hmix. Eq 2.19 is
often used qualitatively to explain ∆Hs differences for various penetrants. ∆Hcond depends
on temperature for a given penetrant. We use ∆Hcond values at 273 K (i.e., the median of
the temperature range explored in this study) to qualitatively understand changes in ∆Hmix


                                             147
from one penetrant to another [203]. The results are recorded in Table 6.7. Values of
∆HS for all penetrants are negative since solubility increases as temperature decreases.
∆Hmix values for CO2 and C2H4 in XLPEGDA-80 are negative, which is not expected on
the basis of the Flory-Huggins model or regular solution theory [38]. Nevertheless,
negative ∆Hmix values for penetrant sorption in rubbery polymers have been reported. For
example, in poly(dimethylsiloxane) (PDMS), ∆Hmix is -3 kJ/mole for propane [203], -2.72
kJ/mole for trichloromethane [204] and -3.97 kJ/mole for trichloroethylene [204]. A
detailed, molecular level explanation of negative ∆Hmix values is beyond the scope of the
simple model (e.g., the Flory-Huggins model) often used to interpret such measurements.
In this study, the slightly negative ∆Hmix value for C2H4 sorption in XLPEGDA-80 is
consistent with weak, favorable interactions between olefins and polar ether oxygens, and
the more negative value of ∆Hmix for CO2 can be interpreted as another sign of significant
favorable interactions between CO2 and the polymer.
       Favorable interactions lead to lower ∆Hmix and, therefore, lower ∆HS. Olefins
such as C2H4 and C3H6 have lower ∆Hmix values than their paraffin analogs, and CO2 has
the lowest ∆Hmix value among all of the penetrants studied. Except for CO2, which
strongly interacts with the polymer and has a very low ∆Hmix value, ∆HS is dominated by
∆Hcond, and, therefore, ∆HS decreases with increasing Tc.

                                                              149
       Table 6.7 also compares ∆HS values in XLPEGDA-80 with those in nonpolar PE
(Grex with a crystallinity of 69-78 vol.%) [117] and glassy AF2400 [199]. All penetrants
exhibit less negative values of ∆HS in polar XLPEGDA-80 than those in other two
polymers, except for CO2. Since penetrant condensability is independent of the polymer,
this difference must be ascribed to differences in ∆Hmix. In general, paraffins have less
favorable interactions with polar polymers than with non-polar polymers such as PE,
resulting in more positive values of ∆Hmix for these gases in polar polymers. These
differences decrease for olefins, probably due to the affinity between the double bonds in
the olefins and the polar groups in XLPEGDA-80. The ∆HS value of CO2 is more
negative in XLPEGDA-80 than in the other polymers, due to the favorable interactions
between polar XLPEGDA-80 and CO2. Glassy polymers such as AF2400 have non-
equilibrium excess volume (or Langmuir sorption sites) which significantly decreases
∆HS [199]. However, due to the lack of specific interactions with CO2, the ∆HS value of
CO2 is still higher in AF2400 than in XLPEGDA-80.
       Figure 6.9 presents a correlation between the solubility pre-exponential factor,
SA0, and ∆HS. The following empirical relation is often observed between these two
parameters [194]:

                      ln S A0 = µ + ν∆H S                                              (6.9)

where µ and ν are -2.27 and 0.138 when SA0 has units of cm3(STP)/(cm3 atm) and ∆HS has
units of kJ/mole, for the sorption of nonpolar penetrants in rubbery polymers [30]. As
illustrated in Figure 6.9, the experimental data obey this correlation in XLPEGDA-80,
with the exception of CO2. The SA0 value of CO2 is much lower than the line shown in
Figure 6.9. lnSA0 is proportional to the entropy change of solution [194]. Presumably,
the favorable interactions between a polymer and a penetrant might lead to a certain

degree of order when the penetrant is sorbed in the polymer [158]. This behavior has
also been observed in liquid mixtures such as the chloroform/acetone system, where
unlike molecules form hydrogen bonds but the like molecules can not; and, therefore,
there is a strong tendency to form chloroform-acetone pairs, which decreases the entropy
of mixing [125].    In this case, CO2 enjoys a strong and favorable interaction with
XLPEGDA-80 and, therefore, this interaction could decrease the CO2 entropy in the
polymer, the entropy change upon mixing and, thus, SA0.

6.3.5   Solubility Selectivity

        The effect of temperature on pure gas CO2/paraffin (i.e., CO2/CH4 and CO2/C2H6)
solubility selectivity in XLPEGDA-80 is presented in Figure 6.10. The detailed values
are also recorded in Table 6.8. Selectivity increases as temperature decreases, primarily



                                                                            151
because CO2 solubility increases more with decreasing temperature than that of the other
gases, due to the favorable interactions between CO2 and polar XLPEGDA-80.                                                            In
particular, CO2 and C2H6 have similar critical temperature (i.e., condensability).
However, CO2/C2H6 solubility selectivity increases from 2.7 to 6.6 as temperature
decreases from 35oC to -20oC. CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity increases from 11 at 35oC
to 41 at -20oC, which could potentially be useful for designing membrane materials for
CO2 removal from natural gas or other light gases.                                                             Figure 6.10 also presents
olefin/paraffin (C2H4/C2H6 and C3H6/C3H8) solubility selectivity as a function of
temperature. These selectivities increase slightly with decreasing temperature, which is
consistent with the slightly more negative ∆HS values for olefins than the corresponding
paraffins.

       Table 6.9 compares CO2 solubility and CO2/CH4 and CO2/C2H6 solubility
selectivity in XLPEGDA-80 with those in rubbery polar crosslinked poly(propylene
glycol) diacrylate (XLPPGDA), nonpolar PE [117] and PDMS [26], nonpolar liquid
hexane [46,132] and polar liquid tetrahydrofuran (i.e., THF) [46,48,132]. XLPEGDA-80
exhibits higher CO2 solubility and much higher CO2/CH4 and CO2/C2H6 solubility
selectivity than nonpolar PE and PDMS. Interestingly, XLPPGDA exhibits similar CO2
solubility but lower CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity.     XLPPGDA contains 86 wt.%
propylene oxide units and, therefore, can be crudely regarded as amorphous
poly(propylene oxide).   It has much higher fractional free volume (0.165, which is
calculated based on its density, 1.059±0.004 g/cm3) than XLPEGDA-80, which increases
gas solubility [132]. Additionally, XLPPGDA has a lower content of ether oxygens than
XLPEGDA-80, and they are probably less sterically accessible for interaction than those
in XLPEGDA-80. Therefore, interactions with CO2 in XLPPGDA would not be as
favorable as in XLPEGDA-80, which could contribute to lower CO2/CH4 solubility
selectivity. The effect of the decreased ether oxygen concentration (which would tend to
make CO2 less soluble in XLPPGDA than in XLPEGDA-80) appears to be offset by the
effect of increased fractional free volume in XLPPGDA (which would tend to increase
the solubility of all gases). As a result, CO2 solubility remains similar in these two
polymers, but CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity is lower in XLPPGDA than in
XLPEGDA-80. Liquids usually exhibit higher gas solubility than the corresponding
polymers with similar chemistry [132]. Due to the lack of specific interactions with CO2,
nonpolar hexane exhibits similar CO2 solubility to XLPEGDA-80 but much lower
solubility selectivity than XLPEGDA-80. Polar THF, which has one ether linkage for
every 4 -CH2- linkages, has higher CO2 solubility than XLPEGDA-80. However, THF

                                                                                   156
6.3.6   Test of the Two Phase Model

        Previously, gas sorption has been measured in pure PEO, which contains 71
vol.% crystallinity [25]. In a rubbery polymer, the effect of crystallinity on penetrant
sorption is often represented using eq 4.5. This model (i.e., the two phase model) was
used to fit gas solubility data in a series of semi-crystalline polyethylene samples with
crystallinity values ranging from 41 to 78 vol.% [117]. However, when gas solubility
values were extrapolated to the wholly amorphous phase, the resulting solubility values
are often not consistent with those measured in a completely amorphous polymer of
similar chemical structure. For example, Michaels and Bixler estimated a value of 3.97
cm3(STP)/(cm3 atm) for C3H8 solubility in wholly amorphous polyethylene at 25oC.
They did not have access to wholly amorphous polyethylene, so they compared this
extrapolated value to the C3H8 solubility in natural rubber, which is 6.2 cm3(STP)/(cm3
atm) at 25oC [117]. In general, natural rubber is chemically similar to polyethylene, so
Michaels and Bixler argued that these two polymers should exhibit similar solubility,
especially for non-interacting penetrants such as C3H8. However, it is not clear that
natural rubber is a very good analog for amorphous polyethylene, so this result should be
interpreted with caution. Mogri and Paul measured CO2 and CH4 solubility in poly(alkyl
acrylates) at temperatures above and below their melting temperatures, Tm [205]. They
obtained the wholly amorphous phase solubility (e.g., SA,a) at Tm by extrapolating
solubility from temperatures well above Tm to Tm using eq 2.16. The solubility in the
semi-crystalline phase (e.g., SA) at Tm was obtained by extrapolating data from
temperatures well below Tm to Tm. The amorphous phase volume fraction (e.g., φ a ) was

calculated as the ratio of SA to SA,a, and the value obtained was about 0.30. However, the
value of φ a estimated from differential scanning calorimetry was 0.60. Discrepancies



                                           157
between experimental results and the model prediction based on eq 4.5 have also been
reported in other semi-crystalline polymers, such as rubbery 1,4-polybutadiene [206].
The failure of eq 4.5 to describe such data has led to the conclusion that the interphase
between an amorphous phase and a crystal phase might sorb gas [207]. Some polymer
crystals, such as those in poly(4-methyl-1-pentene) (PMP), are less dense than their
amorphous phase, and they are able to sorb gas [208]. Nevertheless, the two phase model
has been validated by other researchers in polymers such as polyethylene [209-211] and
copolyesters [212].       Therefore, the crystal morphology and processing conditions
probably have a significant effect on gas solubility in semi-crystalline polymers.
        Based on conflicting reports in prior work as described above, it is interesting to
explore the applicability of the two phase model to PEO. XLPEGDA-80 contains 82
wt.% PEO, and thus could be roughly regarded as a model of amorphous PEO. Table
6.10 compares solubility values of a variety of gases and solubility selectivity of CO2
over the other penetrants in XLPEGDA-80, semi-crystalline PEO at 35oC, and the
estimated amorphous phase solubility in PEO, which are calculated from solubility
coefficients measured in semi-crystalline PEO [25] using eq 4.5. CH4 solubility values in
XLPEGDA-80 and amorphous PEO differ slightly, due to the low gas sorption in
semi-crystalline PEO (which has 71 vol.% crystallinity) and the high uncertainty
associated with measuring the CH4 sorption in semi-crystalline PEO [25]. The excellent
agreement between the solubility values in XLPEGDA-80 and those in wholly
amorphous PEO suggests that the two phase model for solubility is valid for this system
and that the sorption properties of XLPEGDA-80 are dominated by the ethylene oxide
units in the materials.

                                                                159
6.4       CONCLUSIONS

          Sorption isotherms of CO2 and hydrocarbons (i.e., CH4, C2H4, C2H6, C3H6 and
C3H8) in polar XLPEGDA-80 are satisfactorily described using the Flory-Huggins model
with χ parameter values that depend on the temperature and weakly on penetrant volume
fraction. For all penetrants except CO2, as temperature decreases, χ values increase,
indicating more unfavorable interactions between penetrants and XLPEGDA-80 at lower
temperatures. In contrast, χ values for CO2 in XLPEGDA-80 decrease with decreasing
temperature, indicating more favorable interactions between CO2 and XLPEGDA-80.
Linear correlations between ln S A∞ vs. Tc or (Tc/T)2, which often provide good models for

gas sorption in nonpolar rubbery polymers, fail to correlate quantitatively the sorption of
quadrupolar CO2 and olefins in polar XLPEGDA-80.                  The following model, which
considers the effect of polymer/penetrant interactions on gas solubility, provides a
satisfactory                 description                  of            the            data:
      (                        )
ln S ∞ cm 3 ( STP ) /(cm 3 atm) + χ ∞ = − a s + 0.017Tc ( K ) .

          For highly sorbing penetrants such as CO2 and C3H6, gas solubility increases as
fugacity increases. Gas solubility increases with decreasing temperature, so ∆HS values
are negative. ∆H S generally increases as penetrant critical temperature increases, and
CO2 exhibits the largest value of ∆H S , mainly due to negative values of ∆Hmix.

Compared with nonpolar rubbery polymers such as polyethylene and glassy polymers
such as AF2400, XLPEGDA-80 generally exhibits lower ∆H S values, except for CO2.




                                                160
6.5    APPENDIX: FUGACITY CALCULATION

       Pure gas fugacity can be calculated using the viral equation of state, which is
given by [125,213]:
                             pV
                       Z=       = 1 + B' p + C ' p 2 + ⋅ ⋅ ⋅ ⋅                        (6.10)
                             RT
where Z is the compressibility factor, p is the pressure, V is the molar volume, R is the
gas constant, T is the temperature of interest, and B' and C ' are the second and third
virial coefficients, respectively. The above expression, truncated after the C ' p 2 term, is

sufficient for describing the gas behavior in this study. The constants (i.e., B' and C ' ),

which are functions of temperature, are available in the literature [214] and are recorded
in Table 6.11 for the penetrants and temperatures of interest to this study.
       Pure gas fugacity (i.e., f) is related to gas pressure as follows [125,213]:
                          f       p

this study, the f/p values are a linear function of pressure, as shown in Figure 6.1, so eq

                        f = p (1 + B' p )                                             (6.13)

    Gas Permeation and Diffusion in Crosslinked Poly(ethylene glycol

                                                163
7.1    SUMMARY

       The permeability of crosslinked poly(ethylene glycol diacrylate) (XLPEGDA-80)
to He, H2, O2, N2, CO2, CH4, C2H4, C2H6, C3H6 and C3H8 was determined at temperatures
ranging from -20 to +45oC and at fugacities up to approximately 15 atm for some gases.
Diffusion coefficients of CO2 and hydrocarbons were calculated as a function of
temperature and local penetrant concentration. The effect of temperature and fugacity on
permeability and diffusivity is satisfactorily described using an activated diffusion model.
Furthermore, a new model, based on the Cohen-Turnbull and Fujita free volume models
coupled with Chow’s model for estimating glass transition temperatures in polymer/gas
mixtures, also satisfactorily describes the concentration and temperature dependence of
the diffusion coefficients. This result is encouraging, especially since CO2 diffusion
coefficients at -20oC span more than one order of magnitude, ranging from 4.1×10-9 to
5.8×10-8 cm2/s, as the CO2 concentration in the polymer increases from 0 to 72 cm3
(STP)/cm3 polymer.




                                            164
7.2      INTRODUCTION AND BACKGROUND

         In Chapter 6, the effect of fugacity (from 0 to 10 atm) and temperature (from -
20oC to 35oC) on gas solubility in XLPEGDA-80 was reported [215]. The polar PEO
units exhibit favorable interactions with CO2, leading to high solubility selectivity for
CO2/non-polar gas pairs. Such interactions become stronger at lower temperatures [215].
This chapter reports permeability of XLPEGDA-80 to a variety of penetrants (e.g., He,
H2, N2, O2, CH4, CO2, C2H6, C2H4, C3H8 and C3H6) as a function of temperature and
penetrant fugacity. These permeability results are combined with gas solubility data to
calculate diffusion coefficients as a function of temperature and fugacity. The effect of
temperature and fugacity on gas permeability and diffusivity is interpreted using an
activated diffusion model and a new free volume model.
         The permeability of a polymer to a gas A, PA, is [185]:
                               NA ⋅l
                        PA ≡                                                            (7.1)
                               f2 − f 1

where NA is the steady state gas flux through the film, l is the film thickness, and f2 and f1

are the upstream (i.e., high) and downstream (i.e., low) fugacities of gas A, respectively.
This definition of permeability uses fugacity instead of pressure to account for non-ideal
behavior in the gas phase. Some of the more condensable penetrants considered in this
study exhibit significant deviations from ideal gas behavior, particularly at low
temperature and high pressure, so the use of fugacity is warranted [215]. The fugacity
was calculated from the virial equation of state with parameters available in the literature
[215].
         Gas permeability is often empirically related to fugacity as follows [216]:




                                             165
                              PA = PA, o exp(mP , E ∆f ) = PA, o exp(mP , E f 2 )                        (7.2)

where PA,o is the permeability coefficient when f2 = 0 (i.e., infinite dilution permeability),
m is an adjustable constant at a given temperature, and ∆f is the difference between the
upstream and downstream fugacity, ∆f=f2-f1.                        In this study, since the downstream
fugacity, f1, is practically 0, ∆f can be replaced by f2.
         Conventionally, local effective diffusion coefficients, Deff, can be written as [217]:
                                                   dP   df 
                              Deff (C 2 ) =  PA + f A                                               (7.3)
                                                   df  f 2  dC 2  f
                                                                         2




         Substituting eqs 6.2 and 7.2 into eq 7.3 yields the following result:
                                                       f2                1         2χ                 
Deff (C2 ) = PA, o exp(mP , E f 2 )(1 + mP , E f 2 )      φ2 (1 − φ2 ) 2  − 2 χ 0 − 1 − 3χ 2 (1 − φ2 ) (7.4)
                                                       C2                 φ2        T                 

         This approach has been used to estimate diffusion coefficients as a function of
temperature and penetrant concentration [25,26]. However, it requires values of PA,o and
m for each gas at each temperature of interest. This approach leads to a large number of
empirical parameters to characterize permeability and diffusivity in a material.
         The effect of temperature on gas permeability, solubility and diffusivity is often
described using the Van’t Hoff-Arrhenius approach (cf. eq 2.15-2.17). However, this
approach offers no specific prescription for interpreting experimental results when the
∆HS and ED are functions of penetrant concentration. For these cases, which are often
encountered when considering strongly sorbing components in the polymer, we use the
recent activated-state diffusion model developed by Prabhakar et al. [170] to interpret the
results. Moreover, we propose a new self-consistent model for the concentration and
temperature dependence of diffusivity, based on free volume, which can also alleviate
this shortcoming of the conventional approach.


                                                           166
7.3     RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

7.3.1   Permeability

        Figures 7.1a-f present permeability coefficients of various gases as a function of
temperature and upstream gas fugacity (i.e., f2) in XLPEGDA-80. In general, for low
sorbing penetrants, such as the permanent gases (He, H2, N2, O2, and CH4) over the entire
temperature range considered (i.e., -20 to 45oC) and C2H6 at temperatures higher than
10oC, permeability coefficients are essentially independent of f2.      The permeability
coefficients of He, H2, N2, and O2 are not shown here for brevity. In contrast, the
permeability coefficients of highly sorbing penetrants such as CO2, C3H8 and C3H6
increase with increasing f2, especially at lower temperatures. For example, at -20oC, CO2
permeability increases by almost 400%, from 5.9 to 28 Barrers, as f2 increases from 2.2 to
10.1 atm. This behavior is consistent with permeation properties of rubbery polymers
[26]. Polymers can sorb significant amounts of a diluent such as CO2 or C3H8, leading to
a decrease in polymer glass transition temperature, and, in turn, an increase in polymer
chain flexibility and fractional free volume, thereby increasing gas diffusion coefficients
and permeability coefficients [119]. Additionally, solubility typically increases with
increasing fugacity in rubbers [215], and this factor also acts to increase permeability
coefficients at higher fugacity [120]. These effects will be quantitatively discussed later
in this manuscript.

Figure 7.1                               Effect of temperature and fugacity on permeability in XLPEGDA-80. The
                                         lines and curves represent model fits to the experimental data using eq 7.10
                                         and the adjustable constants in Table 7.4. The permeability coefficients
                                         values at infinite dilution are estimated using eq 7.2.

                             The dependence of permeability coefficients on fugacity can be well described
using eq 7.2, and the infinite dilution permeability coefficients (i.e., PA,o) for all
penetrants estimated using this approach are presented in the figures (except for C3H6 and
C3H8 at -10oC). With the for C3H6 and C3H8 data at -10oC, the small number of data
points and the strong sensitivity of permeability to fugacity made extrapolation using
such an empirical model risky. The fitting constants for this empirical model are not
shown here for brevity. To illustrate the relative magnitude of permeability coefficients
among these penetrants, Figure 7.2a presents PA,o as a function of penetrant critical
temperature at 308 K.                                               The infinite dilution permeability coefficients are used for
comparison to minimize the effect of penetrant concentration on permeability [26]. In the
temperature ranges studied, PA,o values decrease in the following order:



                                                                                             169
               CO2 > C3H6 > C2H4 > H2 ≈ He > C3H8 ≈ C2H6 > CH4 ≈ O2 > N2
This is the same order as that observed in semi-crystalline PEO [25].              Generally,
penetrants with higher critical temperature are more condensable and, therefore, more
soluble. However, more condensable penetrants are often larger and, therefore, exhibit
lower diffusion coefficients [23]. Permeability reflects the tradeoff between these often
conflicting contributions from solubility and diffusivity. In rubbery polymers, solubility
selectivity typically dominates permeability selectivity and, in some cases, larger
penetrants such as C3H8 can exhibit higher permeability than smaller penetrants such as
N2 and O2, as illustrated in Figure 7.2a for gas transport in rubbery natural rubber (NR) at
25oC [88].    Such effects can become more significant if a penetrant has favorable
interactions with the polymer. For example, CO2 enjoys favorable interactions with
XLPEGDA-80 [215], and, not surprisingly, CO2 is the most permeable penetrant in this
study. In non-polar NR, CO2 does not exhibit specific interactions with the polymer. As
a result, CO2 permeability in these materials is consistent with its critical temperature (Tc)
rather than being much higher than its Tc value would suggest, which is the case for
XLPEGDA-80. For He and H2, which are the smallest gases, diffusion coefficients are
presumably high enough that their permeability coefficients are larger than those of
several more condensable penetrants, such as O2 and N2. Figure 7.2b presents the effect
of critical temperature on penetrant selectivity over N2 at infinite dilution in
XLPEGDA-80 at 35oC and NR at 25oC. The selectivity values in NR are near those of
XLPEGDA-80, except for CO2/N2.             XLPEGDA-80 exhibits much higher CO2/N2
selectivity than NR due to the specific interactions between XLPEGDA-80 and CO2.

                                                 permeability selectivity over N2 at infinite dilution; and (c) infinite dilution

                                                                                                                                   171
       Recently, gas permeability properties have been reported for semi-crystalline PEO
[25]. Using these data and a simple two phase model (i.e., assuming that crystalline
phase is not accessible to penetrant sorption or diffusion and only increase the penetrant
diffusion path without immobilizing the amorphous phase polymer chains [25,88,117]),
permeability coefficients in wholly amorphous poly(ethylene oxide) (AM-PEO) can be
estimated [25]. These estimated values are reported in Figure 7.2c and compared with
those in XLPEGDA-80. The estimated permeability coefficients in Am-PEO are quite
close to those in XLPEGDA-80, which is gratifying given the approximations in the two
phase model used to make the extrapolation required for Am-PEO, and the fact that
XLPEGDA-80 is only about 82 wt.% PEO (the remainder of the structure of
XLPEGDA-80 is ethyl ester linkages).
       The temperature dependence of infinite dilution permeability coefficients in
XLPEGDA-80 can be modeled using eq 2.15. The values of PA0 and EP at infinite
dilution are recorded in Table 7.1 and are presented in Figure 7.3. With the exception of
CO2, as penetrant size increases, EP values increase and then level off, presumably
reflecting the interplay between ED (which increases with increasing penetrant size) and
∆HS (which often decreases with increasing penetrant size). Figure 7.3 compares EP
values in XLPEGDA-80 with those in natural rubber (NR) [88] and semi-crystalline
poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO) [25]. NR is an amorphous hydrocarbon polymer and, as
such, it provides a useful point of comparison for XLPEGDA-80. Since XLPEGDA-80
contains polar groups, it has lower free volume and higher cohesive energy density than
NR [25,215]; both of these factors act to increase barriers to diffusion [25].
Consequently, EP values in XLPEGDA-80 are higher than those in NR, despite the fact
that the dependence of Ep on Tc is very similar in these polymers. Interestingly, Ep values



                                           172
for larger penetrants such as C2H4 and C3H6 are quite similar in semi-crystalline PEO and
XLPEGDA-80, but Ep values for smaller penetrants, like O2 and CH4, in semi-crystalline
PEO are much larger than those in XLPEGDA-80. The effect of crystal morphology and
crystalline constraints on the mobility of the amorphous material might play a role in
determining Ep values in S-C PEO; however, these effects are not well-understood at a
molecular level [88].

                                                                174
7.3.2   Diffusivity

        Figures 7.4a-f present the calculated results of Deff(C2) as a function of penetrant
concentration and temperature for various gases using eq 7.4, including Deff∞ , the diffusion
coefficient at infinite dilution. Over the temperature range considered in this study, Deff∞

decreases in the following order:
                CO2 > CH4 > C2H4 > C2H6 > C3H6 > C3H8
This order is exactly the same as that for penetrant size as characterized by critical volume.
The penetrant size dependence of gas diffusion coefficients in polymers has been described
using the following empirical equation [26,31,191,218,219]:
                                     τ
                           Deff∞ =                                                     (7.5)
                                     Vcη

where τ and η are adjustable constants. The parameter η characterizes the sensitivity of
diffusivity to penetrant size. Larger η values indicate stronger size-sieving ability. As
indicated in Figure 7.5, all data are well described by eq 7.5, and η values as a function of
temperature are recorded in Table 7.2. As temperature decreases, η values generally
increase, indicating an increase in size-sieving ability with decreasing temperature.
Typically, decreasing temperature increases polymer chain rigidity, resulting in an increase
in size-sieving ability.

        Figure 7.6 compares the dependence of Deff∞ on critical volume in XLPEGDA-80 at

35oC with that in NR at 25oC [88], poly(dimethylsiloxane) (PDMS) at 35oC [26] and semi-
crystalline PEO (S-C PEO) at 35oC [25].                The detailed values of η (and τ for
XLPEGDA-80) are recorded in Table 7.2. Based on the η values at 35oC, XLPEGDA-80
has a very similar size-sieving ability to PDMS, even though XLPEGDA-80 exhibits much
lower diffusion coefficients than PDMS. PDMS has the lowest Tg among all known
polymers, and it exhibits very weak size-sieving ability. Interestingly, XLPEGDA-80 also
has a size-sieving ability similar to that of NR, although it has been conventionally
believed that, due to polar-polar interactions, polar polymers would have higher cohesive
energy density, and thus stronger size-sieving ability, than non-polar rubbery polymers
such as NR. Polar XLPEGDA-80 and non-polar NR may exhibit similar size-sieving
ability due to the flexible nature of the ether oxygen linkages in

Figure 7.6   Correlation between penetrant critical volume and infinite dilution diffusion
             coefficients in XLPEGDA-80 at 35oC, semi-crystalline PEO at 35oC [25],
             PDMS at 35oC [26] and NR at 25oC [88].


XLPEGDA-80 [132]. We have reviewed the effect of polar groups on gas solubility,
diffusivity, permeability and CO2/H2 permeability selectivity [132]. Generally speaking,
the addition of polar groups to a polymer increases polymer cohesive energy density,
leading to a decrease in CO2 diffusion coefficients and CO2/H2 selectivity (i.e., an increase
in the size-sieving ability). However, ether oxygens are an exception to this rule of thumb
[132]. When ether oxygens are inserted in polyethylene (PE) to form poly(ethylene oxide),
CO2 permeability and CO2/H2 selectivity increase [25]. Unlike carbon atoms in PEO
chains, oxygens in PEO have no branches and thus seem to have little hindrance to motion.
In fact, extrapolated Tg values are -85oC for wholly amorphous PEO and -80oC for wholly
amorphous PE [220], so PEO chains are, if anything, more flexible than PE chains.
Therefore, the polar ether oxygens in XLPEGDA-80 do not necessarily increase polymer



                                                    179
chain rigidity and, in turn, size-sieving ability. Semi-crystalline PEO seems to have the
strongest size-sieving ability among these polymers, probably due to its crystalline nature.
To put these values in perspective, η is 10.5 for poly(vinyl chloride) (a very strongly
size-sieving glassy polymer) [26] and 0.45 for organic liquids such as hexane and benzene
(matrices with very weak size-sieving ability) [26].
       Gas diffusion in nonporous polymers is often interpreted as an activated process
described using eq 2.17. ED values were estimated using eq 2.18, and DA0 values were
obtained using an expression from eqs 2.15-2.17, i.e., DA0 = PA0/SA0. The results are
recorded in Table 7.1. Figure 7.7 presents ED values at infinite dilution as a function of
Lennard-Jones diameter squared [39] in XLPEGDA-80, rubbery NR [88], and the glassy
amorphous fluorinated polymer, poly(2,2-bis(trifluoromethyl)-4,5-difluoro-1,3-dioxole-co-
tetrafluoroethylene) (AF2400) [199]. For all polymers, ED values increase as penetrant
size increases. ED values in XLPEGDA-80 are higher than those in NR, presumably due to
the polar nature of XLPEGDA-80, which leads to lower free volume and higher cohesive
energy density [25]. For small penetrants, glassy polymers such as AF2400 exhibit lower
ED values than rubbery polymers because glassy polymers contain microvoids where the
diffusion of small gas molecules requires little polymer segmental motion to open up a gap
to accommodate the diffusion step of a penetrant molecule. On the other hand, the slope of
the best fit line for AF2400 is much larger than that for the rubbery polymers, indicating
that diffusivity in glassy AF2400 is much more sensitive to penetrant size than in the
rubbery polymers [13,30]. A low free volume glassy polymer such as polycarbonate
would have an even larger slope than that of AF2400 [13,30,199]. In addition to high ED
values, the XLPEGDA-80 and NR data points extrapolate to a positive activation energy at
