zero penetrant size. The reason for this behavior, which is observed for other rubbers such
as polyethylene [88], is not well-understood.
       In summary, the size-sieving ability of XLPEGDA-80 is rather weak (i.e., similar
to that of PDMS and NR), but this is not because ED values are low and change little from
one penetrant to another, which would be consistent with a Meares-type view of the
diffusion process if the polymer cohesive energy density were low [221].                Rather,
XLPEGDA-80 has higher cohesive energy density (around 19.8 MPa0.5) [215] than NR
(16.6 MPa0.5) [25] and PDMS (15.4 MPa0.5) [26]. It is weakly size-sieving because ED
values change little from penetrant to penetrant, but the absolute values of ED are quite


                                                          181
high, similar to or higher than those in rigid glassy polymers [199]. This result indicates
that the energy required to open a gap in the polymer matrix large enough to permit a
penetrant diffusion step is large but when these gaps are opened, they are sufficiently large
to accommodate, with little discrimination, any of the penetrant molecules considered in
this study. The molecular origin of this interesting result is not clear.
        Figure 7.8 presents DA0 values as a function of ED/R, and a simple relationship is
observed [30]:
                                     ED
                        ln DA0 = a      −b                                                (7.6)
                                     R

This linear free energy effect describes the so-called compensation effect; that is, higher ED
values are typically accompanied by higher DA0 values [30]. This behavior has been
observed for other activated processes, such as the kinetics of chemical reactions and the
viscosity of organic liquids, molten salts and metals [13,136]. The a and b values obtained
by fitting eq 7.6 to the diffusion parameters in XLPEGDA-80 are 0.0023 K-1 and 8.2,
respectively. As shown in Table 7.3, these values are quite consistent with the literature
data.
        In general, the above interpretation of gas permeability and diffusivity relies on eq
7.2, which empirically relates permeability to fugacity using two temperature dependent
parameters, PA,o and m. PA,o at various temperatures can be modeled using eq 2.15 and
only two parameters (i.e., PA,o and EP). However, the dependence of m on temperature is
unknown, and, thus it is impossible to predict permeability and diffusivity unless m has
been obtained at the temperature of interest. Additionally, the values of m might be
significant, especially for highly condensable penetrants such as CO2 and C3H8, as
illustrated in Figures 7.1a and 7.1f. From this perspective, it would be interesting to

explore new models with fewer adjustable parameters to describe the dependence of

fashion.

7.3.3   Prabhakar et al. Model

        Prabhakar and coworkers have viewed the concentration dependence of diffusivity
from a standpoint of activated state theory, so the concentration dependence of diffusivity
must arise from a concentration dependence of ED, which is described empirically as
follows [170]:

                       ED = ED (1 − kC )

        This model is applied to gas permeability data in XLPEGDA-80 at a variety of
temperatures and fugacities using non-linear least squares fits.       These model fits are

The best-fit values of adjustable constants (i.e., EDo, b and k) are recorded in Table 7.4.

characterize the temperature and concentration dependence of the Flory-Huggins
interaction parameter. These sorption parameters have been reported in Chapter 6 [215]
and are recorded in this table for completeness. The value of a is set to 0.0023 K-1. As
illustrated in Table 7.4, the values of b for all penetrants are in the range of the literature
values in Table 7.3. However, it was not possible to obtain good fits to the data using a
single, universal value of b for all penetrants. It appears that the single value of b obtained
from fitting the infinite dilution results in Figure 7.8 to eq 7.6 does not remain valid when
all of the data, at various concentrations, are included in the more general model (i.e., eq
7.10). This result may also suggest the need for a more critical examination of eq 7.7 and
its ability to describe data over a wide range of conditions and for a variety of penetrants.
Except for C3H6 and C3H8, EDo values are close to the values activation energy of diffusion
at infinite dilution, as recorded in Table 7.1.
        Deff(C2) can be also described by this model using eq 7.8 and the constants in Table
7.4. As illustrated in Figures 7.4a-f, the model fits are generally good. This result is
encouraging since the increase in gas diffusivity with increasing concentration can be
substantial. For example, as concentration increases from 0 to 72 cm3 (STP)/cm3 polymer

at -20oC, CO2 diffusivity increases by more than one order of magnitude, from 4.1×10-9

        The success of this model relies on an empirical equation, eq 7.7. However, the
value of k in eq 7.7 can not be quantitatively predicted. In addition to activated state
theory, free volume models are also often used to interpret gas diffusion in polymers. In
the next section, a modified free volume model will be introduced to account for the effect
of penetrant concentration and temperature on diffusivity. This model circumvents the use
of eq 7.7 and the linear free energy relation (i.e., eq 7.6).

7.3.4   Modified Free Volume Model

        In the framework of free volume models, the diffusion of a small molecule occurs
in the free volume elements of the polymer-diluent system [223]. The rate-limiting step in
diffusion, according to free volume models, is the opening of a gap (i.e., a free volume


                                               186
element) of a size sufficient to accommodate the penetrant; this gap (or free volume
fluctuation) must be near enough to the penetrant that it can access the free volume
fluctuation. The rate-limiting step in generating the free volume elements is the local-scale
segmental dynamics of the polymer chains. In the Cohen and Turnbull model, diffusivity
of a molecule in a pure liquid is related to average free volume, vf, by [27]:
                                         γv ∗
                        D = AC exp(−            )                                      (7.11)
                                          vf

where AC is a pre-exponential factor, which depends on the penetrant size, shape and
polymer properties. v* is the minimum hole size for a gas molecule to diffuse, and γ is a
numerical factor to correct for overlap of free volume elements. Eq 7.11 represents one of
the first efforts to employ the concept of free volume to explain gas diffusion. However,
due to the difficulty in obtaining vf values from independent estimates, this equation can
not be used to quantitatively model or predict diffusion coefficients. Fujita reformulated
the Cohen-Turnbull model using the average free volume of the system (i.e., f) to replace vf
and obtained [28]:

                        D = AF exp(− BF / f )                                          (7.12)

where AF and BF are adjustable constants that are typically taken to be independent of
temperature and diluent concentration [28]. Using the above equation as the starting point,
Fujita derived the following equation to describe the effect of diluent concentration on
diffusivity [28]:
                                               BF β (T )φ2              
                        D = D ∞ exp                                    
                                                                       2 
                                                                                       (7.13)
                                     f (0, T )β (T )φ 2 + [ f (0, T )]  

where D ∞ is the diluent diffusivity at infinite dilution, f(0,T) is the free volume of the pure
polymer, and β(T) is the free volume difference between pure diluent and polymer at


                                                    187
temperature T. In this model, the free volumes of the polymer and diluent are presumed to
be additive. Eq 7.13 is often used to describe the dependence of diffusivity on both
penetrant concentration and temperature for polymer-organic solvent systems [28].
However, it is rarely used for polymer-gas systems, because φ2 in polymer-gas systems is

often so low that there would have too much uncertainty to yield reliable values for the
constants (i.e., f(0,T), BF and β(T)) [28]. Vrentas and Duda further modified the Cohen-
Turnbull-Fujita theory [224]. However, their model also requires many parameters, and it
has not been widely adopted for describing gas diffusion in polymers.
       To summarize, there are no convenient and widely used models to describe the
dependence of gas diffusion coefficients on temperature and penetrant concentration
quantitatively in a predictive way. As the result, diffusion coefficients are often correlated
with penetrant concentration empirically using models such as the one presented earlier in
this manuscript. In this study, we propose a free volume-based model to relate gas
diffusivity to concentration and temperature with a minimum number of empirical
adjustable constants.
       Fujita’s free volume model is used as the framework for the dependence of
diffusivity on temperature and concentration.        A simple definition of free volume,
fractional free volume (i.e., FFV), is used to represent the free volume, f, in eq 7.12. FFV
is defined in eq 2.14. This simple approach has been widely used and often satisfactorily
captures the significant effect of free volume on diffusivity [29,225].
       This approach has been used to correlate gas diffusion coefficient with fractional
free volume in about 40 polymeric materials containing ethylene oxide units (including
XLPEGDA-80) [132].        The fractional free volume is related to the glass transition




                                             188
temperature, Tg, of the polymers or of the amorphous poly(ethylene oxide) phase of multi-
phase materials, by [132]:

                       FFV = 0.055 + 8.4 × 10−4 (T − Tg )                          (7.14)

where 0.055 is the fractional free volume at Tg, and 8.4×10-4 K-1 could be roughly regarded
as the average thermal expansion coefficient of this series of copolymers [132]. The
derivation of this equation will be discussed in Chapter 8 in detail. The value of 8.4×10-4
K-1 is also reasonably close to the thermal expansion coefficient of the amorphous PEO
(7.8×10-4 K-1) [132]. Eqs 7.12 and 7.14 can be combined to provide the following model
of diffusivity on temperature and diluent concentration, if the effect of diluent
concentration on Tg is known:
                                                −B                
                       Deff = AD exp                                             (7.15)
                                     0.055 + 8.4 × 10− 4 (T − T ) 
                                                               g 



where AD and B are adjustable constants for each penetrant that are independent of
temperature and concentration, at least to a first approximation.
        The presence of a strongly sorbing penetrant in a polymer can swell the polymer
matrix, resulting in enhanced segmental mobility and a depression in Tg [226], which has
been widely investigated by experimental measurements [227-230] and theoretical
modeling [228,231-234] because of the significant effect of diluents on material properties
in polymer processing [228] and membrane separations [226]. Chow has developed a
useful expression to predict the decrease in Tg with increasing diluent concentration in
polymer-diluent systems [231]:
                            Tg
                       ln          = β [(1 − θ ) ln (1 − θ ) + θ ln θ ]            (7.16)
                            Tg 0

where


                                                     189
                            Mp     w2                C2 M d                    zR
                       θ=               , w2 =                    , and β =
                            zM d 1 − w2        C2 M d + 22414 ρ p           M p ∆C pp

Here Tg0 and Tg are the glass transition temperatures of pure polymer (i.e., 231 K for
XLPEGDA-80) and the polymer-diluent mixture, respectively, when the diluent weight
fraction is w2. Mp and Md are the molecular weights of the polymer repeat unit (which is
taken as 44 g/mole in this study since this is the molecular weight of an ethylene oxide
moiety) and diluent, respectively. ρp is the polymer density. ∆Cpp is the change in heat
capacity of the pure polymer at its glass transition, 0.99 J/(g oC), which was obtained from
differential scanning calorimetry (DSC) experiments on XLPEGDA-80. This value is
consistent with the value reported in the literature for amorphous poly(ethylene oxide)
(0.948 J/(g oC)) [30]. Thus, all of the parameters in eq 7.16 can be measured or calculated
except z, a coordination number. Chow evaluated 13 diluents in polystyrene and found
that a value of 2 for z gave satisfactory fitting results [231]. This finding has been
confirmed by other researchers for other polymer-diluent systems, such as CO2 in PVC,
polystyrene, polycarbonate and PET [227] and CO2 in substituted polycarbonates [235],
even though in some systems z = 1 might be more appropriate [227]. In this study, the
calculated Tg values do not depend appreciably on z if it is 1 or 2, so in what follows, z is
set to 2. In this sense, there are no adjustable parameters in eq 7.16. The following
assumptions in Chow’s model are of interest: (1) penetrant concentration must be low in
the polymer; and (2) the molar volume of the polymer repeat units should be comparable to
that of the penetrant in the polymer [231]. The first assumption is valid in most cases of
this study. The second assumption is valid for all penetrants except C3H6 and C3H8, which
will be discussed later.

Figure 7.9   Calculated influence of penetrant concentration on the glass transition
             temperature of the polymer/gas mixture (Tg). The Tg of pure XLPEGDA-80,
             Tg0, is 231 K [184]. The calculations correspond to sorption at 273 K, unless
             otherwise mentioned. Chow’s model is used to estimate the influence of gas
             concentration on Tg [231].


       As an example, Figure 7.9 presents the effect of penetrant concentration on the
estimated Tg difference between the polymer/gas mixtures and the pure polymer for C2H4
and C3H6 at 273 K and for CO2 at 273 and 253 K. The largest value of (Tg0 - Tg), about 20
K, is observed for CO2 at a concentration of about 70 cm3(STP)/cm3 polymer. Figures
7.10a-d presents the correlation between diffusion coefficients and fractional free volume.
The lines are drawn based on the eq 7.16 with AD and B treated as adjustable constants for
each penetrant. In general, this model describes the data for CO2, CH4, C2H6 and C2H4
rather well using the values of AD and B recorded in Table 7.5. This model requires only 2
adjustable parameters (AD and B) per penetrant to describe the temperature and pressure
dependence of diffusivity, which represents a significant improvement over earlier


                                                         191
approaches [25,170,216]. The results are particularly satisfactory considering that the
diffusion coefficients for some of the gases vary by nearly 3 orders of magnitude. The
model does not work as well for C3H8 and C3H6 as it does for the smaller gas. One
possible reason could be an inaccurate estimate of the Tg change when C3H6 or C3H8 sorbs
into XLPEGDA-80. The sorption of hydrocarbon such as C3H6 and C3H8 in polymers has
not been modeled previously using Chow’s model. Potentially more importantly, the sizes
of C3H6 and C3H8 (i.e., 73 and 80 cm3/mole, respectively) are almost 100% larger than that
of an ethylene oxide unit (i.e., 37 cm3/mole), which violates one of the assumptions of
Chow’s model [231]. Light gases, such as He, H2, N2 and O2, typically have low solubility
in polymers, and, therefore, the change in Tg due to penetrant sorption can be neglected.
This is a reasonable approximation since the sorption of CH4, a more condensable (and,
therefore, more soluble) penetrant than the light gases, does not affect the Tg of the system
based on calculations using Chow’s model. By assuming solubility is constant for light
gases within this temperature range, eq 7.15 can be used to derive the following model for
permeability [29]:

                                 −B                        −B                
        PA = S A DA = S A AD exp      = AP exp                                  (7.17)
                                 FFV            0.055 + 8.4 × 10 −4
                                                                       (T − T )
                                                                             g 
                                                                                


where AP is an adjustable constant. Figure 7.11 presents the fit of this equation to the
experimental permeability coefficients of the light gases. The results are generally good.

       The constants AP and B can be obtained by non-linear least squares fits of the data
to eq 7.17 and are recorded in Table 7.5. B values are also presented in Figure 7.12 for all
penetrants as a function of Lennard-Jones diameter squared. In general, B values increase
as penetrant size increases, which is qualitatively consistent with the original Cohen-
Turnbull theory, which suggests that B should depend on the penetrant size [27].
       To summarize, this modified free volume model requires only two parameters, AD
(or AP) and B, to describe the effect of temperature and concentration on gas diffusivity or
permeability. These two parameters can be easily evaluated using gas diffusivity or
permeability at two temperatures, if the physical properties of the polymer, such as ∆Cpp
and Tg, and gas sorption data (in cases where solubility depends significantly on
temperature and/or pressure) are known.

7.3.5   Correspondence between the Activated Diffusion and Free Volume Models

        The application of both the activated diffusion model [170] and modified free
volume model to describe diffusion of small penetrants in rubbery XLPEGDA-80 gives
reasonable success, even though the physical basis of these two models are somewhat
different.   The activated diffusion model assumes that diffusion occurs only when a
molecule has an energy which is higher than a threshold value (i.e., the activation energy
of diffusion) [223]. The free volume model views gas diffusion as the re-organization of
free volume elements in the polymer near a molecule with the diffusion step occurring
when the free volume element is large enough to accommodate the penetrant molecule.


                                               196
There is no explicit mention of an activation energy involved in this rate limiting step in
the free volume model.[223] There have been some efforts in the literature trying to
correlate these two models quantitatively, but the possibility of doing so has not been fully
realized [236,237]. In this work, the correspondence between the approaches is further
explored.
       Combining eqs 2.17, 7.6 and 7.15 gives the following expression for the free
volume model estimate of the effective activation energy of gas diffusion:
                                   − B FFV + (ln AD + b )
                        ED , F =                                                      (7.18)
                                        a R − 1 RT

       This equation suggests a linear correlation between ED and B, which is consistent
                                                                               2
with the experimental data, since ED and B increase linearly with increasing d LJ , as

illustrated in Figures 7.7 and 7.12.
       Based on the activated state model, the effective activation energy of diffusion
(ED,A) can be calculated from Deff values by combining eqs 2.17 and 7.6 as follows:
                                   ln Deff + b
                        ED , A =                                                      (7.19)
                                   a R − 1 RT

       ED,A and ED,F can be calculated as a function of temperature and penetrant
concentration, and the results for various penetrants are presented in Figure 7.13. All of
the data points are very close to the line (which represents ED,F = ED,A), indicating good
consistency between the two models and the ability of both models to describe the
experimental data. Thus, it appears that the free volume model and activated state model
of gas diffusion in polymers may represent two essentially equivalent approaches to
describe such data.     It will be interesting to see if future theoretical and molecular
modeling approaches validate and extend this interpretation.

7.4    CONCLUSIONS

       In XLPEGDA-80, permeability increases as penetrant fugacity or concentration in
the polymer increases for highly sorbing penetrants such as CO2, C2H4, C2H6, C3H6 and
C3H8, in large measure due to an increase in diffusivity with increasing concentration.
Permeability to light gases (i.e., He, H2, N2, O2 and CH4) is independent of fugacity. As
temperature increases, permeability and diffusivity for all penetrants increase, indicating
positive values of Ep and ED. Presumably, due to its polar nature, XLPEGDA-80 exhibits
lower permeability coefficients and higher Ep and ED values than nonpolar polymers such
as natural rubber.
       Two models (one based on activated diffusion and the other based on free volume)
have been used to describe the temperature and concentration dependence of local effective



                                                                        198
diffusion coefficients. Both models describe the data rather well in most cases. Unlike
conventional approaches, these two models require very few adjustable constants.




                                          199
                                           CHAPTER 8




  Transport and Structural Characteristics of Crosslinked Poly(ethylene
                            oxide) Rubbers6




6 Reduced in part with permission from the Journal of Membrane Science, submitted for publication.
Unpublished work copyright 2005 Elsevier


                                                   200
8.1    SUMMARY

       Three series of crosslinked poly(ethylene oxide) rubbers have been prepared by
photopolymerization of prepolymer solutions containing: (1) poly(ethylene glycol)
diacrylate (PEGDA) and H2O, (2) PEGDA and poly(ethylene glycol) methyl ether acrylate
(PEGMEA), and (3) PEGDA and poly(ethylene glycol) acrylate (PEGA). All of these
polymers have similar chemical composition (approximately 82 wt.% ethylene oxide) but
the crosslink density and the content and chemistry of the chain end groups are different.
The effect of chain end groups and crosslink density on mass density, glass transition
temperature (Tg), free volume, and H2, N2, CH4 and CO2 transport properties of the
polymers was determined. The effect of temperature on gas permeability and solubility
was also investigated. Many of the samples were amorphous. However, compositions that
were high in PEGMEA could crystallize, and the formation of crystalline regions
significantly decreased permeability.    A generalized free volume model was used to
interpret the effect of crosslink density and chain end groups on gas permeability and
diffusivity, where free volume was characterized using two techniques: (1) density and
group contribution theory, and (2) positron annihilation lifetime spectroscopy. Finally, the
potential application of these materials for CO2/light gas separations was explored.




                                            201
8.2     INTRODUCTION

        In this chapter, gas permeability, solubility and diffusivity in three series of
crosslinked poly(ethylene oxide) materials is reported. These polymers contain similar
ethylene oxide content (about 82 wt.%), but they have different chain end groups and
different extents of crosslinking. The effect of chain end groups and crosslinks on polymer
physical properties such as density, glass transition temperature, and gas transport
properties is reported. Gas permeability and diffusivity are interpreted using a free volume
model, where free volume is evaluated from both density and positron annihilation lifetime
spectroscopy (PALS) data. The effect of temperature on gas solubility and permeability is
also reported, and the separation performance of these solubility selective materials as a
function of temperature is compared to that of conventional strongly size-sieving glassy
polymers.

8.3     RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

8.3.1   Monomer and Crosslinker Molecular Weight Characterization

        The molecular weight values obtained from 1H NMR and FAB-MS results were
very similar to the values provided by the supplier, and the polydispersity index for the
crosslinker (PEGDA, as shown in Figures 5.2 and 5.3) and two monomers (PEGMEA and
PEGA) was less than 1.10, indicating a narrow molecular weight distribution for each of
the components.

8.3.2   Physical Characterization

        Three series of polymers, PEGDA/PEGMEA-XX, PEGDA/PEGA-XX and
XLPEGDA-XX, have been prepared using prepolymer solutions containing: (1) PEGDA
and PEGMEA, (2) PEGDA and PEGA, and (3) PEGDA and H2O, respectively. “XX”


                                            202
represents the weight percent of crosslinker (i.e., PEGDA) in the prepolymer solution. The
characteristic peaks for acrylate groups are completely absent in the FTIR spectra of the
polymerized solid films, indicating essentially complete reaction of the monomers and
crosslinker. An example of the FTIR results has been given previously [184], so the
spectra are omitted for brevity. The polymer films were washed using toluene in a Soxhlet
extraction system for 6 hours, and the weight change of a dried film before and after
washing was negligible (i.e., around 1 wt.% or less), which suggests that all of the
monomer and crosslinker reacted and that the film composition was quite similar to that of
the prepolymer solution. Since each monomer or crosslinker contains about 82 wt.%
ethylene oxide, differences in observed properties do not arise from variations in ethylene
oxide content.
       Changing the amount of PEGDA crosslinker in the prepolymer solution is a
convenient method to vary the overall crosslink density in the resulting polymer [184]. In
addition, the copolymerization of acrylate monomer into the network leads to the
introduction of branch-like chain ends. The PEGDA/PEGMEA series contains methoxy
pendant chain end groups, while the PEGDA/PEGA series contains hydroxyl pendant
chain end groups.      The XLPEGDA series (PEGDA/H2O) does not contain such
copolymerized chain end groups.       Crosslink density can be estimated from dynamic
mechanical data, i.e., it is proportional to the elastic modulus in the rubbery plateau region
(ER) [161,184]. The values of ER for these 3 series of polymers are compared in Figure
8.1. Interestingly, the dependence of ER, or crosslink density, on PEGDA content in the
prepolymer solution follows practically the same trend for all of the polymers. That is, one
can prepare materials with a prescribed crosslink density using water, PEGA, or PEGMEA
as a prepolymerization diluent; the resulting crosslink density depends strongly on the

concentration of crosslinker in the prepolymerization mixture but little on the nature of the
diluent (among those considered in this study). However, the network topography is
presumably very different in these systems, with the PEGDA/H2O polymers containing a
higher concentration of loops and related structures that consume acrylate linkages during
polymerization but which contribute little to the elastic character of the network [184,238].
       Figure 8.2 presents the effect of PEGDA content in the prepolymer solution on the
density of the dry solid polymers at room temperature [59]. The detailed values are also
recorded in Table 8.1. In general, the amount of PEGA or H2O in the prepolymer solution
has essentially no effect on density.                         In contrast, increasing the PEGMEA content
systematically decreases the polymer density.

       Figures 8.3a and 8.3b present DSC thermograms of two series of copolymers,

corresponds to the glass-rubber transition. Some samples display an exothermic peak at
temperatures above Tg, and this peak is ascribed to crystal formation during the DSC
scan. In samples exhibiting this recrystallization peak, there is also an endothermic peak
at still higher temperature that characterizes crystal melting. The thermograms of the
XLPEGDA series are reported elsewhere [184], and they are quite similar to those for the
PEGDA/PEGA polymers. The values of the thermal transitions in Figures 8.3a and 8.3b
including Tg, melting temperature (Tm), and the enthalpies of crystallization (∆Hf) and

                             Crosslinking a polymer can inhibit its ability to crystallize [94]. High molecular
weight, linear poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO) has a melting temperature of 65oC and
contains about 70 vol.% crystallinity [25].                                                           In contrast, the XLPEGDA and
PEGDA/PEGA series of materials do not show any melting peaks in the DSC
thermograms, indicating complete inhibition of crystallization of the ethylene oxide units.
However, for PEGDA/PEGMEA, a small amount of crystallization is encountered at high
PEGMEA contents. As illustrated in Figure 8.3b and Table 8.1, when the PEGMEA
content is approximately 91 wt.% or greater, the PEGDA/PEGMEA materials can

crystallize. In this case they exhibit a melting temperature (0oC) that is much lower than
that measured in high molecular weight PEO (i.e., 65oC [25]), presumably due to the
much smaller runs of ethylene oxide units participating in the crystal structure of these
crosslinked samples than one would find in high molecular weight, linear PEO. The
values of ∆Hf and ∆Hm from the PEGDA/PEGMEA scans are very similar, indicating that
the crystals are formed during the heating portion of the DSC scan and, therefore, the
observed Tg values correspond to those of essentially amorphous polymers.
       Wide angle X-ray diffraction (WAXD) is often used to characterize the crystal
structure of semi-crystalline polymers [105].                                            According to the DSC results,
PEGDA/PEGMEA-9 should crystallize in the range between -20oC and -50oC upon
cooling from room temperature, and it would be of interest to determine if the crystal




                                                                            208
structure is the same as that of pure PEO. However, no crystalline peaks were detected
for PEGDA/PEGMEA-9 using WAXD at -20oC, possibly because the crystallinity in this
copolymer was below the detection limit of the X-ray spectrometer (see discussion of
crystalline content below). To circumvent this problem, the WAXD patterns of the
monomers and crosslinker (PEGDA, PEGA and PEGMEA) were collected at -20oC,
where they are crystalline or semi-crystalline solids. These diffraction patterns are shown
in Figure 8.5 (except for PEGA, which has a pattern essentially equivalent to that of
PEGMEA), and they are compared with that of semi-crystalline PEO at room
temperature. The peak positions are very similar, indicating that the crystal structures of
PEGDA, PEGMEA and PEGA are quite consistent with that of PEO. Therefore, it might
be reasonable to assume that the crystal structures in the PEGDA/PEGMEA crosslinked
networks are identical to those found in PEO, which are monoclinic unit cells with
a=0.796 nm, b=1.311 nm, c=1.939 nm (chain direction), and β= 124o48′ [115].
PEGMEA and PEGA exhibit a halo under the sharp crystalline peaks, indicating that
there is a significant amount of amorphous phase coexistent with the crystals at -20oC.
This result is consistent with the fact that PEGMEA and PEGA have shorter runs of
ethylene oxide units than PEGDA and are, therefore, more difficult to crystallize than
PEGDA.
       The volume fraction of crystals that form in the PEGDA/PEGMEA films during
the DSC heating sweep, φ c , is estimated using eqs 4.1 and 4.2. For PEGDA/PEGMEA-9

(which has a room temperature density of 1.140 g/cm3), the crystallinity is 10 vol.%.

8.3.3   Permeability

        Figure 8.6 presents gas permeability coefficients as a function of upstream
pressure in PEGDA/PEGMEA-30 at 35oC. In general, the gas permeability coefficients
for light gases such as H2, CH4 and N2 are essentially independent of pressure, while CO2
permeability increases with increasing pressure. This behavior is often observed in
rubbery polymers, where strongly sorbing penetrants (such as CO2) can exhibit increases
in solubility with increasing pressure and plasticize the polymer matrix; both of these
effects contribute to increases in diffusivity and permeability [26]. The other polymers
all exhibit a similar dependence of gas permeability on pressure; their behavior is not
shown for brevity.

       To compare the inherent permeability of polymers to various gases, eq 4.4 is often
used to estimate infinite dilution permeability. Figures 8.7a-d present the effect of
prepolymer composition on infinite dilution permeability of N2, CH4, H2 and CO2 at
35oC. In general, the presence of PEGA or H2O in the prepolymer solution has a
negligible effect on gas permeability, but increasing PEGMEA content significantly
increases gas permeability.

                                                                               212
       To summarize, increasing PEGMEA content in the prepolymer solution decreases
density and Tg and increases gas permeability in the polymers, while the presence of
PEGA or H2O has a negligible effect on these properties. Since crosslink density per se
does not appear to exert an important influence on these properties (otherwise, increasing
PEGA or H2O content would change polymer properties such as density, Tg, and
permeability), the changes in PEGDA/PEGMEA are ascribed largely to changes in the
concentration of methoxy chain end groups, not crosslink density. Interestingly, the
change in gas permeability with varying PEGDA content is well described by eq 2.27,
where Φ 1 and Φ 2 are the volume fractions of PEGDA and PEGMEA or PEGA in the

polymer, respectively, and X1 and X2 are the transport properties (such as permeability,
diffusivity, or solubility) in a polymer prepared using 100% PEGDA and 100%
PEGMEA or PEGA, respectively. The volume fraction of PEGDA in the copolymers
can be estimated as follows: φ1 = ρ P w1 / ρ1 , where w1 is the weight fraction of PEGDA in

the copolymers, and ρ1 is the density of XLPEGDA-100. Eq 2.27 can often satisfactorily
describe the correlation between permeability and composition of polymer blends and
copolymers [91], but it does not account for any other changes, such as crosslink density,
in these copolymers. Figures 8.7a-d illustrate that the model can satisfactorily predict the
experimental data, further reinforcing the notion that crosslink density has a minor
influence on permeability in these materials.         For the PEGDA/PEGA series of
copolymers, both P1 and P2 values were obtained from experimental measurements; for
the PEGDA/PEGMEA series, P1 (i.e., the permeability for XLPEGDA-100) was from
experimental measurements. P2 was estimated by inserting the permeability coefficients
of PEGDA/PEGMEA-1.3 and XLPEGDA-100 and the relevant compositions into Eq
2.27 and back-calculating P2.

Figure 8.8   Effect of PEGDA content (vol%, based on dry polymers for the
             PEGDA/PEGMEA and PEGDA/PEGA series) on infinite dilution CO2/H2
             selectivity at 35oC. The lines through the data for PEGDA/PEGMEA and
             PEGDA/PEGA copolymers represent predictions of eq 2.27.

       Figure 8.8 presents CO2/H2 infinite dilution selectivity in PEGDA/PEGMEA and
PEGDA/PEGA copolymers.                            The base material, XLPEGDA-100, exhibits a CO2/H2
selectivity value of 7.5 with a CO2 permeability of 110 Barrers (cf., Figure 8.7d). These
values are similar to the properties reported in other PEO containing materials [65,111].
However, as PEGMEA content increases to 99 vol.%, pure gas CO2/H2 selectivity
increases by 60% to 12, and CO2 permeability increases by 420% to 570 Barrers. As far
as we know, this selectivity value, reported at infinite dilution and with anhydrous feed
gas, is the highest selectivity ever reported for this separation at these conditions
[65,111,239].    Additionally, the exceptionally high permeability values could be
attractive for industrial interest.                    For comparison, cellulose acetate is a standard




                                                               214
commercial membrane used to remove CO2 from natural gas; it has CO2 permeability of
6 Barrers at 35oC [240]. Interestingly, if the -OCH3 end groups are replaced by -OH end
groups, the results are entirely different. The presence of PEGA does not improve
CO2/H2 selectivity.       Additionally, Figure 8.7d shows that CO2 permeability is
independent of PEGA content.

8.3.4   Solubility and Diffusivity

        Gas permeability is often divided into solubility and diffusivity contributions to
understand the details of the transport mechanism. Solubility can be calculated using eq
2.6, and the dependence of gas solubility on pressure in rubbery polymers can often be
described using the following empirical equation [26]:

                        S A = S A, o (1 + mS p )                                      (8.1)

where SA,o is the infinite dilution solubility, and mS is an adjustable constant. Figure 8.9
presents CO2 and CH4 infinite dilution solubility at 35oC in the various polymers as a
function of PEGDA content in the prepolymer solution. All polymers exhibit similar gas
solubility coefficients, which is consistent with the fact that these polymers have similar
chemical composition, i.e., similar ethylene oxide content. Therefore, the differences in
gas permeability are due to differences in gas diffusivity.
        Figures 8.10a and 8.10b present CH4 and CO2 diffusion coefficients at infinite
dilution and 35oC as a function of PEGDA content in the prepolymer solution. These
coefficients are calculated based on eq 2.5 and the infinite dilution permeability and
solubility data presented earlier. Increasing PEGMEA content in the prepolymer solution
increases gas diffusivity, while increasing PEGA or H2O content in the prepolymer
solution has little effect on gas diffusivity.

8.3.5    Effect of Temperature on Gas Transport Properties

         Figures 8.11a and 8.11b present the effect of temperature on gas permeability at
an      upstream   pressure                                     of   4.4       atm    in        three    polymers,   XLPEGDA-80,
PEGDA/PEGMEA-30 and PEGDA/PEGMEA-9.                                                             The first two polymers exhibit no
thermal transitions in DSC over the entire temperature range studied (-20 to +35oC) and,
therefore, the decreases in gas permeability with decreasing temperature are well
described using eq 2.15. In contrast, PEGDA/PEGMEA-9 exhibits a sharp change in gas
permeability with decreasing temperature at about 0oC, which is close to the melting
temperature of the crystalline phase. For each gas studied, the polymer sample was
cooled from room temperature to -20oC, where the first measurement was taken;

subsequent measurements were taken at incrementally higher temperatures. As a result,
the sample had ample time to crystallize during the course of the low temperature
measurements. The presence of crystalline regions typically decreases gas permeability
[88]. The crystalline phase is usually insoluble to penetrants, thereby decreasing gas
solubility. Further, crystallites act as impermeable obstacles to transport through the
polymer, and they can also inhibit chain motion in the amorphous phase, resulting in
increased Tg in the non-crystalline regions; both of these effects decrease gas diffusivity
[241-243].                                      As illustrated in Figures 8.11a and 8.11b, PEGDA/PEGMEA-9 exhibits
higher gas permeability than PEGDA/PEGMEA-30 at temperatures above the crystal
melting temperature due to the higher content of PEGMEA in the former material.

       Figure 8.12 presents CO2 and CH4 infinite dilution solubility as a function of
temperature in three polymers, i.e., XLPEGDA-80, PEGDA/PEGMEA-30 and
PEGDA/PEGMEA-9. All of these polymers exhibit very similar CO2 and CH4 solubility
over the temperature range encompassed by the measurements, and the polymers are
amorphous. The results can be modeled by eq 2.16, and the values of ∆HS are recorded
in Table 8.2. The values of ED, calculated using eq 2.18, are also included. The decrease
in ED with increasing PEGMEA content is consistent with the fact that increasing
PEGMEA content decreases Tg, leading to an increase in polymer chain flexibility.

8.3.6   Free Volume Model

        Gas diffusivity is often related to polymer fractional free volume [132]. Since all
of the polymers studied exhibit similar solubility, permeability can be directly related to
free volume using eq 2.13. Two methods of characterizing polymer free volume, either
polymer density and group contribution theory or positron annihilation lifetime
spectroscopy, have been used in this study. As illustrated in eq 2.14, polymer FFV can
be estimated from polymer density. The values of AD, AP and B have been evaluated
based on gas permeability or diffusivity in XLPEGDA-80 over a wide range of FFV
(from 0.07 to 0.13, varied by changing temperature) [244] and are recorded in Table 8.3.

Table 8.3    Adjustable constants AP and B in the free volume model (i.e., eq 2.13) [244]

Penetrant                            AP [×103 Barrer]                      B

H2                                       6.0±0.6                       0.72±0.01

N2                                       7.3±0.7                       0.97±0.02

CH4                                        27±5                        0.99±0.01

CO2                                      360±70                        0.95±0.02



        Using these parameters, gas permeability in the various series of polymers can be
predicted, and Figures 8.13a and 8.13b present the comparison between the predicted and
the experimental infinite dilution permeability of CO2, CH4, H2 and N2 at 35oC. The
prediction is fairly good, considering that no adjustable constants are used, which
suggests that the observed changes in gas permeability are well correlated with changes
in fractional free volume in these materials. For CO2 and CH4, AP values were calculated
based on AD and solubility values, which were taken as the average values of infinite

                               PALS measurements yield two parameters related to the free volume elements,
the o-PS lifetime (i.e., τ3) and intensity (i.e., I3) [106]. The intensity characterizes the
concentration of free volume elements; higher values of I3 represent higher
concentrations of free volume elements [106]. The lifetime is correlated with the free
volume element size in the following semiempirical equation [245]:

determined from density, and pure gas CO2/light gas selectivity at 35oC and infinite

CO2/N2 and CO2/CH4 selectivities decrease with increasing FFV, while CO2/H2
selectivity increases with increasing FFV. These trends are consistent with the free
volume model
                                            AP , A     B − BA 
                      αA B =                       exp B                                        (8.4)
                                            AP , B     FFV 

which is derived from eqs 2.7 and 2.13, and predicts that α A / B decreases with increasing

FFV if penetrant A is smaller than penetrant B (i.e., BA<BB), and increases with



                                                                       225
increasing FFV if BA>BB. As shown in Table 4.1, CO2 has a kinetic diameter smaller
than N2 and CH4, but larger than that of H2.

8.3.7   Comparison with Other PEO Containing Materials

        In general, decreasing Tg in these polymers seems to increase CO2 permeability,
which is consistent with the trend in Figure 2.7. Tg has been correlated with fractional
free volume in rubbery PEO as follows:[37]

                       FFV = FFV (Tg ) + α r (T − Tg )                              (8.5)

where FFV and FFV(Tg) are the fractional free volume values at T and Tg, respectively,
and αr is the thermal expansion coefficient of rubbery PEO. This equation assumes that
the occupied volume of the polymer is independent of temperature, which is an
approximation [37]. Eq 2.14, which is widely used to estimate FFV, is also based on this
assumption.
        Using copolymers prepared from PEGDA and PEGMEA as base materials, a
series of copolymers with systematically varying fractional free volume and glass
transition temperature are produced. Figure 8.17 presents the correlation between FFV
and (T - Tg), where T is 35oC. Assuming αr to be independent of copolymer composition,
the straight line fit of the data yields a value of 0.055±0.001 for FFV(Tg)           and
(8.4±2.6)×10-4 K-1 for αr. The uncertainties are estimated using the propagation of errors
technique [59]. The uncertainty of FFV is estimated as ±1% since polymer density has
an uncertainty of ±1%, and (T - Tg) is assumed to have an uncertainty of ±2% or about
±2K.    The value of FFV(Tg)      is comparable with other reported experimental and
theoretical values, such as the “universal” values of 0.025 reported by Ferry and
coworkers and 0.12 reported by Simha and Boyer [37]. αr is defined as follows [30]:



                                            226
                              1  ∂V 
                       αr =                                                         (8.6)
                              V  ∂T  p

where V is the specific volume of amorphous rubbery PEO, and T is absolute
temperature. From PVT measurements, the value of αr is 7.8×10-4 K-1 for rubbery
poly(ethylene oxide) dimethyl ether with a molecular weight of 1000 g/mole [116],
which is very close to the value estimated from Figure 8.17. Therefore, eq 8.5, with
parameters obtained from these copolymers, might be a reasonable first approximation to
describe the PEO phase in other PEO containing materials, which are discussed in
Chapter 2. Based on reported Tg values for the PEO phase, FFV values were estimated
using eq 8.5 for the PEO phase of various PEO containing materials at 35oC, and the

results are recorded in Table 2.4.                             Figure 8.18 presents the correlation of CO2
permeability with 1/FFV using eq 2.13. This model has been applied to a wide range of
data and, qualitatively, it shows an encouraging ability to unify the data in Figure 8.18,
considering the simplicity of this equation. The much lower permeability exhibited by
block copolymer B1 (cf. Figure 8.18) might be due to partial mixing of the PEO phase
and the hard phase as discussed above.
       This simple model might be used to rationalize the concept of the immobilization
factor (βc) used by Michaels and Bixler to characterize restricted segmental mobility of




                                                                228
amorphous polyethylene due to constraints imposed by the presence of crystals [88]. The
following equation can be derived from eqs 8.6 and 2.13:
                                      PA,a      Bα r      Tg − T g   o

                        ln β c = ln          =                                           (8.7)
                                      PA                         (
                                               FFV o FFV o − α r Tg − Tgo   )
where PA,a and FFVo are the permeability and fractional free volume of the wholly
amorphous polymer, respectively. PA and Tg are for the PEO phase in PEO containing
polymers. Since high molecular weight, pure, amorphous PEO does not exist at 35oC, the
polymer prepared from pure PEGMEA is proposed as a model of amorphous PEO,
partially due to the similarities in densities and, therefore, fractional free volume, of these
two materials (i.e., 1.124 g/cm3 for hypothetical amorphous PEO [25] and 1.13 g/cm3 for
pure PEGMEA). In the original Cohen-Turnbull model of diffusion that underlies eq
2.12, B is proportional to penetrant size, so eq 8.7 clearly predicts that βc would increase
as penetrant size increases and as Tg increases, which is consistent with experimental
results in the studies of semi-crystalline polyethylene [88]. Michaels and Bixler found
larger βc values for larger penetrants and in samples with higher crystallinity, which was
inevitably accompanied by higher Tg values [88].

8.3.8   Gas Selectivity

        Polymers containing ethylene oxide, including those prepared in this work, have
been studied for the removal of CO2 from mixtures with light gases such as CH4, N2 and
H2 [6,25,36,65,78,85,111,132]. PEO has an affinity for CO2 but not for the light gases
and, therefore, it exhibits high solubility selectivity for CO2/light gas pairs. Additionally,
PEO has a very weak size-sieving ability at the temperatures studied. In contrast to these
PEO-containing polymers, most polymers investigated for gas separation in the literature




                                                  229
are rigid glassy polymers, such as aromatic polyimides, that achieve high gas selectivity
mainly due to high diffusivity selectivity [12,14,246].
       Figures 8.19a-e illustrate the differences between these two classes of polymers
for a variety of gas separations. XLPEGDA-80 (which is prepared from a prepolymer
solution containing 80 wt.% PEGDA and the balance H2O) is used as an example.
Polyethylene is a nonpolar rubbery polymer analogue of XLPEGDA80. PI-1 is a rigid,
high Tg polyimide prepared by Ube Industries, Ltd. that exhibits high CO2/CH4 separation
performance [247].     PI-2 is a rigid, high Tg fluorinated polyimide which has been
considered for CO2/CH4 separation [248]. Both polyimides exhibit strong size-sieving
capabilities. The chemical structures of these polymers are presented in Table 8.4. The
separation properties of all polymers in Figure 8.19 are presented over a temperature
range from -20oC to 45oC; the tail of the line through the data represents 45oC, and the
arrowhead represents -20oC. The points are the experimental data, and lines extending
beyond the experimental data have been extrapolated based on reported activation energy
values and pre-exponential factors; additionally, the polyethylene data were corrected to
an amorphous basis [88].       In general, gas permeability decreases with decreasing
temperature in all of the materials in Figure 8.19. In some cases, such as H2/CH4 and
CO2/H2, if H2 permeability values are not available, helium data are used since these two
penetrants often have quite similar permeability coefficients, due to their similar size and
condensability. In these cases, the substitution is noted on the figure.

            upstream pressure of 4.4 atm, polyethylene (PE) at an upstream pressure of
            1 atm [88] and two polyimides (PI-1 [247] and PI-2 [248]). In all cases, the

            1 and 10 atm for XLPEGDA-80, PE and PI-2, respectively. (a) H2/CH4; (b)

                                                          O                  O

                                                  N                    CF3               N              C

                                                                                                  232
       The H2/CH4 case exhibits favorable diffusivity selectivity (since H2 is much
smaller than CH4 (cf., Table 4.1) and, thus, more mobile) and unfavorable solubility
selectivity (since H2 has a far lower critical temperature than CH4 and is, thus, much less
soluble than CH4). Therefore, to achieve high H2/CH4 selectivity, optimal membrane
materials must exhibit strong size-sieving ability. As illustrated in Figure 8.19a, rigid
glassy polymers such as PI-1 and PI-2 exhibit much better separation performance than
rubbery polymers such as XLPEGDA-80. Decreasing temperature increases H2/CH4
selectivity, which is consistent with the general notion that decreasing temperature
increases the rigidity of the polymer matrix and decreases free volume; both of these
effects act to increase diffusion selectivity. The O2/N2 case (cf., Figure 8.19b) involves
both favorable diffusivity selectivity (since O2 is smaller than N2 and thus more mobile)
and favorable solubility selectivity (since O2 has a higher critical temperature than N2 and
is, thus, more soluble). These light gases have no specific interactions with the polymers
under consideration, so gas solubility selectivity is dominated by relative condensability
and, therefore, would be more or less constant in these different polymers. As a result,
strongly size-sieving polymers, such as polyimides, would have higher selectivity due to
their high O2/N2 diffusivity selectivity. On the other hand, the size difference between O2
and N2 (0.18 Å, as characterized by the difference between their kinetic diameters) is
much less than that between H2 and CH4 (0.91 Å). As a result, O2/N2 diffusivity
selectivity would not be as high as H2/CH4 diffusivity selectivity, and diffusivity
selectivity contributes less to the overall permeability selectivity in the O2/N2 case than in
the H2/CH4 case.      Accordingly, the gap in selectivity between glassy and rubbery
polymers is much smaller for O2/N2 than for H2/CH4, as shown in Figure 8.19b.




                                             233
       The CO2/CH4 case (cf., Figure 8.19c) also involves both favorable diffusivity
selectivity (since CO2 is smaller than CH4) and favorable solubility selectivity (since CO2
has a higher critical temperature than CH4). Since polyimides enjoy high CO2/CH4
diffusivity selectivity due to their strong size-sieving ability, PI-1 and PI-2 exhibit much
higher selectivity than PE in the temperature range considered. However, XLPEGDA-80
enjoys favorable interactions with CO2, which boosts its CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity
considerably above that of the other materials.       Therefore, XLPEGDA-80 exhibits
CO2/CH4 permeability selectivity similar to that of PI-2 and higher than that of PI-1,
despite the fact that XLPEGDA-80 has diffusivity selectivity similar to that of PE [244]
and lower than that of the two polyimides. This result indicates that solubility-selective
materials, such as XLPEGDA-80, can exhibit pure gas CO2/CH4 separation properties in
the same range as those of strongly size-sieving glassy materials. Additionally, rubbery
materials such as XLPEGDA-80 can be an order of magnitude or more permeable than
conventional rigid, glassy polymers used commercially for such separations.
       Similar to the case of CO2/CH4 separation, CO2/N2 (cf., Figure 8.19d) separation
has both favorable diffusivity selectivity (since CO2 is smaller than N2) and favorable
solubility selectivity (since CO2 has a higher critical temperature than N2). On the other
hand, the size difference between CO2 and N2 (0.34 Å, as characterized by the difference
between their kinetic diameters) is less than that between CO2 and CH4 (0.5 Å). As a
result, diffusivity selectivity should not be as important in CO2/N2 separation as it
potentially is in CO2/CH4 separation and as it certainly is in H2/CH4 separation. As a
result, even PE exhibits permeability selectivity values within a factor of 2 or 3 of those
of the highly size-sieving polyimides, as shown in Figure 8.19d. Due to the specific
interactions between XLPEGDA-80 and CO2, which enhance CO2/N2 solubility



                                            234
selectivity, XLPEGDA-80 exhibits the best selectivity among all the polymers
considered, and it is much more permeable than the rigid polyimides.
          CO2/H2 separation (c.f., Figure 8.19e) exhibits unfavorable diffusivity selectivity
(since CO2 is larger than H2) and favorable solubility selectivity (since CO2 has a much
higher critical temperature than H2). Therefore, to achieve high CO2/H2 selectivity,
optimal membrane materials must have high solubility selectivity and very weak
size-sieving ability. Rubbery polymers such as XLPEGDA-80 and PE have much better
separation performance than strongly size-sieving polymers such as PI-1 and PI-2. In
fact, PI-1 and PI-2 are more permeable to H2 than to CO2 due to their strong size-sieving
abilities, even though there is still a substantial CO2/H2 solubility selectivity in these
polymers. Furthermore, XLPEGDA-80 exhibits superior separation performance relative
to PE, due to the more favorable solubility selectivity in XLPEGDA-80. This advantage
becomes more significant as temperature decreases.

8.4       CONCLUSIONS

          Three series of crosslinked polymers containing ethylene oxide were prepared
based on poly(ethylene glycol) diacrylate (PEGDA). By adding a second component
(i.e., H2O, PEGMEA or PEGA) to the prepolymer solution, crosslinking density and the
amount and chemistry of the chain end groups in the polymers were systematically
varied.     Increasing PEGMEA content decreased polymer density, glass transition
temperature, and activation energy of permeation and diffusion, and increased free
volume, gas permeability and diffusivity. However, gas solubility was not changed. In
contrast, the presence of PEGA or H2O in the prepolymer solution had a negligible
impact on the properties mentioned above, which indicates that crosslink density, per se,
has a weak influence on these properties among the polymers studied.


                                             235
       The changes in gas permeability of these polymers were well described using a
free volume model, where free volume was characterized using either density or positron
annihilation lifetime spectroscopy. Increasing PEGMEA content increased the size of the
free volume elements, which leads to the increased CO2 permeability and CO2/H2
selectivity and decreased CO2/CH4 and CO2/N2 selectivity, but did little to change the
concentration of the free volume elements.
       The pure gas separation performance for CO2/light gas pairs in crosslinked
poly(ethylene oxide) polymers was illustrated by comparison to a nonpolar rubbery
polymer (i.e., polyethylene) and two rigid glassy polyimides. As the separations moved
from those demanding high diffusivity selectivity (e.g., H2/CH4) to ones benefiting from
high solubility selectivity (e.g., CO2/H2), the performance of the polar rubbers improved
dramatically relative to that of conventional glassy polymers.




                                             236
              CHAPTER 9




Plasticization-Enhanced CO2/H2 Separation




                   237
9.1    SUMMARY

       Crosslinked poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO) rubbers demonstrate excellent mixed gas
CO2/H2 separation performance. Decreasing temperature significantly increase CO2/H2
mixed gas selectivity while CO2 permeability does not necessary decreases.        The
plasticization (induced by high pressure CO2 or water) can be harnessed to improve
CO2/H2 separation performance.    A new model, based on free volume theory, can
describe this interesting behavior [27]. This model successfully predicts mixed gas H2
and CO2 permeability based only on pure gas data and on the amount of CO2 sorbed in
the polymer. This approach, though simple and intuitively appealing, has never been
demonstrated before.




                                         238
9.2    INTRODUCTION

       Recently, hydrogen has attracted great attention as the cornerstone of a future
“hydrogen economy” in part because it can function as an energy carrier without
releasing greenhouse gases such as CO2 [3,249]. Today, however, hydrogen is produced
principally by steam reforming of hydrocarbons followed by the water-gas shift reaction,
which yields a hydrogen product containing impurities such as CO2, H2S, H2O, etc. [1]
Membrane technology is attractive for molecular scale separations due to inherent
advantages such as high energy efficiency, excellent reliability and small footprint
[10,11,250,251]. The potential applicability of membrane technology relies heavily on
the ability of membrane materials to exhibit high separation performance at practical feed
conditions (e.g., with feed streams that might contain high pressure CO2 and impurities
such as H2S and H2O).
       Highly permeable and highly selective membrane materials are desired for
CO2/H2 separation.      PEO containing materials have been proposed for CO2/H2
separations because ethylene oxide (EO) units interact favorably with CO2, which results
in high solubility selectivity [132], and they also enhance polymer chain flexibility,
which leads to weak size-sieving behavior and high diffusion coefficients, which
contribute directly to high CO2 permeability and high CO2/H2 permeability [132]. As
discussed in Chapter 8, PEGDA/PEGMEA copolymers are particularly interesting, since
the addition of PEGMEA or free methoxy chain end groups further increases free volume
element size, further improving CO2 permeability and CO2/H2 selectivity.




                                           239
9.3    RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

9.3.1 Effect of CO2 on Permeability and Selectivity

       Permeation experiments were performed using pure gas feeds and three CO2/H2
mixtures at 35oC, 10oC and -20oC. Figure 9.1a and 9.1b illustrate the dependence of CO2
and H2 permeability coefficients on CO2 partial pressure in the feed at various
temperatures. H2 and CO2 permeability increase with increasing CO2 partial pressure at
all temperatures; however, the increase is more remarkable at lower temperatures due to
the higher thermodynamic activity of CO2 (at a fixed partial pressure) at lower
temperatures. The pure gas and mixed gas permeabilities follow the same trendlines,
suggesting that the increase in gas permeability is in large measure a result of increasing
CO2 partial pressure and that the presence of H2 has a negligible influence on the gas
permeability. Presumably, sorbed CO2 in the polymer plasticizes the polymer chains,
leading to an increase in fractional free volume and, in turn, gas permeability. As
temperature decreases, CO2 sorption increases and, therefore, the effect of CO2 pressure
on permeability becomes stronger.
       Decreasing temperature typically decreases gas permeability [5,252]. However,
Figure 9.1a demonstrates that decreasing temperature may not always decrease CO2
permeability, especially at high CO2 partial pressure. For example, at a CO2 partial
pressure of 17 atm, CO2 exhibits a permeability coefficient of 410 Barrers at -20oC,
which is higher than the CO2 permeability at 10oC (300 Barrers) and very similar to that
at 35oC (440 Barrers). Presumably, the decrease in permeability that would normally
accompany a reduction in temperature (due to reduction in chain mobility and, in turn,
diffusivity as temperature is lowered) is essentially offset by the increase in CO2

solubility and the increased plasticization of the polymer by CO2 as temperature is
reduced.
                                  Decreasing temperature considerably increases CO2/H2 mixed gas selectivity. As
illustrated in Figure 9.2, at a CO2 partial pressure of 17 atm, mixed gas selectivity
increases from 9.4 to 31 as temperature decreases from 35oC to -20oC. The mixture
selectivity value of 31 is the highest value ever reported for CO2/H2 separation using
solid non-facilitated transport membranes [13,25,65,111,239].                                                                                         Moreover, this
extraordinary selectivity is accompanied by a CO2 permeability of 410 Barrers, which is
orders of magnitude higher than that observed in conventional polymer membranes used
for CO2 separations [5].                                       Furthermore, while facilitated transport membranes might


                                                                                       241
exhibit high CO2/light gas selectivity at very low CO2 partial pressure, the selectivity in
such materials decreases precipitously with increasing CO2 pressure; such materials
cannot operate at the high pressures required for hydrogen applications since such
membranes are often based on liquids supported in porous media, so the liquids typically
cannot be maintained in the porous support at the high pressure difference across the
membrane encountered in such application [253,254].

Figure 9.2   Effect of CO2 partial pressure and temperature on CO2/H2 mixed gas

             (●), 50:50 (∆), and 80:20 (▼) (mol CO2 : mol H2). The lines are drawn
             based on the model described by eqs 9.2 and 7.16.

       The effect of CO2 partial pressure on mixed gas CO2/H2 selectivity is interesting.
Mixed gas selectivity is essentially independent of CO2 partial pressure at 35oC and 10oC,
but increases with increasing CO2 partial pressure at -20oC. As CO2 partial pressure
increases from 0.4 to 17 atm at -20oC, CO2/H2 mixed gas selectivity increases by 35%,



                                                 242
from 23 to 31. This behavior is completely opposite to that observed in conventional
size-sieving polymers used for CO2 separations. In such materials, plasticization of the
polymer by CO2 or other components results in mixed gas selectivity which decreases,
often dramatically, with increasing CO2 partial pressure [17] or in the presence of other
strongly sorbing impurities such as higher hydrocarbons [16]. For example, at a CO2
partial pressure of 1 atm, a rigid, glassy, strongly size-sieving aromatic polyimide had a
mixed gas selectivity of 40 for CO2 over CH4 (an important separation in natural gas
purification), but as the feed CO2 partial pressure increased to about 10 atm, the
selectivity decreased by an order of magnitude, to around 4 [17].

9.3.2   Free Volume Model

        We ascribe these interesting results to the unique transport properties of these
reverse-selective materials.    At high CO2 partial pressures, these materials sorb
considerable amounts of CO2, which swells them, thereby increasing their free volume,
which decreases the glass transition temperature (Tg) [231] and weakens the size-sieving
ability of the membrane.       However, to the best of our knowledge, a quantitative
connection between these phenomena for both pure and mixed gas permeation has not
been presented.    Here, we present a model to quantitatively describe the effect of
penetrant-induced plasticization on pure and mixed gas permeation properties in rubbery
polymers. The fractional free volume (FFV) in materials, such as those considered in this
study, at a temperature T is correlated with that at Tg as follows [132]

                       FFV = FFV (Tg 0 ) + α r (T − Tg )                             (9.1)

where FFV(Tg0) is the fractional free volume of the pure polymer at its glass transition
temperature, Tg0 (i.e., 216 K for PEGDA/PEGMEA-30), and αr is the thermal expansion



                                             243
coefficient of the polymer in the rubbery state. In PEGDA/PEGMEA copolymers, Tg and
FFV can be systematically varied by adjusting the PEGMEA content, so the values of
FFV(Tg0) and αr can be evaluated; they are 0.055 and 7.8×10-4 J/(mole K), respectively
[132]. Gas diffusivity and, therefore, gas permeability, are often interpreted using the
following free volume model [27]

                                  B                         B              
                P = SD = SA exp −    = SA exp −                                 (9.2)
                                FFV              FFV (Tg0 ) + α r (T − T )
                                                                           g 
                                                                              


where A and B are constants depending only on the polymer and penetrant. The effect of
sorbed penetrant concentration on Tg can be described using Chow’s model, i.e., eq 7.16.
As discussed in Chapter 8, there are no adjustable parameters in eq 7.16.
       Eqs 9.2 and 7.16 were developed separately decades ago. However, they have
never been used together to quantitatively model the effect of temperature and pressure
on permeability. Since H2 solubility presumably changes little with temperature and
pressure, SA for H2 in eq 9.2 is independent of temperature, and H2 permeability is
directly correlated with FFV. However, CO2 solubility is sensitive to temperature and
pressure, so S and A in eq 9.2 are evaluated independently. Figure 9.3a and 9.3b illustrate
the extraordinary ability of the model to describe the pressure and temperature
dependence of both pure and mixed gas CO2 diffusivity and H2 permeability in two
different materials from this family of polymers. The only adjustable constants, A and B
for CO2 and SA and B for H2, are evaluated from pure gas CO2 diffusivity and H2
permeability at infinite dilution.

Figure 9.3                               Correlation between fractional free volume of the polymer/gas mixture and
                                         pure and mixed gas (a) H2 permeability (b) CO2 diffusivity at 35oC (●),
                                         10oC (∆) and -20oC (▼). For H2, the values of SA and B are 6000±600
                                         Barrers and 0.72±0.02, respectively. For CO2, the values of A and B are
                                         (1.8±0.2)×10-3 cm2/s and 0.95±0.02, respectively. The red (♦: at infinite
                                         dilution (i.e., extrapolated to an upstream pressure of 0); ◊: experimental
                                         data at various pressures) and black symbols represent the properties of
                                         XLPEGDA-80 and PEGDA/PEGMEA-30, respectively.

                                 The model fits are also shown in Figure 9.1a, 9.1b and 9.2, and they describe
remarkably well the CO2 and H2 permeability and CO2/H2 selectivity in
PEGDA/PEGMEA-30, given the small number of adjustable parameters. Here, eq 7.16
is used to predict the effective Tg of the polymer/CO2 mixture at any temperature and
pressure, and this effective Tg value, when inserted in eq 9.2, yields CO2 permeability as
a function of temperature and pressure. Furthermore, the effect of CO2 partial pressure
on the permeability of H2 in CO2/H2 mixtures may be predicted by using appropriate
values of SA and B for H2 in eq 9.2 and the procedure just described to calculate the



                                                                                         245
influence of CO2 sorbed concentration on Tg. Because H2 sorbs to a very small extent in
these polymers (its solubility was below the detection limit of our sorption equipment),
there is essentially no effect of H2 sorption on Tg, FFV, and, in turn, the temperature and
pressure dependence of either CO2 or H2 permeability. In general, this simple model
successfully captures the physical essence of the effect of plasticization on mixed gas
separation properties with no adjustable constants (the constants A and B were obtained
from pure gas transport properties at infinite dilution in a similar material). The B value
for CO2 (i.e., 0.95±0.01) is greater than that for H2 (i.e., 0.72±0.02), which is consistent
with the larger molecular size of CO2; free volume theory predicts qualitatively that B
should increase as penetrant size increases [27].

9.3.4 Upper Bound

       Figure 9.4 presents a permeability/selectivity map for CO2/H2 separation. The
upper bound line in the figure gives an estimate of the highest pure gas selectivity
possible for a given permeability in polymer-based materials [239]. Each point on the
graph represents the separation characteristics of a specific material. Unlike separation
based on strong size-sieving ability (where there is a distinct trade-off between
permeability and selectivity [13,239]), the positive slope of the upper bound indicates that
high CO2 permeability and high CO2/H2 selectivity may be achieved simultaneously.
The copolymers explored in this study exhibit excellent separation performance for
CO2/H2 mixtures, and decreasing temperature actually moves the CO2/H2 separation
performance above the upper bound.

9.3.5   Effect of H2S and H2O on Permeability and Selectivity

        The effect of impurities such as H2O and H2S on CO2/H2 separation properties
was investigated in PEGDA/PEGMEA-30 at 22oC. The addition of 0.33 mol% H2O (i.e.,
100% relative humidity) to a feed gas containing a 1:3 mixture of CO2:H2 at a feed
pressure of 8.0 atm increases CO2 permeability from 360 to 515 Barrers and increases
CO2/H2 mixed gas selectivity from 7.8 to 12.                              This change can be ascribed to
plasticization by H2O, which tends to improve CO2/H2 separation performance, probably
by a mechanism very similar to that by which CO2/H2 selectivity is increased at higher


                                                         247
CO2 partial pressures. We also tested this copolymer using a simulated synthesis gas
feed containing 1.0% H2S, 12.5% CO2, and 35.7% H2 in CO. Like CO2/H2 separation,
H2S/H2 separation requires reverse-selective membrane materials with weak size-sieving
ability and a polar nature to interact with acid gases. At a feed pressure of 7.8 atm, the
copolymer has an H2S permeability of 2500 Barrers and an H2S/H2 selectivity of 50,
which is the highest value ever reported for a solid, non-facilitated transport membrane.
Additionally, CO2 permeability and CO2/H2 selectivity remain unchanged in the presence
of H2S, indicating the robustness of this series of polymers for CO2/H2 separation, even in
the presence of additional components such as H2O and H2S.

9.3.6   Simulation

        H2 purification is usually achieved using pressure swing adsorption (PSA) [1].
The main disadvantage of PSA is the low recovery rate, which is often in the range from
70% to 85%. It would be interesting to see if the membrane materials developed above
could achieve such performance.
The CO2/H2 separation performance using PEGDA/PEGMEA-30 has been simulated
with the membrane module available at membrane.ces.utexas.edu. It is assumed to be a
one step membrane separation, and the simulation conditions are shown in Figure 9.5.
Gas permeability coefficients are experimental results, and the thickness of the membrane
is assumed to be 1 µm. By varying the feed flow rate, H2 purity in the residue flow (i.e.,
the product) and H2 recovery (defined as the ratio of the amount of H2 in the residue flow
to that in the feed) can be obtained. The results are presented in the Figure 9.6. H2 purity
decreases with increasing H2 recovery, and decreasing temperature improves the
separation performance. At -20oC, to achieve 70% recovery, H2 purity was 99.9%, while
to achieve 85% recovery, H2 purity was 0.977.
