                                                                             249
9.4    CONCLUSIONS

       In summary, this series of reverse-selective membrane materials based on highly
branched, crosslinked poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO) exhibits outstanding separation
performance for H2 purification by removing acid gases such as CO2 and H2S from feed
streams of practical interest. Moreover, the presence of water and high pressure CO2 in
the feed stream actually improves permeability and selectivity, in striking contrast to the
behavior of conventional membrane materials.         Looking forward, these molecularly
engineered copolymers are not only interesting for separations such as CO2 and H2S
removal from hydrogen streams, but they might also be used to remove CO2 and/or H2S
from flue gas (mixtures with N2) and natural gas, and SO2 and NH3 from nonpolar gasses.
Such materials have also attracted interest in drug delivery [255] and high energy density
lithium metal batteries [256], since PEO-containing materials are of great interest in these
applications. The new perspective of understanding the effect of diluents on polymer
transport properties, which relates the diluent concentration to the mixture Tg, free
volume and, therefore, the diffusion coefficients of chemical species in the polymer,
could aid the design of functional materials for applications such as lithium metal
batteries [257,258], and polymer processing in the presence of compressed fluids, such as
polymer foaming [259].




                                            250
                                         CHAPTER 10




  High Performance Polymer Membranes for Natural Gas Sweetening1




1 Reduced in part with permission from Advanced Materials, submitted for publication. Unpublished work
copyright 2005 Wiley InterScience



                                                 251
10.1   SUMMARY

       Unlike conventional size-sieving membrane materials, crosslinked poly(ethylene
oxide) rubbers exhibit high CO2 permeability, high CO2/CH4 mixed gas selectivity and
excellent stability to contaminants in natural gas. Operating the membranes at reduced
temperature, near the required dewpoint for pipeline grade natural gas [5], markedly
enhances separation performance. This unusual combination of outstanding properties
derives from a fundamentally different approach to designing membrane materials for
such separations. Crosslinked PEO rubbers achieve high permeability selectivity based
on high solubility selectivity.




                                         252
10.2   INTRODUCTION

       Natural gas is a vital energy source expected to be the fastest growing segment of
the world energy supply [4]. Approximately twenty percent of natural gas contains
excess CO2 which must be removed to meet pipeline specifications [5]. Due to the large
volume of natural gas produced annually (6.8×1011 m3(STP) in 2003 in the U.S. alone
[260]), even small improvements in CO2 removal efficiency could lead to considerable
cost reduction. Membranes are typically used for the separation only after the natural gas
has been subjected to rigorous and energy intensive pretreatment steps to remove
components (e.g., higher hydrocarbons) [5] that could plasticize the membranes and
compromise their separation performance [16]. It is a goal of this work to explore new
families of membrane materials that would exhibit good separation properties and be
more robust than conventional materials, potentially reducing or eliminating the need for
such rigorous feed pretreatment prior to acid gas removal. Such materials could reduce
the cost of processing natural gas and/or provide additional process design flexibility,
which could enhance process efficiency.
       In CO2/CH4 separation, CO2 is more condensable (as characterized by higher
critical temperature [25]) and more mobile (as characterized by smaller kinetic diameter
[25]) than CH4. Therefore, using membranes for this separation favors CO2 permeation
over CH4 since both CO2 solubility and diffusivity are higher than those of CH4. For this
reason, purified CH4 is produced at or near feed pressure.       In this way, expensive
recompression of CH4 is avoided, and high purity CO2, a primary agent in global
warming, could be recovered for other applications or sequestration.




                                           253
          Very permeable and highly selective membrane materials are desired for natural
gas purification. During the last two decades, research has focused primarily on polymer
materials that achieve high overall selectivity via high CO2/CH4 diffusivity selectivity
[5,17].    Such materials are rigid, glassy polymers with strong size-sieving ability,
including cellulose acetate and various polyimides [5]. However, an inherent limitation
of this approach is a trade-off between permeability and selectivity (higher selectivity
often results in lower permeability) which was demonstrated empirically by Robeson
[239] and modeled by Freeman [13]. More importantly, raw natural gas often contains
CO2, H2S, H2O, and other hydrocarbons (i.e., C2H6, C3H8, and higher aromatic and
aliphatic hydrocarbons) [5,15], contaminants which are highly condensable and,
potentially, strongly sorbed by polymers. The dissolved penetrants plasticize the polymer
chains, (which can be observed, in some cases, by significant depression of the glass-
rubber transition temperature, Tg) [226,231], and decrease the size-sieving ability of the
polymer matrix, which compromises permeability selectivity [16,17,89]. Figure 10.1
presents the effect of CO2 partial pressure on mixed gas CO2/CH4 selectivity in a glassy
polyimide (6FDA-mPD) at 35oC [17]. At low CO2 pressure, the polyimide exhibited
high CO2/CH4 selectivity due to high diffusivity selectivity. However, the selectivity
decreased by more than an order of magnitude at a CO2 partial pressure of 10 atm [17],
which is in the range of typical CO2 partial pressures in natural gas. Here, we propose a
polar rubbery polymer containing mainly PEO which separates CO2/CH4 streams in large
measure based upon solubility selectivity and, therefore, exhibits stable separation
performance even in strongly plasticizing gas streams.

Figure 10.1 Effect of CO2 partial pressure on pure and mixed gas CO2/CH4 selectivity at
            35oC in XLPEO and in a polyimide, 6FDA-mPD (4,4’-
            (hexafluoroisopropylidene)diphthalic anhydride-m-phenylene diamine) [17].
            The CO2/CH4 feed gas mixture was 50:50 for the polyimide. The feed gas
            mixtures for XLPEO were 10:90 (●), 50:50 (∆), and 80:20 (▼) (mol:mol)
            CO2:CH4. The lines serve as guides to the eye.


       Ether oxygen linkages in ethylene oxide units interact more favorably with
quadrupolar CO2 than with CH4, leading to high CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity [132].
Furthermore, the flexibility of ether oxygen linkages permits high CO2 diffusivity and, in
turn, high permeability [132]. PEGDA/PEGMEA copolymers are very attractive for this
separation.

10.3   RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

10.3.1 Effect of CO2 Pressure and Temperature on Permeability and Selectivity

       Permeation experiments were performed using pure gas feeds and three CO2/CH4
mixtures (i.e., 10/90, 50/50, and 80/20 mol CO2/mol CH4) at 35oC, 10oC, and -20oC.


                                                     255
Feed CO2 partial pressures in this study ranged from near zero to 17 atm, conditions that
are representative of natural gas streams [5]. As illustrated in Figure 10.1, mixed gas
CO2/CH4 selectivity in XLPEO at 35oC is essentially independent of CO2 partial
pressure, in stark contrast to the polyimide behavior.       Table 10.1 emphasizes the
importance of solubility selectivity by comparing XLPEO to two glassy polymers (i.e., a
polyimide and cellulose acetate (CA)). CA is widely used for CO2/CH4 separation and is,
therefore, a good benchmark for any material targeted for this separation [89]. Glassy
polymers such as CA typically exhibit high pure gas CO2/CH4 selectivity (i.e., in the
absence of plasticization). However, the mixed gas selectivities are often much lower
and are actually very close to the pure gas solubility selectivities. These results suggest
that conventional membrane materials (i.e., those based on strong size-sieving ability)
may not represent optimum membrane materials to achieve high CO2/CH4 separation
properties, since the mixed gas diffusivity selectivity would eventually decrease
substantially due to plasticization at high CO2 partial pressure or in the presence of
significant amounts of higher hydrocarbon contaminants. While the polyimide exhibits
very low mixed gas selectivity at high CO2 pressure, CA still exhibits a significant mixed
gas selectivity because the polar ether oxygen groups in CA contributes to elevated
solubility selectivity. XLPEO exhibits CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity much higher than
CA and mixed gas selectivity similar to CA. However, its CO2 permeability is orders of
magnitude higher than that of CA or the polyimide, which would reduce the required
membrane area proportionately and, in turn, the capital cost of membrane systems based
on XLPEO or similar materials.




                                           256
Table 10.1 Pure and mixed gas CO2/CH4 separation properties in a strongly size-
           selective aromatic polyimide, cellulose acetate, and PEGDA/PEGMEA-30.

                       Temp.         PCO2                   α CO / CH [a]
                                                                                   SCO2 / SCH 4 [b]
                                                                2   4
  Polymer
                        [oC]       [Barrer]
                                                    Pure Gas        Mixed Gas

  6FDA-mPD [17]          35           11              40                 <4             3.5

  CA [89,240]            35          6.9              36                 9-15           7.6

  PEGDA/PEGM             35          400              16                  12             12
  EA-30
                         -20         120              330                 40             40
[a]
    Pure gas permeability selectivities were calculated based on pure gas permeability at
10 atm. Mixed gas CO2/CH4 feed was 50:50 (mol:mol) at 20 atm for 6FDA-mPD and
80:20 (mol:mol) at 12.5 atm for XLPEO. CA was studied using two natural gas feed
streams containing 36 mol% CO2 and 4 mol% CO2, respectively, at a total pressure of 60
atm [89].
[b]
    Pure gas solubility selectivities were calculated based on pure gas solubility at 10 atm
for 6FDA-mPD and CA and at infinite dilution for XLPEO.



       A membrane unit might be more attractive for integration into natural gas
purification process trains if it could operate efficiently and reliably at low temperature.
After dehydration, raw natural gas streams are often cooled to -20oC or lower to condense
heavy hydrocarbons to achieve pipeline specifications for dewpoint (-20oC) and heating
value [5]. To use strongly size-sieving membrane materials to remove CO2, the gas
stream is then re-heated to 35oC or more, in part, to minimize any detrimental effects of
plasticization (lower temperatures usually increase gas sorption, which enhances
plasticization) and improve membrane flux [5]. Conversely, XLPEO relies much more
on high CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity (derived from the affinity of the polymer for CO2)
to achieve and maintain high permeability selectivity.                  Table 10.1 shows that as



                                              257
temperature decreases, this affinity becomes stronger and more favorable (as evidenced
by higher CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity at lower temperature), resulting in enhanced
CO2/CH4 solubility selectivity and mixed gas permeability selectivity. Furthermore, due
to the Joule-Thomson cooling effect [261], permeation of CO2 through a membrane from
the high pressure feed to the low pressure permeate can result in significant retentate and
permeate cooling (as high as 20-40oC, depending on the CO2 concentration in the feed
and the amount of CO2 permeated [261]). This effect could be harnessed to improve
CO2/CH4 separation performance in materials such as XLPEO, since mixed gas
selectivity increases as temperature decreases.      This approach may be even more
attractive for natural gas separations in cold climates, where less refrigeration would be
required to reach low temperature.
       As illustrated in Figure 10.2a, at a CO2 partial pressure of 17 atm, mixed gas
selectivity increases from 11 to 33 as temperature decreases from 35oC to -20oC. Mixed
gas selectivity remains essentially independent of mixture composition and decreases
with increasing CO2 partial pressure, presumably due to the decrease in CO2/CH4
diffusivity selectivity at higher CO2 partial pressures. At -20oC, XLPEO has a mixed gas
selectivity of 65 at low CO2 partial pressure and a value of 40 at a CO2 partial pressure of
10 atm, and the value decreases to 33 at a CO2 partial pressure of 17 atm. The CO2/CH4
selectivity of 33 is similar to the pure gas solubility selectivity (40) at the same
temperature.

Figure 10.2 Effect of CO2 partial pressure on (a) mixed gas CO2/CH4 selectivity; and (b)
            CO2 permeability in XLPEO at 35oC, 10oC and -20oC. Mixed gas CO2/CH4
            composition was 10:90 (●), 50:50 (∆), and 80:20 (▼) (mol:mol).
            ○ represents pure CO2. The lines are provided to guide the eye.


              Typically, decreasing temperature decreases gas permeability for small penetrants
[5]. However, Figure 10.2b demonstrates that lowering temperature does not always
decrease CO2 permeability, particularly at high CO2 partial pressure. For example, at a
CO2 partial pressure of 17 atm, CO2 permeability coefficients at 35oC, 10oC, and -20oC
are 440, 300, and 450 Barrers, respectively. At a constant CO2 partial pressure, the
thermodynamic activity of CO2 increases as temperature decreases, which favors higher
CO2 permeability due, in part, to higher CO2 sorption at lower temperature; this factor
appears to essentially offset the tendency for CO2 diffusivity to decrease as temperature
decreases. Additionally, the CO2 permeability of the polymer to both pure gas and the
three mixtures falls on the same trendline with pressure at each temperature, indicating
that the presence of CH4 has a negligible effect on CO2 permeability.



                                                               259
10.3.2 Upper Bound

       Figure 10.3 presents a permeability/selectivity map for CO2/CH4 separation. The
upper bound line drawn in the figure provides an indication of the highest pure gas
selectivity possible for a given permeability in polymer-based materials [239]. The effect
of temperature and CO2 partial pressure on CO2 separation properties is also illustrated.
In general, as temperature decreases, the CO2/CH4 mixed gas separation performance of
XLPEO moves closer to the upper bound and even crosses it at high CO2 partial
pressures. Included for comparison are data for cellulose acetate. XLPEO exhibits
mixed gas CO2/CH4 selectivity values similar to or higher than those of CA, and CO2
permeability coefficients are more than an order of magnitude higher than those of CA.

                                                         260
10.3.3 Effect of Higher Hydrocarbons on Permeability and Selectivity

       The presence of higher hydrocarbon contaminants, such as those found in natural
gas, can decrease CO2/CH4 separation performance dramatically in strongly size-sieving
polymers [16]. For example, the presence of hexane or toluene (at an activity of around
0.12 based on fugacity) in a mixed gas feed (10:90 CO2/CH4) at 48oC and a feed pressure
of 69 atm decreased mixed gas CO2/CH4 selectivity in an aromatic polyimide (i.e.,
6FDA/DMB [16]) from 17 to around 10 [16]. The effect of representative model higher
hydrocarbon contaminants on CO2/CH4 separation performance in XLPEO was also
investigated using a 50:50 CO2/CH4 feed containing 50.4 ppm (volume) toluene at 35oC
and 10oC and at feed pressures ranging from 4.4 to 21 atm. At the highest toluene
activity (0.038), CO2/CH4 selectivity is essentially independent of the presence of the
toluene, as illustrated in Table 2. The sample was also tested using a 10:90 CO2/CH4
feed containing 10 ppm toluene (corresponding to an activity of 0.002) and 500 ppm n-
hexane (with an activity of 0.018) at 35oC and a total pressure of 13.6 atm; CO2/CH4
selectivity remains at 14 during the 22-hour testing period, which is the same as that
using similar feed conditions without these hydrocarbons. The effect of another model
higher hydrocarbon, n-C4H10, on mixed gas CO2/CH4 separation performance is also
illustrated in Table 10.2. The presence of n-C4H10 in the feed increases CO2 permeability
but has a negligible effect on mixed gas CO2/CH4 selectivity at an activity up to 0.31,
which was the highest value considered in this study, indicating that XLPEO exhibits
good stability towards these model higher hydrocarbon contaminants.        Additionally,
XLPEO is, interestingly, significantly more permeable to n-C4H10 than CH4, so it can
potentially be used to reduce the dewpoint of natural gas streams by simultaneously
removing higher hydrocarbons and acid gases such as CO2.

10.3.4 Free Volume Model

       In Chapter 9, mixed gas H2 permeability can be satisfactory predicated using pure
gas permeability and the modified free volume model. Following the same procedure,
pure and mixed gas CH4 permeability is presented in Figure 10.4 as a function of
fractional free volume of the polymer/penetrant system. The model fit is reasonably
good, indicating the general applicability of the new model developed in this project.

10.4   CONCLUSIONS

       In summary, a novel approach to design polymeric membranes for natural gas
purification has been proposed and illustrated using a crosslinked poly(ethylene oxide)
based family of materials.                                     These solubility-selective membranes exhibit superior
performance for CO2/CH4 separation relative to conventional size-selective membranes,
especially in strongly plasticizing gas streams (i.e., high pressure natural gas laced with
higher hydrocarbon contaminants).                                          Their advantages could be further enhanced by




                                                                               263
operating at low temperatures, which may provide additional flexibility for integrating
membranes into natural gas processes.




                                         264
          Chapter 11




Conclusions and Recommendations




              265
11.1   CONCLUSIONS

       Membrane technology has been investigated for removing CO2 from mixtures
with light gases such as CH4, N2 and H2, and optimal membranes with high CO2
permeability and high CO2/light gas selectivity are of great interest. This project shows
that introducing polar groups in polymers is an efficient material science designing
approach to achieve high CO2 solubility and CO2/light gas solubility selectivity, leading
to high CO2 permeability and CO2/light gas selectivity.

11.1.1 Effect of Polar Groups on CO2/Light Gas Separation

       CO2 solubility and CO2/N2 solubility selectivity in both liquid solvents and solid
polymers containing a variety of polar groups were discussed. Optimum materials appear
to have a solubility parameter of about 21.8 MPa0.5 to achieve both high solubility and
high solubility selectivity. However, the introduction of polar groups can decrease CO2
diffusion coefficients and can make a material more size-selective, which is detrimental
to, for example, CO2/H2 separation properties. So far, ether oxygens in ethylene oxide
(EO) units appear to be the most useful groups for achieving high CO2 permeability and
high CO2/H2 selectivity.
       Poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO) is the simplest polymer containing EO units. PEO
exhibits better CO2/light gas separation performance compared to a non-polar analogue,
polyethylene. For example, at 35°C and infinite dilution, PEO exhibits CO2 permeability
coefficient of 12 Barrers, and CO2/H2 and CO2/N2 pure gas selectivities of 6.7 and 48,
respectively. In contrast, at similar conditions, the permeability of PE to CO2 is 13
Barrers, but the CO2/N2 selectivity is only 13.




                                            266
11.1.2 Preparation of Amorphous PEO by Introducing Crosslinks

       One drawback of using pure poly(ethylene oxide) (PEO) is its strong tendency to
crystallize, which could significantly decrease permeability.      Crystallization can be
efficiently inhibited using crosslinking and the introduction of ethylene oxide units as
short chain branches into the polymer structure.            Three series of crosslinked
poly(ethylene oxide) rubbers have been prepared by photopolymerization of prepolymer
solutions containing: (1) poly(ethylene glycol) diacrylate (PEGDA) and H2O, (2)
PEGDA and poly(ethylene glycol) methyl ether acrylate (PEGMEA), and (3) PEGDA
and poly(ethylene glycol) acrylate (PEGA), which are named as XLPEGDA-XX,
PEGDA/PEGMEA-XX and PEGDA/PEGA-XX, respectively.                    “XX” represents the
weight percent of PEGDA in the prepolymer solution. All of these copolymers are
amorphous at room temperature, indicating the effective inhibition of ethylene oxide
crystallization; they have similar chemical composition (approximately 82 wt.% EO) but
the crosslink density and the content and chemistry of the chain end groups are different.

11.1.3 Effect of Crosslink Density

       Decreasing PEGDA content in the prepolymer solutions decreases crosslink
density, which is estimated from rubbery modulus and/or water sorption data.             In
polymers based on PEGDA/H2O and PEGDA/PEGA, the polymer density, fractional free
volume, glass transition temperature, polymer d-spacing and gas solubility, diffusivity
and permeability are independent of crosslink density. Therefore, ascribing changes in
such properties to changes in crosslink density alone, as is commonly done in the
literature, should be done with great care.




                                              267
11.1.4 Effect of Pressure and Temperature on Gas Transport Properties

        Sorption isotherms of CO2 and hydrocarbons (i.e., CH4, C2H4, C2H6, C3H6 and
C3H8) in polar XLPEGDA-80 are satisfactorily described using the Flory-Huggins model
with χ parameter values that depend on the temperature and weakly on penetrant volume
fraction. For all penetrants except CO2, as temperature decreases, χ values increase. In
contrast, χ values for CO2 in XLPEGDA decrease with decreasing temperature,
indicating more favorable interactions between CO2 and XLPEGDA at lower
temperatures.     To illustrate the effect of temperature on intermolecular interactions,
CO2/C2H6 solubility selectivity at infinite dilution increases from 2.7 to 6.6 as
temperature decreases from 35oC to -20oC, even though these two penetrants have almost
the same condensability as characterized by Tc.
        Linear correlations between ln S A∞ and Tc or (Tc/T)2, which often provide good

models for gas sorption in nonpolar rubbery polymers, fail to correlate quantitatively the
sorption of quadrupolar CO2 and olefins in polar XLPEGDA. The following model,
which considers the effect of polymer/penetrant interactions on gas solubility, provides a
satisfactory description of the data: ln S ∞A (cm3 ( STP ) /(cm3 atm) ) + χ ∞ = − as + 0.017Tc ( K ) ,

where as is an adjustable constant.
        In XLPEGDA-80, permeability increases as penetrant fugacity or concentration in
the polymer increases for highly sorbing penetrants such as CO2, C2H4, C2H6, C3H6 and
C3H8, in large measure due to an increase in diffusivity with increasing concentration.
Permeability to light gases (i.e., He, H2, N2, O2 and CH4) is independent of fugacity. The
effect of temperature and fugacity on permeability and diffusivity is satisfactorily
described using an activated state diffusion model and a new model, based on the Cohen-




                                                268
Turnbull and Fujita free volume models coupled with Chow’s model for estimating glass
transition temperatures in polymer/gas mixtures.

11.1.5 Effect of Chain End Groups

       Adding PEGMEA or PEGA into the copolymers introduces methoxy and
hydroxyl chain end groups. Increasing PEGMEA content decreases polymer density and
glass transition temperature, and it increases free volume element size (estimated from
density or positron annihilation lifetime spectroscopy).      Thus, polymers with more
PEGMEA exhibit higher CO2 permeability and higher CO2/H2 selectivity; however,
CO2/CH4 and CO2/N2 selectivity decrease somewhat with increasing PEGMEA content.
For example, introduction of 99 wt.% PEGMEA (or 6.6 wt.% methoxy chain ends)
increases the average free volume element size (as probed by PALS) from 3.17 to 3.30 Å
in diameter, thereby improving CO2 infinite dilution permeability by 420%, from 110 to
570 Barrers at 35oC, and increasing CO2/H2 pure gas selectivity by 60%, from 7.5 to 12
at 35oC; these materials exhibit the best separation performance reported to date for solid
non-facilitated transport membranes.      In contrast, the presence of PEGA in the
copolymers has a negligible impact on the properties mentioned above, which indicates
that crosslink density, per se, has a weak influence on these properties among the
polymers studied.

11.1.6 Mixed Gas Separation

       These crosslinked PEO rubbers demonstrate excellent mixed gas CO2/H2
separation performance, as exemplified by PEGDA/PEGMEA-30.                     Decreasing
temperature significantly increases selectivity but does not necessarily decrease CO2
permeability, especially when CO2 content in the polymer is high. Increasing CO2 partial



                                           269
pressure at -20oC can increase CO2/H2 selectivity. At -20oC and a CO2 partial pressure of
17 atm, PEGDA/PEGMEA-30 exhibits a mixture CO2/H2 selectivity of 31 and CO2
permeability of 410 Barrers, which is the best performance ever reported for CO2/H2
separation using solid non-facilitated transport membranes. A simple simulation also
shows that this combination of permeability and selectivity could be promising for
CO2/H2 separation, as its permits the production of 99+% H2 at feed pressure and at
recovery values in the range of 70% (consistent with PSA) starting from a feed gas
mixture typical of that from a H2 production reactor train.
       PEGDA/PEGMEA-30 also exhibits good performance for mixed gas CO2/CH4
separation.   Unlike conventional size-sieving membrane materials, the membrane
exhibits high CO2 permeability, high CO2/CH4 mixed gas selectivity and excellent
stability to strongly plasticizing gas streams (i.e., high pressure natural gas laced with
higher hydrocarbon contaminants). Additionally, operating the membranes at reduced
temperature, near the required dewpoint for pipeline grade natural gas, markedly
enhances separation performance.
       Pure and mixed gas diffusivity and permeability in these crosslinked PEO rubbers
were successfully modeled using a new free volume model with only two adjustable
parameters, i.e., mixed gas permeability can be predicted based on pure gas permeability.

11.2   RECOMMENDATIONS

11.2.1 Interpretation of Gas Solubility

       In polar polymers such as XLPEGDA-80, gas solubility can not be simply
characterized by condensability.       Instead, interactions between the polymer and
penetrants are critically important. In this study, we use the Flory-Huggins model to



                                            270
analyze the gas sorption data while the Flory-Huggins interaction parameter, χ, is treated
essentially as an empirical fitting parameter. Sanchez-Lacombe model, which provides a

more modern description of polymer/gas thermodynamic interactions than the
Flory-Huggins model, could be used to model gas solubility in these polymers, and it
could provide new insights into the interactions between the polymer and penetrants as
well as a less empirical route to describe the experimental data [202,262,263].

11.2.2 CO2/C2H6 Separation

       CO2 is often present in mixtures with C2H6, which is usually used to produce
C2H4 [264]. To maximize production capacity, the impurities such as CO2 need to be
removed.    Currently, this is done using cryogenic distillation, which, for example,
operates at 35 bar and reflux condition of -3oC [264]. However, due to the CO2/C2H6
azeotrope (for example, about 7:3 molar ratio of CO2:C2H6 at -23oC and a total pressure
of 21 atm [264,265]), C2H6 recovery is not high. Membrane units could be used to break
this azerotrope.   These crosslinked PEO rubbers have high CO2 permeability and
CO2/C2H6 solubility selectivity, which could lead to good CO2/C2H6 separation
performance. At the current processing condition (about -3oC or lower [264,265]), these
solubility selective membranes achieve better performance than at higher temperature,
since decreasing temperature often increases CO2/non-polar penetrant solubility
selectivity. CO2 permeability might not decrease with decreasing temperature if the CO2
partial pressure is high enough.       Pure and mixed gas CO2/C2H6 solubility and
permeability should be measured at various pressures and temperatures to further explore
the utility of such membrane materials for this separation.




                                           271
11.2.3 New models to Correlate Gas Diffusivity with Gas Sorption

       Chapter 7 presents a modified free volume model to relate gas diffusivity or
permeability to (T-Tg), where Tg is the glass transition temperature of the
polymer/penetrant mixture under consideration. Chow’s model was used to estimate the
depression of glass transition temperature due to the gas sorption, and it has (at least) two
restrictions on its applicability: (1) gas sorption is low and (2) the sizes of the penetrants
and the repeat units in the polymer are comparable [231]. However, in the case of CO2 at
high pressure, the first restriction is probably violated, and the second assumption does
not hold for C3H6 and C3H8 sorption, as discussed in Chapter 7. As the result, the
modeling in these situations is not so satisfactory. There are other models in the literature
account for the effect of gas sorption on Tg without invoking these restrictions [234], such
as the Sanchez-Lacombe equation coupled with the Gibbs-Di Marzio criterion for Tg
[228]. It would be interesting to see if this approach could improve the modeling
description of the diffusion coefficients.

11.2.4 Mixed Gas Sorption

       In the mixed gas CO2/H2 and CO2/CH4 studies, H2 and CH4 permeability are
strongly influenced by CO2 partial pressure (or volume fraction) in the polymers. The
analysis in Chapters 9 and 10 assumes that the presence of CO2 in the polymer only
affects H2 or CH4 diffusivity and not their solubility. However, it is not known if high
CO2 sorption could change the light gas solubility. Mixed gas CO2/CH4 or CO2/C2H6
sorption measurement can be performed as a function of CO2 partial pressure and
temperature. Since H2 solubility is often too low to be measured, CH4 or even C2H6
could be used as an example to understand if CO2 sorption can influence the sorption of
other gases.


                                             272
11.2.5 Effect of Chain End Groups

       Chapter 9 describes that the addition of only a few percent methoxy chain end
groups could significantly increase free volume size, CO2 permeability and CO2/H2
selectivity, while the addition of hydroxyl groups has no effect. Presumably, hydroxyl
groups can form hydrogen bonds with each other or with carbonyl and ether groups in the
polymers. It will be nice to use FTIR to confirm the formation of hydrogen bonds. Such
a study would require an FTIR sample holder which can be evacuated to eliminate (or at
least reduce) the effect of water on the spectra, since water can be easily sorbed in these
hydrophilic PEO materials and generate peaks for hydrogen bonds. It would also be
interesting to systematically investigate the effect of the chemistry and the concentration
of chain end groups on CO2/H2 separation performance. Potentially interesting chain end
groups include -OCF3, -Si(CH3)3, -C6H5, etc.

                     Nomenclature

a      adjustable constant in eq 7.6
a′     adjustable constant in eq 2.25
as     adjustable constant in eq 6.6
AC     pre-exponential factor in 7.11 (cm2/s)
AD     pre-exponential factor in eq 2.12 (cm2/s)
AF     pre-exponential factor in eq 7.12 (cm2/s)
Am     film area assessable to gas permeation (cm2)
AP     pre-exponential factor in eq 2.13 (Barrer)
b      adjustable constant in eq 7.6
b′     adjustable constant in eq 2.25
bs     adjustable constant in eq 6.6
B      adjustable constant in eq 2.12 or 2.13
B′     the second virial coefficient (atm-1)
BF     adjustable constant in eq 7.12
C      concentration of gas A dissolved in the polymer (cm3 (STP)/cm3
       polymer)
C′     the third virial coefficient (atm-2)
d      penetrant kinetic diameter (Å)
ds     d-spacings in a polymer (Å)
DA     average effective diffusivity of gas A (cm2/s)
DA0    pre-exponential factor (cm2/s)
DA,a   diffusion coefficient in amorphous polymer (cm2/s)



                             297
DA/DB      diffusivity selectivity of gas A over gas B
Deff       gas effective diffusion coefficient (cm2/s)
Dloc       gas local diffusion coefficient (cm2/s)
E′         elastic modulus in the rubbery plateau (MPa)
ED         activation energy of diffusion (kJ/mole)
EP         activation energy of permeation (kJ/mole)
f          penetrant fugacity (atm)
f(0,T)     free volume of the pure polymer
f2 L       fugacity of hypothetical liquid penetrant (atm)
fsat       penetrant saturation vapor fugacity (atm)
FFV        fractional free volume defined by eq 2.14
FFVo       fractional free volume of the wholly amorphous polymer
FFV(Tg0)   fraction free volume of the pure polymer at its glass transition
           temperature
∆Hc        melting enthalpy of 100% crystalline polymer (J/g)
∆Hcond     enthalpy of condensation (kJ/mole)
∆Hf        crystallization enthalpy, from calorimetry data (J/g)
∆Hm(∆H)    melting enthalpy, from calorimetry data (J/g)
∆Hmix      enthalpy of mixing (kJ/mole)
∆HS        enthalpy change of sorption (kJ/mole)
∆Hvap      liquid enthalpy of vaporization (kJ/mole)
I3         o-Ps Intensity (%)
k          adjustable constant in eq 7.7
kD         Henry’s Law constant (cm3(STP)/cm3 atm)



                                298
l          film thickness (cm)

m          ratio of polymer molar volume to penetrant partial molar volume
mP,E       adjustable constant in eq 7.2 (atm-1)
mP,L       adjustable constant in eq 4.4 (atm-1)
mS         adjustable constant in eq 8.1 (atm-1)
M′         adjustable constant in eq 6.8
MA         polymer film weight in air (g)
Mc         number average molecular weight between crosslinks (g/mole)
Mc,ideal   ideal number average molecular weight between crosslinks
           (g/mole)
ML         polymer film weight in the auxiliary liquid (g)
Mn         number average molecular weight of monomer or crosslinker
Mn,P       number average molecular weight of primary polymer chains
Mw         weight average molecular weight of monomer or crosslinker
n          number of ethylene oxide repeat units
N          number of links per chain (eq 5.5)
N′         adjustable constant in eq 6.8
NA         steady state flux of gas A (cm3(STP)/(cm2 s))
∆p         pressure difference across the polymer film (atm)

                                         VITA

       Haiqing Lin was born in Putian, Fujian, P.R. China on October 9, 1974, the son of
Shuanying Zhuang and Jinkun Lin. After completing his work at Hushi High School,
Putian, China in 1992, he entered Xiamen University in Xiamen, China, from where he
graduated with a B.S. degree in Chemical Engineering in July 1996 and a M.S. degree in
Chemical Engineering in July 1999. Then he joined North Carolina State University in
Raleigh, NC that same year. He was awarded a M.S. degree in Chemical Engineering in
December 2001. In January 2002, he transferred to the University of Texas at Austin and
continued to work with the same supervisor, Professor Benny D. Freeman, in the
Chemical Engineering department.
